Chapter 1: The Human Is the Loop

"I do not prompt Claude anymore. I have loops running that prompt Claude and figure out what to do. My job is to write loops."
— Boris Cherny, Head of Claude Code, Anthropic

1.1 The Review Bottleneck

Every software team has a chokepoint, and in 2026, that chokepoint is a human staring at AI output. The pattern is so universal it has become invisible: you type a prompt, the model responds, you read the response, you find the mistake, you prompt again. The model is fast. The human is slow. The human is the bottleneck.

Consider the raw numbers. A senior engineer using an AI coding assistant today spends roughly 40% of their time reviewing generated output, 25% re-prompting to fix issues the model got wrong, 20% integrating the output into the broader system, and 15% actually thinking about the problem. The model generates code in seconds. The human review cycle takes minutes to hours. In a typical eight-hour day, the engineer might complete twelve prompt-review-fix cycles. The model could handle hundreds if freed from the human gate.

This isn't a complaint about AI quality. The models are remarkably capable. The bottleneck exists because we've inserted a human verification step into every iteration without asking whether that verification could itself be automated. We've built systems where the AI does the work and the human does the checking — then wondered why throughput doesn't scale.

The review bottleneck manifests differently across domains:

In code generation: A developer asks for a function, reads the output, spots an edge case, re-prompts, reads again, notices a type error, re-prompts again. Each cycle costs two to five minutes of human attention. The model could have caught both issues itself if given a test suite and a type checker.

In content creation: A writer generates a draft, reads it for tone and accuracy, marks up corrections, re-prompts with the corrections, reads the new version, finds that the fix introduced a new problem. The model could have evaluated its own draft against a rubric before presenting it.

In research: An analyst asks for a summary, spots a suspicious claim, asks for sources, finds a hallucination, asks for a correction, gets a plausible-sounding but still unsourced claim. The model could have verified claims against sources before ever surfacing the summary.

In each case, the human is performing a function that could be encoded: run the tests, apply the rubric, verify against sources. The human is the loop — the feedback mechanism that catches errors and drives iteration. But there's nothing inherently human about that function. It's checkable. And if it's checkable, it's automatable.

1.1.1 The Ceiling of Better Prompts

The natural response to the review bottleneck has been prompt engineering — writing better instructions so the model gets it right the first time. This works, up to a point. A well-crafted system prompt with examples, constraints, and output format specifications can eliminate entire categories of common errors. Chain-of-thought prompting improves reasoning. Few-shot examples anchor the model's behavior.

But prompt engineering has a hard ceiling, and that ceiling is lower than most practitioners want to admit.

The ceiling exists because prompts operate on a single forward pass. No matter how detailed your instructions, the model generates its response in one shot. It cannot step back, evaluate what it just wrote, and course-correct. It cannot run a test, see a failure, and revise its approach. It cannot check its citations against actual sources. It cannot compare its output to acceptance criteria and iterate until they're met.

A prompt says: "Do this well."

A loop says: "Do this, check if it's right, and keep going until it is."

The difference isn't subtle. It's the difference between telling a junior developer to "write good code" and giving them a test suite, a linter, a type checker, a code reviewer, and a CI pipeline. The former relies on talent. The latter relies on infrastructure. Infrastructure scales. Talent doesn't.

Here's the mathematical reality. If a model achieves 95% accuracy on a single prompt — an excellent result by any measure — then a sequence of twenty decisions yields a cumulative accuracy of 0.95²⁰ ≈ 0.36. Roughly one-third of multi-step tasks succeed end-to-end. No prompt, however brilliant, changes this arithmetic. Only a loop that catches and corrects errors at each step does.


# The arithmetic that breaks single-pass prompting
def cumulative_accuracy(per_step: float, steps: int) -> float:
    return per_step ** steps

# Even excellent per-step accuracy collapses over sequences
print(cumulative_accuracy(0.95, 5))   # 0.77 — acceptable
print(cumulative_accuracy(0.95, 10))  # 0.60 — concerning
print(cumulative_accuracy(0.95, 20))  # 0.36 — failure is the norm
print(cumulative_accuracy(0.95, 50))  # 0.08 — virtually guaranteed failure

# A loop with per-step verification changes the math entirely
def loop_accuracy(per_step: float, detection_rate: float, steps: int) -> float:
    effective_per_step = per_step + (1 - per_step) * detection_rate * per_step
    return effective_per_step ** steps

# With 90% error detection, the picture transforms
print(loop_accuracy(0.95, 0.90, 20))  # 0.90 — the loop saves you

1.2 Cherny's Inversion

In early 2026, Boris Cherny articulated what many practitioners had been groping toward: the shift from prompting to loop design as the core engineering discipline. "My job is to write loops," he said. Not prompts. Not agents. Loops.

The distinction matters because it reframes what we're building. We're not building smarter AI. We're not writing better prompts. We're building systems — feedback systems — that take imperfect AI outputs and refine them into verified results. The model is a component. The loop is the product.

This is Cherny's Inversion: the realization that the highest-leverage work isn't making the model better at a task, but making the verification of that task cheaper and more automated. Once verification is cheap, you can let the model attempt the task repeatedly, at scale, without human gates. The quality comes from the loop, not the model.

Consider two engineering approaches to the same problem — generating a function that correctly handles all edge cases:

Approach A: Better Prompting

Spend an hour crafting a detailed system prompt with examples of edge cases, explicit instructions about error handling, output format constraints, and few-shot demonstrations. Run the prompt. Review the output manually. Re-prompt if wrong.

Approach B: Loop Engineering

Write a test suite that covers the edge cases (10 minutes). Write a type specification (2 minutes). Set up a loop: generate → run tests → if tests fail, feed error back → regenerate → repeat until all tests pass (5 minutes). Run the loop unattended.

Approach A requires human attention on every invocation. Approach B requires human attention once — to build the loop — and then runs without humans indefinitely. Approach A gets better with better models. Approach B gets better with better tests. Tests are cheaper than models.

1.2.1 The Practitioner's Shift

The shift from prompting to loop engineering changes what you spend your time on:

Before (Prompt Engineering) After (Loop Engineering)
Crafting the perfect instruction Defining the acceptance criteria
Adding examples to the prompt Building the verification oracle
Tweaking temperature and parameters Tuning the stop condition
Reading and reviewing AI output Monitoring loop health metrics
Re-prompting on failure Fixing the oracle or the constraints
Model selection for capability Model selection for cost at quality

The mental model shifts from "how do I ask clearly enough" to "how do I verify cheaply enough." This is a fundamental reorientation. It's the difference between being a manager who gives clear instructions and a manager who builds good processes. Instructions scale linearly with the manager's attention. Processes scale independently of it.

1.3 A Compressed History — Setup, Not Archaeology

Agents aren't new. The concept dates to the 1980s — Minsky's Society of Mind, Hewitt's actor model, Brooks's subsumption architecture. But those agents lived in controlled environments with complete state observability. They could plan because they could perceive everything relevant.

The modern incarnation began in 2022 with ReAct (Yao et al.), which showed that interleaving reasoning and action — think, act, observe, think — could make language models effective tool users. This was the first practical loop: a model that could take an action, observe the result, and adjust its next action accordingly.

Then came a rapid succession:

  • 2023: AutoGPT and BabyAGI — demonstrated that loops could run autonomously but also demonstrated how badly they could drift without verification. AutoGPT's main contribution was proving that unbounded loops burn money and produce garbage without oracles.
  • 2023-2024: Tool-use frameworks (LangChain, CrewAI, AutoGen) — made multi-agent orchestration accessible but left verification as an afterthought. Most implementations assumed the model would "just get it right."
  • 2024: Devin and SWE-bench — proved that a well-engineered loop (plan → code → test → fix) could solve real engineering tasks autonomously. The breakthrough wasn't the model — it was the harness.
  • 2025: Claude Code, Cursor Agent, Windsurf — brought loop engineering to mainstream developers. These tools don't just generate code; they edit, test, fix, and iterate. The loop is the product.
  • 2026: Anthropic's Claude Code workflows, skills, and fleet orchestration — formalized the concepts of skills (reusable project knowledge), memory (cross-run continuity), and fleet loops (orchestrator + specialists). Loop engineering became a named discipline.

The history compresses into a single insight: every advance in agentic AI has been an advance in loop engineering, not in model capability. Better models make each step more reliable. Better loops make the overall system more reliable. The two are multiplicative, but the loop provides the structure that the model fills.

1.3.1 Why History Matters (Briefly)

Three lessons from this compressed history inform everything that follows:

Lesson 1: Unbounded loops destroy value. AutoGPT proved that a loop without a stop condition and a budget ceiling will spend arbitrarily much money producing arbitrarily bad results. Every loop needs a budget, a stop condition, and an escalation path. (We formalize this as the Loop Contract in Chapter 3.)

Lesson 2: The harness dominates the model. Devin and Claude Code proved that identical models produce wildly different outcomes depending on the scaffolding around them. A $20/month model inside a well-engineered loop outperforms a $200/month model inside a naive prompt-and-pray workflow. (We explore this in Chapter 4.)

Lesson 3: Verification must be cheaper than generation. If checking costs more than producing, you've built an expensive human-review pipeline with extra steps. The loop only delivers value when the oracle (the verification step) is significantly cheaper than the generation step. (We build the oracle hierarchy in Part IV.)

1.4 The Old Way vs. The New Way

Let's make the contrast sharp and concrete.

The Old Way (Human-in-the-Loop)


You type a prompt
    ↓
Model generates output
    ↓
You read the output (2-5 minutes)
    ↓
You find the error
    ↓
You type a correction
    ↓
Model generates again
    ↓
You read again (2-5 minutes)
    ↓
Eventually acceptable → ship

Total human time: 15-45 minutes per task.

Model time: seconds.

Bottleneck: human review.

Scales with: number of humans.

The New Way (Loop Engineering)


You define the goal + acceptance criteria (once)
    ↓
Loop discovers what's needed
    ↓
Loop plans the approach
    ↓
Agent executes
    ↓
Oracle verifies the result
    ↓
If verification fails → loop fixes and retries
    ↓
If verification passes → ship
    ↓
If budget exhausted → escalate to human

Total human time: 10-30 minutes to build the loop (once). Zero minutes per subsequent run.

Model time: seconds to minutes per run.

Bottleneck: oracle quality.

Scales with: compute budget.

The new way doesn't eliminate human judgment. It moves human judgment upstream — from reviewing every output to designing the verification criteria that review outputs automatically. You spend your intelligence once, on the oracle. The loop spends compute repeatedly, on the generation.

1.5 What This Book Is (and Isn't)

This book teaches you to design, build, and operate loops that ship verified work without human review at every step. It's for practitioners — people who build systems, not people who theorize about them.

What this book is:

  • A systems engineering manual for autonomous AI workflows
  • Heavily grounded in real implementations (Claude Code, production fleets, open-source harnesses)
  • Concerned with verification, cost, and reliability above all else
  • Honest about what doesn't work, what's expensive, and what's still unsolved
  • Written for engineers who will build these systems in their day jobs starting tomorrow

What this book is not:

  • A prompt engineering cookbook
  • An ML/AI fundamentals textbook (we assume you know what an LLM is)
  • A survey of every agent framework (we focus on principles, not libraries)
  • A hype piece about AGI or autonomous AI replacing humans
  • An academic treatment of agent theory

The book's central thesis: The quality of an AI system's output is determined more by the quality of its verification loop than by the quality of its generation model. Build better oracles, and you can ship verified work at machine speed.

1.6 The Loop Contract (Preview)

Every chapter in this book references a single formalism — the Loop Contract — introduced fully in Chapter 3 and filled in part by part through the book. Here's the preview:


loop_contract:
  goal: "What constitutes done, in machine-checkable terms"
  oracle: "How the loop knows the goal is met"
  budget: "Maximum tokens, time, money, or retries before escalation"
  stop_condition: "When the loop terminates (success, budget, oscillation)"
  escalation_path: "What happens when the loop can't resolve"

Every loop you build must answer these five questions. A loop missing any one of them is either unbounded (dangerous), unverifiable (useless), or uneconomical (expensive failure). The rest of this book is about making each answer precise, cheap, and reliable.

1.7 How to Read This Book

The book is structured in eight parts. Each part answers one question and fills in one element of the Loop Contract:

Part Question Contract Element
I Why loops? — (motivation)
II What does the loop run on? — (substrate)
III How do you build one? Goal + Stop condition
IV How does it know it's done? Oracle
V How do you run many? Escalation path
VI How do you afford it? Budget
VII How do you trust it? — (hardening)
VIII How do you do it for real? All five, integrated

You can read linearly, or you can jump to the part that answers your current question. If you're already building loops and they're too expensive, jump to Part VI. If they're unreliable, jump to Part IV. If you need to scale from one to many, jump to Part V.

But if you read nothing else, read Chapter 3 (the Loop Contract), Chapter 20 (the oracle hierarchy), and Chapter 27 (token economics). Those three chapters contain the intellectual core of everything else.

Next: Chapter 2 maps the autonomy levels — from autocomplete to self-designing fleets — giving you a navigation device for every subsequent chapter.

Chapter 2: Levels of Agency — The Autonomy Ladder

Level: B (Brief) · ~2,800 words · Navigation device for the entire book

2.1 Six Levels of Autonomy

Not all agents are created equal, and not all loops require the same level of autonomy. The Autonomy Ladder provides a shared vocabulary for describing how much control a system exercises over its own actions — and therefore how much verification infrastructure it requires.

Each level represents a qualitative jump in what the system decides on its own, what can go wrong without human intervention, and what verification machinery you need to trust it.


L5 │ Self-Designing Fleet    │ Designs own loops, spawns specialists, curates memory
L4 │ Autonomous Fleet        │ Orchestrator + specialists, cross-agent coordination
L3 │ Autonomous Single Agent │ Plans, executes, verifies, iterates — no human gate
L2 │ Guided Agent            │ Executes a human-designed plan, asks when stuck
L1 │ Tool-Augmented Model    │ Responds to prompts, calls tools when asked
L0 │ Autocomplete            │ Predicts next token, no tool use, no agency

L0: Autocomplete

The model predicts the next token based on context. No tools, no actions, no state, no iteration. This is GPT-3 in 2020 or any model running in a pure chat interface without function calling.

What can go wrong: Hallucination. Inaccurate information. Nothing external changes.

Verification needed: Human reads the output. That's it.

Book chapters that serve L0: None. This book starts at L1.

L1: Tool-Augmented Model

The model can call external tools — search the web, read files, query databases, execute code — but only when explicitly directed by a human prompt. Each interaction is a single turn: human asks, model responds (possibly using tools), human evaluates.

This is the standard AI assistant experience in 2024-2025: ChatGPT with plugins, Claude with tool use, Copilot with @workspace queries.

What can go wrong: Tool misuse, incorrect tool arguments, acting on hallucinated information from tool results.

Verification needed: Human reviews each response before acting on it.

Book chapters that serve L1: Chapters 9 (Tool Design), 10 (MCP).

L2: Guided Agent

The model executes a multi-step plan that a human designed. It follows the plan sequentially, uses tools as specified, and escalates when it encounters ambiguity or failure. The human defines the path; the model walks it.

Examples: a CI/CD pipeline where the model performs code review at a specified step, a data processing workflow where the model transforms data according to a schema, a testing workflow where the model runs a defined test plan.

What can go wrong: Misinterpretation of plan steps, failure to escalate when stuck, silent deviation from the plan.

Verification needed: Checkpoints at each step. Human spot-checks rather than reviews every output.

Book chapters that serve L2: Chapters 14 (Triggers), 16 (Planning), 17 (Execution and State).

L3: Autonomous Single Agent

The model plans its own approach, executes, verifies its own output against criteria, and iterates until done — all without human intervention. This is a single-agent loop: discover → plan → execute → verify → iterate.

Examples: Claude Code fixing a bug (reads the error, investigates, writes a fix, runs tests, iterates until tests pass), a research agent that searches, synthesizes, verifies claims, and stops when confidence is sufficient.

What can go wrong: Infinite loops, budget exhaustion, goal misinterpretation, reward hacking (gaming its own verification), silent scope drift.

Verification needed: Budget ceilings, stop conditions, oracle quality, post-hoc audit of results.

Book chapters that serve L3: Part III (Building the Loop), Part IV (Verification), Part VI (Economics).

L4: Autonomous Fleet

An orchestrator agent decomposes a goal into sub-tasks and delegates them to specialist agents. Each specialist is itself an L3 loop. The orchestrator coordinates, aggregates results, handles cross-agent dependencies, and makes strategic decisions about resource allocation.

Examples: Claude Code's workflow system (orchestrator fans out review agents, synthesis agents, verification agents), a security audit fleet (scanner agents, exploit-verification agents, report-synthesis agents), a content production pipeline (research agents, writing agents, editing agents, fact-checking agents).

What can go wrong: Everything from L3, plus: coordination failures, context contamination between agents, cascading errors, resource starvation (one specialist consumes the entire budget), deadlock.

Verification needed: All L3 requirements, plus: inter-agent consistency checks, resource allocation monitoring, topology-level health metrics.

Book chapters that serve L4: Part V (Fleets), Chapter 25 (Coordination), Chapter 29 (Observability).

L5: Self-Designing Fleet

The system designs its own loops, decides what specialists to spawn, writes its own skills, curates its own memory, tunes its own rubrics, and evolves its own verification criteria. The human defines the meta-goal and constraints; the system designs the architecture to achieve it.

Examples: An agent that, given "improve test coverage for this repository," designs its own workflow (analyze coverage → identify gaps → prioritize by risk → spawn per-module agents → verify each agent's tests → merge), writes the specialist prompts, runs the fleet, evaluates outcomes, and refines its approach for next time.

What can go wrong: Everything from L4, plus: specification gaming (optimizing for the metric rather than the intent), loss of human interpretability, emergent objectives misaligned with human intent, inability to audit the system's reasoning about its own design.

Verification needed: All L4 requirements, plus: meta-level audits (is the system's design of its own loops sound?), human-interpretable explanations of design decisions, periodic human review of evolved verification criteria.

Book chapters that serve L5: Chapter 38 (Self-Designing Loops).

2.2 The Ladder as Navigation Device

Every chapter in this book states which levels it serves. This isn't decoration — it's a practical guide for where to focus your reading:

  • Building your first loop (L2→L3)? Focus on Parts II and III.
  • Already have loops, need them cheaper and more reliable (L3)? Focus on Parts IV and VI.
  • Scaling to multi-agent (L3→L4)? Focus on Part V.
  • Everything's working but security keeps you up at night (L4)? Focus on Part VII.
  • Pushing toward self-improvement (L4→L5)? Focus on Chapter 38, but be honest about whether your L3/L4 fundamentals are solid first.

The ladder also provides a planning framework. Before building a system, ask: what level does this task actually require? The most common mistake in agentic AI is over-engineering — building an L4 fleet for an L2 problem. A guided agent with clear checkpoints is cheaper, more reliable, and easier to debug than an autonomous fleet for most tasks.

2.3 What Agentic AI Isn't — Honest Debunking

The term "agentic" has been diluted by marketing until it means almost anything an AI system does autonomously. Let's be precise about what doesn't count:

Not agentic: A chatbot that uses tools. If a human initiates every action and reviews every response before anything happens in the world, there's no loop. There's a tool-augmented conversation. Useful, but not agentic.

Not agentic: A pipeline with an LLM step. If you have a deterministic pipeline — fetch data → format → call LLM → parse output → store — that's an LLM-in-the-loop pipeline, not an agent. The LLM doesn't decide what to do next. It fills a slot in a human-designed sequence. (This is fine. Most production LLM uses are exactly this, and they work well.)

Not agentic: A single retry. If your system calls an LLM and retries on malformed output, that's error handling, not agency. Agency implies the system adapts its approach based on feedback, not just re-rolls the dice.

Not agentic: Agentic framing on non-agentic systems. Many "AI agent" products are thin wrappers around API calls with hardcoded fallback logic. They appear autonomous because the user doesn't see the pipeline. They aren't — the behavior is entirely predetermined by the developer.

Actually agentic: A system that observes a result, evaluates it against criteria, and makes a non-trivial decision about what to do next — where "non-trivial" means the next action wasn't predetermined by the developer. The system exercises judgment, even if that judgment is constrained by guardrails.

2.4 The Cost Multiplier

Each level up the autonomy ladder multiplies cost in three dimensions:

Level Typical Token Cost Verification Overhead Failure Blast Radius
L0 1x (one prompt) 0 (human reads) None (no action)
L1 2-5x (tools) Low (human checks) Low (one action)
L2 10-50x (multi-step) Medium (checkpoints) Medium (plan scope)
L3 50-200x (iterate) High (oracle per step) High (autonomous scope)
L4 500K-2M tokens/run Very high (multi-agent) Very high (fleet scope)
L5 Unbounded Requires meta-verification Unbounded

This table is why Chapter 5 (When Not to Build a Loop) exists. Not every problem benefits from climbing the ladder. The right level is the lowest level that meets the task's actual requirements — never higher.

2.5 Climbing the Ladder Safely

The safe way to increase autonomy is incremental and evidence-based:

  1. Start at L2: Build a guided workflow with human-designed steps and checkpoints. Measure reliability.
  2. Graduate to L3: Once reliability at L2 exceeds 95%, remove the human gate and add automated verification. Measure cost and reliability.
  3. Graduate to L4: Once a single L3 loop handles its task reliably, add specialists only where the single agent demonstrably struggles (context overflow, conflicting tool sets, tasks that benefit from independence).
  4. Never jump to L5 without extensive L4 experience. Self-designing systems are research, not production, for most teams in 2026.

The criteria for each graduation:

  • L2→L3: Your oracle catches >95% of errors the human would have caught, and the cost of oracle operation is <20% of the cost of human review.
  • L3→L4: A single agent's context window is demonstrably insufficient, or task independence would provide >3x speedup with maintained quality.
  • L4→L5: You have >6 months of L4 fleet operation data, your verification infrastructure is mature, and the meta-goal is stable enough that self-modification won't drift.

2.6 Level Annotations in This Book

Throughout the rest of this book, you'll see annotations like:

Levels served: L3, L4

These tell you whether the chapter's content applies to your current situation. If you're building your first L3 loop, you can skip chapters marked L4/L5 on your first read. If you're operating a fleet, the L3 chapters are still foundational — L4 is built on L3 loops, so the fundamentals always apply.

Next: Chapter 3 dissects the anatomy of a loop — the five stages, six building blocks, and the Loop Contract that every subsequent chapter references.

Chapter 3: Anatomy of a Loop

Level: A (Anchor) · ~6,000 words · The book's recurring formalism — every chapter references this

3.1 The Five Stages

Every productive loop — whether a single agent fixing a bug or a fleet auditing a codebase — passes through five stages. The stages may execute in seconds or hours. They may be explicit or implicit. But skip any one of them and the loop either can't start, can't improve, or can't stop.


┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                                                      │
│   ┌──────────┐    ┌──────┐    ┌─────────┐          │
│   │ DISCOVER │───▶│ PLAN │───▶│ EXECUTE │          │
│   └──────────┘    └──────┘    └────┬────┘          │
│                                     │               │
│                                     ▼               │
│   ┌─────────┐                 ┌──────────┐         │
│   │ ITERATE │◀────────────────│  VERIFY  │         │
│   └────┬────┘   (if failed)   └────┬─────┘         │
│        │                            │               │
│        └───────────▶ DISCOVER       │ (if passed)   │
│                    (next cycle)     ▼               │
│                                   STOP              │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Stage 1: Discover

The loop gathers the information it needs to act. This is not passive — the agent actively searches for, retrieves, and synthesizes context relevant to its goal. Discovery answers: "What do I need to know before I can plan?"

In a coding loop: read the error message, find the relevant source files, understand the call chain, identify the test that's failing.

In a research loop: search for sources, identify conflicting claims, find the most authoritative references.

In a security loop: enumerate the attack surface, read the code under review, pull related vulnerability patterns.

Discovery is where most loops fail silently. An agent that plans without adequate discovery is planning in the dark. The output may look confident. It will be wrong.

Key engineering decision: How much discovery is enough? Too little produces bad plans. Too much fills the context window with noise and burns budget on irrelevant information. The answer varies by task, but a good heuristic: discover until you can articulate, in concrete terms, what you're going to do. If the plan would be vague, discovery is insufficient.

Stage 2: Plan

Given what the agent discovered, it produces a plan — a sequence of concrete steps that, if executed correctly, will achieve the goal. The plan is a durable artifact. It lives in the context, in a file, or in a task list. It can be inspected, critiqued, and revised.

In a coding loop: "I'll modify auth.py line 42 to validate the token expiry, add a test case for expired tokens, and run the test suite."

In a content loop: "I'll write sections on X, Y, Z. Each section needs an example and a cross-reference to chapter N."

In a fleet loop: "I'll spawn three specialists — one for each subsystem — have them investigate independently, then synthesize their findings."

The plan is not the prompt. A prompt is what you say to the model. A plan is the model's commitment to itself about what it will do. The plan provides continuity across iterations — if the first attempt fails, the plan tells the next iteration what was tried and what to try differently.

Plans decay. A plan made with incomplete information becomes stale as execution reveals reality. This is why replanning (Chapter 16) exists as a first-class capability. The plan is a living document, not a contract.

Stage 3: Execute

The agent carries out the plan's current step. This is where tools get called, code gets written, files get modified, APIs get hit. Execution is the only stage that changes the world.

Execution must be reversible or isolated. If execution can't be undone (sending an email, deleting a database record), the loop needs an approval gate before the execute step. If execution is reversible (editing a file, writing to a branch), the loop can execute freely and roll back if verification fails.

Execution produces artifacts. Code, text, data, API responses. These artifacts are what the verification stage evaluates. If execution doesn't produce something checkable, the loop has no way to improve.

Stage 4: Verify

The oracle evaluates the execution's output against the goal's acceptance criteria. Verification answers: "Is this done correctly?"

This is the most important stage. Without verification, the loop is just generation — producing output without knowing whether it's good. Verification is what transforms a generation system into a quality system.

Verification can be:

  • Deterministic: Run tests, type-check, lint, parse. Binary pass/fail.
  • Heuristic: Apply a rubric, check against property-based tests, validate structural constraints.
  • Model-based: Use a separate LLM as a judge, with a different context and different incentives than the executor.
  • Human: Escalate to a person for cases the automated oracle can't handle.

The oracle hierarchy (Chapter 20) formalizes the choice. The rule of thumb: use the cheapest oracle that can still discriminate good output from bad.

Stage 5: Iterate

If verification fails, the loop doesn't stop — it feeds the failure information back into a new cycle. The failure signal (test error, rubric score, judge critique) becomes input to the next discovery or planning step.

Good iteration is targeted: "Test X failed because of Y. Modify Z to fix it."

Bad iteration is blind: "That didn't work. Try again."

The quality of the iteration signal determines how many cycles the loop needs to converge. A precise error message from a test suite sends the loop straight to the fix. A vague "the output wasn't good enough" sends it on a random walk through solution space.

Iteration is where cost lives. Each cycle costs tokens, time, and money. The faster the loop converges, the cheaper the result. This is why oracle quality matters so much — a good oracle doesn't just verify, it provides actionable feedback that accelerates convergence.

3.2 The Six Building Blocks

The five stages describe what a loop does. The six building blocks describe what a loop is made of — the infrastructure components that make the stages possible.

Block 1: Automations (The Heartbeat)

An automation is what starts the loop without a human clicking a button. If you start the loop by hand every time, the human is still in the loop — they're just in a different position (trigger rather than reviewer).

Types of automations:


schedule:    "Every weekday at 9am, scan for new PRs"
event:       "When a PR is opened, run the security review loop"
threshold:   "When error rate exceeds 1%, run the diagnosis loop"
webhook:     "When Linear ticket moves to 'ready', start implementation"
chained:     "When loop A completes, start loop B with A's output"
completion:  "Run until all items in the queue are processed"

The rule: if you have to remember to start it, it's not automated enough. A loop's automation is its heartbeat — the thing that keeps it alive without human attention.

Block 2: Worktrees (Isolation)

When multiple agents — or multiple iterations of the same agent — need to modify shared state (files, branches, databases), they collide. Agent A edits line 42 while Agent B edits line 43. Without isolation, the result is corruption.

Worktrees provide isolation: each agent (or each iteration) gets its own copy of the relevant state. In code: a git worktree on a fresh branch. In data: a transaction or a snapshot. In infrastructure: a sandbox environment.


# Claude Code's worktree pattern
git worktree add .claude/worktrees/agent-1 -b agent-1-fix
git worktree add .claude/worktrees/agent-2 -b agent-2-fix

# Agents work in isolation
# Results merge back only after verification

Worktrees are expensive (disk, setup time, merge complexity). Use them only when agents would actually collide. A single-agent loop modifying one file at a time doesn't need isolation. A fleet of five agents all editing the same codebase does.

Block 3: Skills (Reusable Knowledge)

A skill is project-specific knowledge that the loop needs every time it runs. Instead of explaining the project from scratch on every iteration, you encode the knowledge once and load it into context on demand.

A good skill file includes:

  • Vision: What this project does and why
  • Architecture: How the system is structured
  • Rules: Constraints the agent must respect
  • Build/test commands: How to verify output
  • Anti-patterns: Things the agent must never do

# SKILL.md — Example for a web application

## Architecture
- Frontend: React 18, TypeScript strict, Zustand state
- Backend: FastAPI, PostgreSQL 15, Redis cache
- Tests: pytest + Playwright E2E

## Rules
- Never modify migration files after they've been applied
- All API endpoints require authentication middleware
- Error responses must use RFC 7807 format

## Build & Verify
- `npm run build` must pass with zero warnings
- `pytest tests/ -x` must pass before any PR
- `npx playwright test` for UI changes

Without skills, the loop starts cold every run — spending tokens rediscovering what the project is, how it's structured, and what the rules are. With skills, the loop starts warm and spends its budget on the actual work.

Block 4: Connectors (Integration)

A loop that only sees files is limited. Connectors wire the loop into the real tools and systems that production work requires.

Connector What it enables
GitHub/ADO Open PRs, read diffs, post comments, watch CI
Slack/Teams Escalate findings, notify humans, receive commands
Linear/Jira Read tickets, update status, create subtasks
Database Read schema, query data, validate migrations
CI/CD Trigger builds, read test results, gate deployments
Monitoring Read metrics, detect anomalies, correlate with changes

The connector transforms the loop from "here's a suggestion" to "I did the thing, verified it, and updated all the relevant systems." This is the difference between L2 and L3 on the autonomy ladder.

MCP (Model Context Protocol) is the standard connector interface in 2026. Chapter 10 covers it in depth.

Block 5: Subagents (Specialization)

A subagent is a separate execution context with its own prompt, tools, model, and scope. Subagents exist for two reasons:

Reason 1: Context isolation. The maker and the checker should not share context. An agent that wrote code will be generous when reviewing it — it already "knows" what the code is supposed to do, so it fills in gaps with its own intent rather than evaluating what's actually on the page. A separate checker agent, given only the code and the spec, provides an independent evaluation.

Reason 2: Specialization. Different tasks benefit from different configurations. A code-writing agent needs access to the filesystem and test runner. A security-review agent needs access to vulnerability databases and OWASP references. A documentation agent needs access to the API spec and style guide. Mixing all these tools into one context makes each task worse.


# Conceptual fleet structure
orchestrator = Agent(
    model="opus",
    tools=["plan", "delegate", "aggregate"],
    role="Decompose the goal and coordinate specialists"
)

code_agent = Agent(
    model="sonnet",
    tools=["read_file", "write_file", "run_tests", "search_code"],
    role="Implement changes according to the plan"
)

review_agent = Agent(
    model="opus",
    tools=["read_file", "search_code"],
    role="Review changes for correctness and security"
)

Block 6: Memory (Cross-Run Continuity)

A loop that forgets everything between runs is doomed to repeat its mistakes. Memory is what lets a loop learn across invocations — remembering what worked, what failed, what patterns recur, and what the human corrected.

Memory lives at multiple timescales:

  • Working memory: The current context window. Lost on compaction or session end.
  • Episodic memory: Records of specific past events. "Last run, the test failed because of a timezone issue."
  • Semantic memory: Accumulated knowledge. "This codebase uses UTC internally and converts to local time at the display layer."
  • Procedural memory: Learned workflows. "When tests fail with a database error, check if the migration has been applied first."

Memory can be stored in files (markdown notes, JSON logs), databases (structured recall), or model context (loaded from skill files). Chapter 12 covers the full memory architecture.

The critical insight: memory is not just storage. It's consolidation — the process of deduplicating, resolving contradictions, surfacing recurring patterns, and forgetting what's no longer relevant. Anthropic calls this "dreaming" — a background process that curates accumulated memory into useful knowledge.

3.3 The Loop Contract

The Loop Contract is this book's central formalism. Every loop you build must answer these five questions. A loop missing any answer is broken in a specific, predictable way.


loop_contract:
  goal:
    description: "What constitutes done, in machine-checkable terms"
    failure_mode_if_missing: "The loop has no target. It runs but produces nothing useful."
    
  oracle:
    description: "How the loop knows the goal is met"
    failure_mode_if_missing: "The loop cannot verify its own output. It runs once and ships garbage."
    
  budget:
    description: "Maximum resources (tokens, time, dollars, retries) before escalation"
    failure_mode_if_missing: "The loop runs forever, burning money on a problem it can't solve."
    
  stop_condition:
    description: "Precise criteria for termination — success, failure, or indeterminate"
    failure_mode_if_missing: "The loop oscillates, thrashes, or hangs. It never converges."
    
  escalation_path:
    description: "What happens when the loop exhausts budget or detects it cannot succeed"
    failure_mode_if_missing: "The loop fails silently. No one knows. The task is dropped."

Goal

The goal must be machine-checkable. "Make the code better" is not a goal — it's a vibe. "All tests pass, type checker reports zero errors, and the linter produces no warnings" is a goal. The precision of the goal determines the precision of the oracle, which determines the reliability of the loop.

The goal specification skill (Chapter 15) is the single highest-leverage skill in loop engineering. Everything downstream depends on it.

Goals can be:

  • Binary: All tests pass (yes/no)
  • Threshold: Rubric score exceeds 8/10
  • Convergent: No new issues found in two consecutive passes
  • Comparative: Output is better than baseline by metric X

Oracle

The oracle is the mechanism that evaluates the output against the goal. Chapter 20 builds the full oracle hierarchy. Here's the preview:


Cheapest ─────────────────────────────────────────── Most expensive
│ Deterministic │ Property │ Rubric  │ Model   │ Human  │
│ (tests/lint)  │  tests   │ grading │  judge  │ review │
│    $0.00      │  $0.001  │ $0.01   │ $0.10   │ $50    │

The rule: use the cheapest oracle that still discriminates. If tests can tell you the code works, don't use a model judge. If a model judge can tell you the prose is good, don't use a human editor.

Budget

Every loop needs a ceiling. Without one, a loop encountering a hard problem will spend your entire monthly API budget in an afternoon. Budget can be expressed as:

  • Tokens: Maximum 500K tokens per run
  • Time: Maximum 10 minutes wall-clock
  • Money: Maximum $5 per task
  • Retries: Maximum 5 iteration cycles
  • Composite: The first limit hit wins

budget = LoopBudget(
    max_tokens=500_000,
    max_time_seconds=600,
    max_cost_usd=5.00,
    max_iterations=5,
)

Budget isn't just a cost control. It's a convergence signal. If a loop hasn't solved the problem within its budget, either the problem is harder than expected (escalate to human) or the loop is thrashing (stop, diagnose, redesign). Either way, more budget won't help.

Stop Condition

The stop condition tells the loop when to terminate. It's distinct from the oracle (which evaluates quality) because the loop can also stop for reasons other than success:

  • Success: Oracle says the output meets the goal. Ship it.
  • Budget exhausted: The loop has spent its allocation. Escalate.
  • Oscillation detected: The loop is making changes, undoing them, and remaking them. It's stuck.
  • No progress: The last N iterations produced no improvement. The approach is wrong.
  • External signal: A human interrupted, a dependency changed, the context became stale.

def should_stop(state: LoopState) -> StopReason | None:
    if state.oracle_passed:
        return StopReason.SUCCESS
    if state.iterations >= state.budget.max_iterations:
        return StopReason.BUDGET_EXHAUSTED
    if state.last_n_scores_identical(n=3):
        return StopReason.NO_PROGRESS
    if state.detected_oscillation():
        return StopReason.OSCILLATION
    return None  # Continue

Chapter 18 (The Stop Problem) covers this in depth, because getting termination wrong is the most common failure mode in production loops.

Escalation Path

When the loop can't solve the problem, what happens? The answer should never be "nothing." An escalation path ensures that failed loops don't disappear silently.

Escalation options:

  • Notify human: Post to Slack, create a ticket, send an email
  • Reduce scope: Attempt a simpler version of the goal
  • Change approach: Switch to a different strategy (different model, different tools, different decomposition)
  • Park and document: Save the current state, log what was tried, and wait for human guidance

The escalation path is the loop's safety valve. Without it, a loop that encounters a hard problem either burns money forever or fails silently. With it, the loop degrades gracefully.

3.4 ReAct as the Primitive Loop

The Loop Contract formalizes what ReAct (Reason + Act) introduced informally in 2022. ReAct's pattern — Think, Act, Observe, Think — maps directly to our five stages:

ReAct Loop Stage What happens
Think Discover + Plan The model reasons about what it knows and what to do
Act Execute The model calls a tool or performs an action
Observe Verify The model reads the result of the action
Think (next) Iterate The model reasons about what to do differently

ReAct is the primitive loop — the simplest possible implementation. Every more sophisticated pattern (reflection, tree-of-thought, self-consistency, multi-agent debate) is a loop built on top of this primitive. Understanding ReAct as a loop (not as a "prompting technique") is the conceptual key to everything that follows.


# ReAct as the primitive loop — explicit five-stage decomposition
def react_loop(goal: str, tools: list, max_iterations: int = 10) -> Result:
    context = discover(goal)  # Stage 1: Gather initial context
    
    for i in range(max_iterations):
        plan = think(context, goal)              # Stage 2: Plan next action
        action_result = act(plan, tools)         # Stage 3: Execute
        verification = observe(action_result)    # Stage 4: Verify
        
        if verification.goal_met:
            return Result(success=True, output=action_result)
        
        context = iterate(context, verification) # Stage 5: Feed back
    
    return Result(success=False, reason="budget_exhausted")

3.5 Filling the Contract Part by Part

The rest of this book fills in each element of the Loop Contract with increasing precision:

  • Part III (Building the Loop) teaches you to specify goals and stop conditions
  • Part IV (Verification) teaches you to build oracles
  • Part V (Fleets) teaches you to design escalation paths for multi-agent systems
  • Part VI (Economics) teaches you to set and enforce budgets
  • Part VII (Hardening) teaches you to make all five elements robust against adversarial conditions

By the time you finish the book, you'll be able to write a complete Loop Contract for any task — and more importantly, you'll know what breaks when each element is weak.

3.6 A Complete Example: The Bug-Fix Loop

Let's see the full anatomy in action. Here's a bug-fix loop, annotated with stages and contract elements:


loop_contract:
  goal: "The failing test test_user_login_expired_token passes, and no existing tests regress"
  oracle: "pytest tests/ -x exits 0"
  budget: {max_iterations: 5, max_tokens: 200_000, max_time: 300s}
  stop_condition: "All tests pass OR budget exhausted OR oscillation detected"
  escalation_path: "Create GitHub issue with diagnosis, tag team lead"

The loop execution:


DISCOVER: Read the failing test. Read the error message. Find the source file.
           Read related code. Understand the expected behavior.
           
PLAN:     "The test expects a 401 response when token is expired, but the code
           doesn't check token expiry. I'll add an expiry check in auth.py
           at line 42, before the existing validation."

EXECUTE:  Edit auth.py:42. Add: if token.expired: return Response(401)

VERIFY:   Run pytest tests/ -x
           Result: 2 tests fail — the new test passes but test_valid_token broke

ITERATE:  "My fix broke test_valid_token. The issue is that I'm checking expired
           before checking validity. I need to restructure: check validity first,
           then check expiry."

DISCOVER: Read test_valid_token to understand what it expects.

PLAN:     "Restructure: validate format → check expiry → check signature"

EXECUTE:  Rewrite auth.py:40-50 with the new order.

VERIFY:   Run pytest tests/ -x
           Result: All 47 tests pass ✓

STOP:     Oracle passed. Ship it.

Two iterations. Under budget. The loop verified its own work, caught its own regression, fixed the regression, and confirmed success — all without a human reviewing any intermediate step.

This is what loop engineering produces: verified outcomes at machine speed. The rest of this book teaches you to build loops that work this reliably across every domain, at every scale.

Next: Chapter 4 explains why the harness — the scaffolding around the model — matters more than the model itself for loop quality.

Chapter 4: The Harness Is the Product

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Why scaffold quality dominates model quality

4.1 The Same Model, Wildly Different Outcomes

In March 2025, Anthropic released Claude 3.5 Sonnet. Within weeks, three different products shipped it as their backbone:

  • Product A wrapped Sonnet in a simple chat interface with file reading. Users reported ~40% task completion on SWE-bench-style problems.
  • Product B built a loop: read → plan → edit → test → fix. Same model. Same problems. Task completion jumped to ~65%.
  • Product C added skills (project context loaded on demand), worktrees (parallel editing without conflict), and a maker-checker split. Same model. Task completion hit ~78%.

The model didn't change. The weights were identical. What changed was the harness — the code that wraps the model, manages its context, selects its tools, verifies its output, and decides what happens next.

This isn't a hypothetical. It's the documented history of SWE-bench, where identical base models score dramatically differently depending on the scaffold:

System Base Model SWE-bench Score Key Harness Feature
Raw prompt Claude 3.5 Sonnet 19% None — single pass
Agentless Claude 3.5 Sonnet 27% Localization + patch generation
SWE-agent Claude 3.5 Sonnet 33% Interactive shell + error feedback
Devin Claude 3.5 Sonnet* 42% Full loop + test + plan + retry
Claude Code Claude 3.5 Sonnet 49% Loop + skills + tools + memory

(*Devin's exact model mix is proprietary; the point is the harness delta, not the specific model.)

The spread from 19% to 49% — a 2.5x improvement — comes entirely from the harness. No model upgrade provides that kind of lift. This is the central insight of this chapter: if you're choosing between investing in a better model and investing in a better harness, invest in the harness. The returns are larger and more predictable.

4.2 What a Harness Owes the Model

A model inside a harness is like an employee inside an organization. The employee brings skill and judgment. The organization provides structure, tools, information, and feedback. A brilliant employee in a dysfunctional organization produces mediocre work. A competent employee in a well-run organization produces excellent work.

Here's what the harness owes the model:

4.2.1 The Right Context at the Right Time

The model can only reason about what's in its context window. A harness that dumps an entire codebase into context overwhelms the model with noise. A harness that provides too little context forces the model to guess. The optimal harness provides:

  • Relevant context: only what matters for the current step
  • Structured context: organized so the model can parse it quickly
  • Fresh context: updated as the loop progresses, not stale from the start
  • Sized context: within the model's effective attention range

The "smallest high-signal set" principle (Chapter 7) governs this. Every token in context should earn its place. If removing it would degrade performance, it stays. If removing it wouldn't matter, it's noise.

4.2.2 Clear Affordances

The model needs to know what it can do. A harness with thirty tools but no description of when to use each one produces a model that flails between tools randomly. A harness with clear, well-documented affordances produces a model that selects the right tool for each situation.

Good tool affordances include:

  • A name that describes the action (not tool_7, but run_tests)
  • A description that states when to use it (not "executes code", but "run the test suite to verify your changes compile and pass")
  • Parameters with types and constraints
  • Error messages that guide recovery

4.2.3 Actionable Feedback

When the model's output is wrong, the harness must tell it why in terms the model can act on. "Test failed" is feedback. "Test test_auth_expired_token failed: expected status 401, got 200. The response body indicates the token expiry check is not being reached — see auth.py:42" is actionable feedback.

The quality of feedback determines convergence speed. Vague feedback → random walk → slow convergence → high cost. Precise feedback → targeted fix → fast convergence → low cost.

4.2.4 Protection from Irreversible Mistakes

The model will, inevitably, attempt actions that shouldn't be taken. Delete production data. Force-push to main. Send an email to a customer. The harness must prevent these actions, not rely on the model to avoid them.

Protection mechanisms:

  • Permission scoping: The model literally cannot call tools that would cause irreversible harm
  • Approval gates: Destructive actions require human confirmation
  • Sandboxing: Execution happens in an isolated environment where mistakes can't propagate
  • Dry-run mode: The model can see what an action would do without actually doing it

4.3 The Harness Quality Hierarchy

Not all harnesses are created equal. Here's a hierarchy from worst to best, with the marginal improvement at each level:

Level 1: Prompt + Model (Baseline)

The model receives a prompt and responds. No tools, no iteration, no verification. This is the floor.

Level 2: Prompt + Tools

The model can call tools (read files, search, execute code). It responds to a single prompt, using tools as needed, and produces one output. The human evaluates.

Marginal improvement over Level 1: the model can ground its responses in real data rather than relying on training knowledge.

Level 3: Prompt + Tools + Error Recovery

When a tool call fails or produces unexpected results, the harness feeds the error back and lets the model retry. This is a minimal loop — one iteration of execute → verify → iterate.

Marginal improvement over Level 2: the model can recover from tool-use errors without human intervention.

Level 4: Prompt + Tools + Iteration Loop

The harness implements a full loop: the model plans, executes, verifies against criteria, and iterates until criteria are met or budget is exhausted.

Marginal improvement over Level 3: the model can achieve complex goals that require multiple steps and self-correction.

Level 5: Prompt + Tools + Iteration + Skills + Memory

The harness loads project-specific knowledge (skills) so the model starts warm. It records what worked and what didn't (memory) so future runs benefit from past experience.

Marginal improvement over Level 4: the model doesn't repeat past mistakes and doesn't spend budget rediscovering context.

Level 6: Full Harness (Iteration + Skills + Memory + Subagents + Automation)

The harness orchestrates multiple models, each with its own tools and scope. It runs automatically on triggers. It learns and evolves across runs.

Marginal improvement over Level 5: the system handles problems too complex for a single context, parallelizes work, and provides independent verification.

4.4 Model Choice Is a Harness Decision

The conventional wisdom is to pick the "best" model and build around it. Loop engineering inverts this: the harness determines which model is cost-effective at which step.

Consider a fleet loop for code review:

Step Task Model Needed Why
Discovery Read files, find relevant code Haiku Simple retrieval, doesn't need reasoning
Planning Identify what to review, in what order Sonnet Moderate reasoning about priority
Review Find bugs, security issues, logic errors Opus Deep reasoning, catches subtle issues
Verification Check if the review findings are real Sonnet Confirm/deny claims against code
Summarization Write the final report Haiku Formatting, no reasoning needed

A naive approach uses Opus for everything — expensive. A smart harness cascades: cheap models for cheap steps, expensive models only where they provide unique value. The harness makes the model choice, dynamically, per step.

This is why "which model should I use?" is the wrong question. The right question is: "what does each step of my loop actually require, and what's the cheapest model that provides it?"

4.5 The Scaffold Advantage Is Compounding

Better prompts produce a linear improvement: each prompt improvement helps one task by a fixed amount. Better harnesses produce a compounding improvement: each harness improvement helps every subsequent run of every loop that uses it.

Consider these harness improvements and their compound effect:

  • Add test-running to the loop → every code task now self-verifies (saves ~30% of human review time per task)
  • Add skills → every run starts warm (saves ~15% of tokens per run)
  • Add memory → recurring patterns get handled automatically (saves ~10% of iteration cycles)
  • Add maker-checker split → quality issues caught before shipping (reduces post-ship bugs by ~40%)

Individually, each is modest. Together, over hundreds of runs, they transform the system from "AI assistant that helps a little" to "autonomous system that ships verified work."

4.6 Why Teams Underinvest in the Harness

Despite the evidence, most teams spend 90% of their effort on prompts and 10% on the harness. Three reasons:

1. Prompts are immediately gratifying. You change a prompt, rerun, and see the result in seconds. Harness improvements require building infrastructure, and the payoff only shows up over many runs.

2. Prompts feel like "AI work." Harnesses feel like "plumbing." Engineers want to work on the interesting part (the AI) and neglect the mundane part (the scaffold). But the mundane part is where the value compounds.

3. Model providers incentivize prompt thinking. The industry's marketing focuses on model capabilities, not scaffold capabilities. "Try our new model — it's smarter!" is a simpler sales pitch than "build a better loop around our model."

The antidote is measurement. Track your loops' cost-per-verified-outcome over time. When you invest in the harness, that number goes down reliably. When you invest in prompts, it goes down sporadically. The data makes the case.

4.7 What the Harness Is Not

The harness is not:

  • A framework. Frameworks (LangChain, CrewAI, AutoGen) provide primitives for building harnesses. The harness is your specific implementation for your specific loops. The framework is the toolbox. The harness is the house.
  • A prompt library. Prompts are components of the harness. The harness includes prompts, but also tools, verification logic, state management, error recovery, and orchestration.
  • The model itself. The harness wraps the model. The model is replaceable. If you switch from Sonnet to Opus (or vice versa), the harness should still work — it just works differently because the model's capabilities changed.
  • Deterministic automation. A traditional automation (CI/CD pipeline, cron job, ETL pipeline) follows a fixed path. A harness adapts: the model's reasoning determines the path through the harness, within the constraints the harness defines.

The harness is the environment in which the model operates. It provides structure, resources, feedback, and boundaries. The model provides reasoning, creativity, and judgment within those boundaries. The quality of the outcome is a product of both — but the harness has more variance and more upside.

4.8 Building Your Harness Incrementally

You don't build a Level 6 harness on day one. You build incrementally, adding capability as you encounter limitations:

Week 1: Basic loop — model + tools + run-tests-on-output. This alone gives you 80% of the value.

Week 2: Add skills — load project context so the model stops asking "what testing framework do you use?" every run.

Week 3: Add memory — record what worked and what didn't across runs so the model stops repeating the same mistakes.

Week 4: Add maker-checker — a separate verification agent that evaluates the main agent's output with fresh context.

Month 2: Add automation — trigger loops on events (PR opened, ticket moved, schedule) so humans don't have to remember to start them.

Month 3: Add fleet orchestration — multiple specialists, coordinated by a lead, for tasks too complex for a single agent.

Each step is independently valuable. Each step builds on the previous one. The harness grows with your confidence and your understanding of where the bottlenecks are.

4.9 The Meta-Insight

The harness is the product because the harness is what you ship. Users don't interact with the model — they interact with the system. The system's reliability, speed, cost, and correctness are all harness properties, not model properties.

When a user says "this AI is good," they mean the harness is good. When they say "this AI is unreliable," they mean the harness doesn't verify output adequately. When they say "this AI is too expensive," they mean the harness doesn't control cost effectively.

Model capability is necessary but not sufficient. Harness quality is what transforms model capability into user value. This is why the rest of this book focuses on harness engineering — the specific techniques for building loops that verify, iterate, and ship without human gates.

Next: Part II opens with Chapter 5 — a sobering counterweight that asks when NOT to build a loop, because not every problem deserves one.

Chapter 5: When Not to Build a Loop

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: All

5.1 The Automation Trap

Every new capability creates a temptation to apply it everywhere. Loops are powerful. They ship verified work without human gates. Therefore — the reasoning goes — every task should be a loop.

This reasoning is wrong. Loops carry costs that simple workflows don't: token burn on iteration, oracle maintenance, failure mode complexity, and the cognitive overhead of debugging autonomous systems. A loop that costs $3 to produce what a human could produce in 30 seconds isn't engineering — it's performance art.

Before building a loop, pass the task through three gates. If it fails any one, don't build a loop. Build a simpler system instead.

5.2 Gate 1: Verifiability

The question: Can you write a machine-checkable oracle for this task's output?

If you can't verify the output automatically, you can't close the loop. Without an oracle, the loop degenerates into generation-without-verification — which is just a single prompt with extra steps and extra cost.

Tasks that pass Gate 1:

  • Code generation: Tests verify correctness. Type checkers verify safety. Linters verify style.
  • Data transformation: Schema validation. Input/output pair comparison. Row counts and checksums.
  • Structured content: Format validation. Required-field checks. Cross-reference consistency.
  • Classification: Ground-truth labels exist for evaluation.

Tasks that fail Gate 1:

  • Creative writing with no rubric: "Write something beautiful" has no oracle. (But "Write a paragraph that scores 8+ on these five criteria" does — so reframe before rejecting.)
  • Strategic decisions: "Should we enter the European market?" cannot be mechanically verified.
  • Taste judgments: "Is this logo good?" depends on subjective evaluation that doesn't reduce to computable criteria.
  • Novel research: "Discover something new about protein folding" has no oracle because you don't know the answer yet.

The reframe test: Before concluding a task is unverifiable, ask: "Could I write a rubric?" Many seemingly unverifiable tasks become verifiable once you define explicit criteria. "Write good documentation" fails Gate 1. "Write documentation that covers all public API methods, includes at least one example per method, and passes the readability formula at grade 8" passes.

If you can't write the rubric and you can't reframe the task into something with a rubric — don't build a loop. Use a single-pass model call with human review.

5.3 Gate 2: Unit Economics

The question: Is the cost of loop operation (tokens + oracle + infrastructure) less than the cost of human operation for the same task?

A loop that costs more than a human is a net loss. This sounds obvious, but it's surprisingly easy to build expensive loops for cheap tasks.

The math:


Loop cost per task = (avg_iterations × tokens_per_iteration × cost_per_token)
                   + oracle_cost_per_verification
                   + infrastructure_overhead

Human cost per task = hourly_rate × time_in_hours

Build a loop when: loop_cost_per_task × volume < human_cost_per_task × volume + loop_build_cost

Where the math kills loops:

Low-volume tasks: If a task happens once a week, the build cost of the loop may never amortize. A 2-hour loop build that saves 5 minutes per run needs 24 runs to break even. At once-per-week, that's 6 months.

Fast human tasks: If a human can do the task in under a minute (quick review, simple classification, obvious fix), the loop's iteration overhead likely exceeds the human cost. Don't automate tasks that are already fast.

Tasks that rarely fail: If the model gets it right on the first pass 98% of the time, the verification and iteration machinery exists for 2% of cases. The infrastructure cost of maintaining the oracle for those 2% may exceed the cost of human review for the 2%.

Where the math favors loops:

High-volume tasks: Processing 500 PRs per day for security review. At 10 minutes human review each, that's 83 hours of human time daily. A loop at $0.50 per PR costs $250/day vs. $6,000+/day in human cost.

Human-slow tasks: Tasks where human review takes 30+ minutes each but loop verification takes seconds. Deep code review, comprehensive test generation, multi-source research synthesis.

Consistency-critical tasks: Tasks where human fatigue introduces errors. The 400th PR reviewed at 4pm on Friday gets less attention than the first PR on Monday morning. Loops don't get tired.

5.4 Gate 3: Blast Radius

The question: If the loop produces wrong output and ships it, what's the worst-case damage?

Loops can fail. Oracles can miss. The question isn't whether failure is possible — it's whether failure is survivable.

Low blast radius (loop-friendly):

  • Generating a draft that a human will review before publishing
  • Creating a PR that requires human approval before merging
  • Producing an internal report that informs but doesn't trigger action
  • Running analysis on a copy of data, not production data

High blast radius (loop-hostile):

  • Sending emails to customers
  • Deploying to production
  • Modifying financial records
  • Deleting data
  • Publishing content publicly
  • Making irreversible infrastructure changes

The mitigation spectrum:

High blast radius doesn't automatically disqualify a loop. It means the loop needs stronger safeguards:

Blast Radius Mitigation Loop Example
None No mitigation needed Generate an internal summary
Low Automated oracle sufficient Fix a failing test on a feature branch
Medium Oracle + dry-run + human approval before final action Generate a PR with changes
High Oracle + approval gate + canary + rollback plan Deploy a configuration change
Critical Don't loop. Human in the loop. Always. Delete customer data

5.5 The Workflow Alternative

When a task fails one or more gates, the answer isn't always "just use a human." There's a middle ground: deterministic workflows with an LLM step.

A workflow is a fixed pipeline where each step is predetermined by the developer. An LLM fills one or more slots in the pipeline, but the LLM doesn't decide what happens next — the pipeline does.


Workflow (deterministic):
  Step 1: Fetch data from API
  Step 2: Call LLM to transform data
  Step 3: Validate output schema
  Step 4: Store in database

Loop (adaptive):
  Step 1: LLM decides what data to fetch
  Step 2: LLM fetches and transforms
  Step 3: LLM evaluates its own output
  Step 4: LLM decides whether to retry or proceed

Workflows are cheaper (no iteration), simpler (no oracle needed beyond schema validation), and more predictable (fixed paths are debuggable). They sacrifice adaptability — the system can't recover from novel failures — but for many tasks, adaptability isn't needed.

The decision rule: If the task has a known path from input to output and the only uncertainty is "can the LLM fill this slot correctly?" — build a workflow, not a loop. Use a loop only when the path itself is uncertain or when iteration is needed to reach quality.

5.6 The Hybrid Pattern

Many production systems combine workflows and loops:


Workflow level:
  1. Trigger: PR opened
  2. Fetch diff (deterministic)
  3. LOOP: Review diff for issues (iterate until confident)
  4. Post review comment (deterministic)
  5. LOOP: If author replies, re-evaluate (iterate until resolved)
  6. Cast vote (deterministic)

The workflow provides the skeleton — the fixed sequence of what happens. The loops fill in the adaptive parts — where the system needs to reason, iterate, and verify. This hybrid gives you the predictability of workflows with the quality of loops, at the cost of complexity.

5.7 The Decision Matrix

Pull it all together into a quick-reference matrix:

Verifiable? Economical? Low blast? Build
Yes Yes Yes Full loop — autonomous, iterate until done
Yes Yes No Loop + approval gate — loop produces, human ships
Yes No Yes Workflow with retry — fixed path, one retry on failure
Yes No No Workflow + human review — LLM slot, human gate
No Yes Yes Single pass + human eval — generate once, human checks
No Yes No Human only — don't automate
No No * Human only — don't automate

5.8 Antipatterns

Common mistakes that violate these gates:

The Gold-Plated Loop: Building a full fleet with specialists, memory, and cross-run learning for a task that happens three times a month. The infrastructure cost exceeds the value for years.

The Unverifiable Loop: "Generate marketing copy that resonates with our audience." There's no oracle. The loop iterates blindly, producing variants but never converging on quality. The human still reviews everything. You've built an expensive random-content generator.

The High-Stakes YOLO: Running an autonomous loop that posts to social media, sends emails, or modifies production infrastructure without a human gate. When it fails — and it will — the damage is immediate and public.

The Premature Fleet: Splitting a task across five specialist agents when a single agent with a good skill file would handle it in one context window. Fleets add communication overhead, coordination complexity, and cost. Use them only when a single agent demonstrably can't handle the task.

The Infinite Oracle: Building a verification system that costs more than the generation it verifies. If your oracle is a full manual test run by a QA team, the loop hasn't automated anything — it's just added a generation step before the existing human process.

5.9 The Meta-Rule

When in doubt, start with the simplest thing that could work:

  1. Single model call with human review
  2. Model call with automated validation (schema, type, format)
  3. Model call with retry on validation failure
  4. Loop with oracle and budget
  5. Fleet with specialists and orchestration

Escalate only when the simpler approach demonstrably fails. Each escalation adds cost, complexity, and failure modes. The right level is the lowest level that meets the task's actual requirements.
