Chapter 6: LLMs as Unreliable Reasoning Engines

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

6.1 The Reliability Illusion

Large language models feel reliable on any single call. Ask Claude to write a function, and it produces something correct 90-95% of the time. Ask it to explain a concept, and the explanation is sound 95%+ of the time. The per-step accuracy is impressive enough that most practitioners trust it implicitly.

This trust breaks down over sequences. A loop doesn't make one call — it makes five, ten, fifty. Each call introduces a small probability of error. Those probabilities compound multiplicatively, not additively. And the result is catastrophic failure rates that the per-step accuracy completely masks.

6.2 The Compounding Math

The fundamental equation of loop reliability:


P(all steps correct) = P(step correct) ^ number_of_steps

This is the product rule of independent probabilities. If each step has accuracy p, then a sequence of n steps has accuracy p^n.

The numbers are sobering:

Per-Step Accuracy 5 Steps 10 Steps 20 Steps 50 Steps
99% 95.1% 90.4% 81.8% 60.5%
95% 77.4% 59.9% 35.8% 7.7%
90% 59.0% 34.9% 12.2% 0.5%
85% 44.4% 19.7% 3.9% 0.03%

At 95% per-step accuracy — which is genuinely excellent for any individual LLM call — a 20-step sequence fails two-thirds of the time. A 50-step sequence fails 92% of the time.

This isn't a criticism of LLMs. It's mathematics. The same math applies to any system with independent failure modes: network packets, manufacturing tolerances, sequential test execution. The difference is that those domains have decades of engineering practice around error correction. LLM-based systems are only now developing theirs.

6.3 Why Steps Aren't Independent

The situation is actually worse than the naive calculation suggests, because steps in an LLM loop are not independent. Errors correlate and cascade:

Correlated failures

If a model misunderstands the project architecture on step 1 (discovery), every subsequent step builds on that misunderstanding. Steps 2-20 don't each have an independent 5% failure chance — they have a 5% chance of failure given that step 1 was correct. If step 1 was wrong, steps 2-20 may all be wrong with much higher probability.

Error amplification

A small error in step 3 can produce a larger error in step 7. The model makes an off-by-one error in an array index. Later, it uses that index in a calculation. Still later, it uses that calculation in a condition. The original off-by-one becomes a logic error that's much harder to detect and fix.

Confidence drift

Models don't distinguish between things they know and things they believe. If a model hallucinates a fact in step 5 and that fact enters the context, the model will treat it as ground truth in all subsequent steps. The hallucination doesn't get weaker with repetition — it gets stronger, because now it has the reinforcement of appearing in the conversation history.

6.4 The Decomposition Consequence

The compounding math has a direct engineering consequence: loop length must be inversely proportional to per-step unreliability.

If your model achieves 95% per-step accuracy, you can run approximately 5-7 steps before cumulative reliability drops below 75%. Beyond that, you need verification checkpoints.

If your model achieves 99% per-step accuracy (only achievable on narrow, well-specified tasks with clear success criteria), you can run 10-15 steps safely.

This creates the decomposition imperative: complex goals must be broken into sub-goals, each achievable in a short sequence, with verification between sub-goals. The verification step resets the error accumulation.


Without verification:
  Step 1 → Step 2 → Step 3 → ... → Step 20
  Cumulative reliability: 0.95^20 = 35.8%

With verification every 5 steps:
  [Step 1 → ... → Step 5] → VERIFY → [Step 6 → ... → Step 10] → VERIFY → ...
  Per-segment reliability: 0.95^5 = 77.4%
  4 segments × 77.4% × detection correction = much higher overall

The verification step serves two functions:

  1. Error detection: Catch failures before they propagate
  2. Context reset: If the verification step feeds clean state into the next segment, you reset the error accumulation

6.5 Error Categories in LLM Reasoning

Not all errors are equal. Understanding the error taxonomy helps you build targeted verification:

Factual errors (hallucination)

The model states something that isn't true. "This function is defined in utils.py" when it's actually in helpers.py. "The API requires authentication" when it doesn't.

Detection: Ground-truth comparison. Check the claim against reality (read the file, test the API, verify the assertion).

Logic errors

The model reasons incorrectly. "Since A implies B, and we have B, therefore A." (Affirming the consequent.) "The function handles null because it checks for empty string." (Null ≠ empty string.)

Detection: Property testing. Check logical invariants that the output must satisfy regardless of the specific content.

Omission errors

The model forgets to handle a case. Edge cases, error paths, race conditions, boundary values — things that require actively thinking about what's not there.

Detection: Coverage analysis. Checklist validation against known requirements.

Drift errors

The model gradually moves away from the original intent. Each step is locally reasonable, but the overall direction diverges from the goal. The model rewrites a function for "clarity" and subtly changes its semantics.

Detection: Goal-level verification. Compare the output against the original specification, not just against the previous step.

Sycophancy errors

The model agrees with implied preferences in the prompt rather than stating the truth. If the context suggests a certain approach is preferred, the model will validate that approach even if it's flawed.

Detection: Adversarial checker. A separate agent, prompted to find flaws, without the context that created the bias.

6.6 The Verification Dividend

Here's the good news: verification per step doesn't need to match generation accuracy. Even imperfect verification dramatically improves cumulative reliability.


def loop_with_verification(
    per_step_accuracy: float,
    error_detection_rate: float,
    correction_accuracy: float,
    steps: int
) -> float:
    """
    Model: after each step, an oracle catches errors with probability
    detection_rate. Caught errors are corrected with probability
    correction_accuracy.
    """
    effective_accuracy = (
        per_step_accuracy +
        (1 - per_step_accuracy) * error_detection_rate * correction_accuracy
    )
    return effective_accuracy ** steps

With 95% per-step accuracy, 80% error detection, and 90% correction accuracy:

  • Effective per-step: 95% + 5% × 80% × 90% = 98.6%
  • Over 20 steps: 98.6%^20 = 75.5% (vs. 35.8% without verification)

With 95% per-step accuracy, 95% error detection, and 95% correction:

  • Effective per-step: 95% + 5% × 95% × 95% = 99.5%
  • Over 20 steps: 99.5%^20 = 90.5%

Imperfect verification still provides a dramatic improvement. You don't need a perfect oracle. You need a good-enough oracle that catches most errors and provides actionable feedback for correction.

6.7 Practical Implications for Loop Design

The reliability math drives five concrete design principles:

Principle 1: Short steps with frequent verification

Don't plan a 20-step sequence and run it end-to-end. Plan 4 segments of 5 steps, with verification between segments. The cumulative reliability per segment is much higher than end-to-end.

Principle 2: Decompose until steps are verifiable

If you can't verify the output of a step, you can't detect errors at that step. Decompose until each step produces something checkable. "Improve the codebase" is not verifiable. "Make this specific test pass" is.

Principle 3: Invest in oracle quality proportional to sequence length

Short loops (3-5 steps) can tolerate weak oracles. Long loops (15+ steps) need strong oracles at each checkpoint, or error accumulation will destroy reliability regardless of model quality.

Principle 4: Use independent verification

The agent that produced the output is the worst judge of that output. It shares the context, including any misconceptions, that produced the error in the first place. Independent verification — a separate model, a separate context, or a deterministic check — catches errors the producer is blind to.

Principle 5: Budget for iteration, not just execution

A 20-step task at 95% per-step accuracy will fail 64% of the time without verification. With verification and correction, it will succeed ~90% of the time — but only if the loop has budget for the correction iterations. Budget 2-3x the minimum tokens needed for a single pass.

6.8 The Model Quality Investment

Given these realities, where should you invest in model quality?

The high-leverage investment: Improve per-step accuracy from 90% to 95%. This changes 20-step reliability from 12.2% to 35.8% — still bad, but it reduces the correction burden dramatically.

The diminishing-returns investment: Improve per-step accuracy from 97% to 99%. Over 20 steps, this improves from 54.4% to 81.8%. Meaningful, but expensive in model cost. The same improvement could often be achieved cheaper by improving the oracle.

The practical approach: Use the best model you can afford at the per-step level, then invest heavily in verification. A 95%-accurate model with a 90%-detection oracle outperforms a 99%-accurate model with no oracle for any sequence longer than 10 steps.

6.9 Why This Makes Loops Necessary, Not Optional

The compounding failure math seems like an argument against loops. "If LLMs fail over sequences, don't run sequences."

The opposite is true. The compounding math is an argument for loops — specifically, for loops with verification. Without a loop, you have a single-pass generation with no error correction. The output quality is exactly the model's one-shot accuracy. For simple tasks, that's fine. For complex tasks requiring multiple decisions, the math guarantees failure.

Only a loop — with verification, correction, and iteration — can achieve reliable outcomes on multi-step tasks. The loop doesn't eliminate unreliability. It contains it. Each verification step bounds the error, and each correction step reduces it. The result is a system that reliably produces correct output despite being built on an unreliable foundation.

This is not a new engineering pattern. Error-correcting codes, TCP/IP, and RAID arrays all achieve reliability from unreliable components through redundancy and verification. Loop engineering is the LLM-native version of the same principle: reliable systems from unreliable parts, through structured verification and iteration.

Next: Chapter 7 tackles the context window as a budget — the engineering discipline of putting exactly the right information in front of the model at exactly the right time.

Chapter 7: Context Engineering I — The Window Is a Budget

Level: A (Anchor) · ~6,000 words · Levels served: L2, L3, L4, L5

7.1 Context as the Scarce Resource

In 2024, the bottleneck was model capability — the model couldn't reason well enough. In 2026, the bottleneck has shifted. Models are capable enough for most tasks. The bottleneck is now context — getting the right information into the model's window at the right time.

A context window is a budget. Every token in that window has a cost (literal dollars) and an opportunity cost (displaces potentially more useful information). The engineering discipline of context management is about maximizing the signal-to-noise ratio within a fixed budget.

The numbers make this concrete:

  • A 200K-token context window costs $0.60 to fill at $3/M input tokens
  • Every unnecessary token is money spent on noise
  • Attention quality degrades with context length — information at position 100K gets less attention than information at position 1K
  • A loop that runs 10 iterations with a poorly-managed context spends $6 on noise that degrades quality with each iteration

Context engineering isn't optimization for the sake of optimization. It's a prerequisite for loop reliability. A model reasoning over noisy, irrelevant context makes worse decisions at every step. Those worse decisions compound across iterations (Chapter 6). The result: expensive loops that produce poor output.

7.2 The Smallest High-Signal Set Principle

The goal of context engineering is to present the model with the smallest set of information that enables correct reasoning about the current step. Not the maximum information. Not "everything that might be relevant." The minimum sufficient signal.

This principle has three components:

Smallest

Less context is better than more, all else being equal. Every additional token:

  • Increases cost
  • Dilutes attention
  • Introduces potential noise that could mislead reasoning
  • Competes with truly relevant information for the model's limited attention budget

High-Signal

The information that IS in context must be genuinely useful for the current decision. Test: if you removed this piece of context, would the model's output quality degrade? If yes, it earns its place. If no, it's noise.

Set

Context is a collection, and the collection matters. Individual pieces of information may be low-value in isolation but essential when combined with others. The auth.py file alone doesn't explain the bug — but auth.py + the failing test + the error message together make the fix obvious.

7.3 Context Rot

Context doesn't stay fresh. In a loop, the window accumulates the history of every previous iteration: old plans that were superseded, tool outputs that are no longer relevant, error messages from bugs already fixed, reasoning about approaches already abandoned.

This is context rot — the progressive contamination of the window with stale information that was once relevant but now only adds noise.


Iteration 1: [goal + discovery + plan + execution + failure]
Iteration 2: [goal + discovery + plan + execution + failure + OLD failure from iter 1]
Iteration 3: [goal + discovery + plan + OLD plan from iter 1 + OLD plan from iter 2 + ...]
...
Iteration 8: [90% stale context, 10% current information]

By iteration 8, the model is reasoning primarily over irrelevant history. Its decisions degrade not because the model got dumber, but because the signal-to-noise ratio in its context collapsed.

Symptoms of context rot:

  • The model references information from earlier iterations that's no longer current
  • The model "forgets" recent corrections because they're buried under old context
  • The model's output quality degrades monotonically with iteration count
  • Token usage per iteration grows without corresponding increase in output quality

Solutions to context rot:

  1. Aggressive pruning: Remove tool outputs, old plans, and resolved errors after each iteration
  2. Summarization: Replace verbose history with a compact summary of what was learned
  3. Sliding window: Only keep the last N iterations in full detail
  4. Structured state: Maintain a small "current state" document that's updated each iteration, replacing the full history with the current situation

7.4 Attention Decay

Even within a single context window, not all positions are equal. Models exhibit attention decay — information early and late in the context gets more attention than information in the middle. This is the "lost in the middle" phenomenon documented extensively in research.

The practical consequence: where you place information in the context matters as much as what information you include.


High attention ──────────────────────────────── High attention
[System prompt]  [Middle content]  [Recent content + user message]
     ↑               ↑                    ↑
 Well-attended    Under-attended        Well-attended

Placement strategy for loops:

Information Optimal Position Why
Loop Contract (goal, oracle, budget) System prompt (start) Always visible, never buried
Current state / what's been tried Just before the current step Most recent = most attended
Skills / project knowledge Early system context Stable, foundational
Tool results from current step End of context (most recent) Needs immediate attention
History of past iterations Middle (if kept) or removed Least important for current step

7.5 Just-In-Time Retrieval

The alternative to pre-loading context is just-in-time retrieval: loading information only when the model needs it, at the moment it needs it.

Pre-loaded context (RAG-style):


[System prompt + 50K tokens of potentially-relevant documentation]
→ Model reasons over everything, mostly ignoring 80% of it
→ Cost: high. Quality: moderate. The relevant 20% competes with the irrelevant 80%.

Just-in-time retrieval (agentic search):


[System prompt + goal + current step]
→ Model decides what information it needs
→ Model calls search/read tools to retrieve ONLY that information
→ Model reasons over a small, highly-relevant context
→ Cost: lower (fewer input tokens). Quality: higher (pure signal).

The tradeoff: just-in-time retrieval requires an extra inference step (deciding what to search for) and tool-call latency. But for complex tasks where the relevant information depends on what the model discovers during execution, just-in-time retrieval dramatically outperforms pre-loaded context.

When to pre-load vs. retrieve just-in-time:

Pre-load (RAG) Just-in-time (Agentic Search)
Relevant set is small and known in advance Relevant set depends on what the model discovers
Information is stable across iterations Information changes with each step
Task is simple enough that more context doesn't confuse Task is complex enough that irrelevant context hurts
Latency-sensitive (no time for extra retrieval step) Quality-sensitive (worth the retrieval latency)

7.6 The Context Budget Allocation

Think of your context window as a budget with line items. For a 200K-token window:


context_budget:
  system_prompt_and_skills: 5K-15K       # Stable, loaded once
  current_goal_and_contract: 1K-2K       # Always present
  current_state_summary: 2K-5K           # Updated each iteration
  tool_results_current_step: 10K-50K     # Variable, pruned aggressively
  working_history: 10K-30K               # Last 2-3 iterations
  reserved_for_model_output: 10K-30K     # Generation budget
  
  # What does NOT get budget:
  full_conversation_history: 0           # Compacted or pruned
  large_file_contents: 0                 # Read on demand
  irrelevant_tool_results: 0            # Pruned immediately

The exact allocation depends on your task. Code-heavy tasks need more room for file contents. Research tasks need more room for source material. The principle is constant: allocate deliberately, not by default accumulation.

7.7 Agentic Search vs. Pre-Loaded RAG

Traditional RAG (Retrieval Augmented Generation) retrieves documents before the model sees the query. The retrieval system decides what's relevant based on embedding similarity. This works well for simple Q&A but fails for complex agentic tasks because:

  1. The query isn't known in advance. In a loop, what the model needs at step 7 depends on what it discovered at step 6. No pre-retrieval system can predict this.
  1. Relevance is task-dependent. The same document may be crucial for one step and irrelevant for the next. Pre-loading it wastes budget on irrelevant turns.
  1. The model is a better judge of relevance. Given a goal and current state, the model can reason about what information would help. A vector-similarity search cannot.

Agentic search inverts the RAG pattern: the model decides what to search for, retrieves it on demand, and discards it when it's no longer needed.


# Traditional RAG: retrieve before reasoning
docs = retriever.search(user_query, top_k=20)  # 20K tokens pre-loaded
response = model.generate(system_prompt + docs + user_query)

# Agentic search: reason about what to retrieve
for step in loop:
    needed = model.decide_what_to_search(current_state, goal)
    docs = retriever.search(needed, top_k=3)    # 3K tokens, precisely targeted
    action = model.reason_and_act(current_state + docs + goal)
    current_state = update_state(action)

The agentic approach uses fewer tokens per step (3K vs. 20K) with higher relevance (model-directed vs. similarity-guessed). Over a 10-iteration loop, this saves 170K tokens of noise.

7.8 The Tool-Result Problem

Tools produce output. Sometimes a lot of output. A file read returns the entire file. A search returns multiple results. A command execution returns stdout and stderr. This output enters the context and stays there unless actively managed.

The tool-result problem: in a 10-iteration loop where each iteration calls 3 tools that each return 5K tokens, the accumulated tool results consume 150K tokens — three-quarters of a 200K window — most of which is irrelevant to the current iteration.

Solutions:

1. Immediate summarization: After each tool call, the harness (not the model) summarizes the result to its essential content. A 5K file read becomes a 500-token summary of the relevant portions.

2. Expiring results: Tool results from more than 2 iterations ago are automatically removed from context. If the model needs them again, it can re-call the tool.

3. Selective inclusion: The harness analyzes tool results and only includes portions relevant to the current step. A grep that returns 50 matches only shows the 5 most relevant in context.

4. External storage: Large tool results are saved to a file and only a reference is kept in context. The model can read the file if it needs the full content.


# Claude Code's approach: tool results cleared after use
class ContextManager:
    def after_tool_call(self, tool_name: str, result: str) -> str:
        if len(result) > 2000:
            summary = self.summarize(result, current_goal)
            self.store_full_result(tool_name, result)  # Accessible via read
            return summary
        return result
    
    def between_iterations(self, context: list) -> list:
        # Remove tool results older than 2 iterations
        return [msg for msg in context 
                if msg.age < 2 or msg.type != "tool_result"]

7.9 Context Engineering in Practice

A well-engineered loop manages context actively at four moments:

Before the loop starts:

  • Load skills (project knowledge, rules, constraints)
  • Set the goal and Loop Contract in the system prompt
  • Initialize the current-state document

Before each iteration:

  • Update the current-state summary with results from the last iteration
  • Prune stale tool results and old history
  • Verify the context budget isn't exceeded

After each tool call:

  • Summarize long results
  • Discard clearly irrelevant portions
  • Update the current-state if the tool revealed something important

When the context approaches capacity:

  • Trigger compaction (Chapter 8)
  • Remove the oldest history first
  • Preserve: goal, current state, last iteration's results, skills
  • Discard: old tool results, superseded plans, resolved errors

7.10 Measuring Context Quality

How do you know your context engineering is working? Three metrics:

Token efficiency: Verified outcomes per dollar spent on input tokens. If context engineering is working, this number increases over time because you're spending fewer tokens on noise.

Iteration count: Average iterations to convergence. If context engineering is working, the model needs fewer iterations because it's reasoning over better information at each step.

Context utilization: What percentage of context tokens are actively referenced in the model's reasoning? This is harder to measure but can be approximated by tracking which context elements appear in the model's output or tool-selection decisions.

The goal isn't to minimize context — it's to maximize the ratio of useful context to total context. Sometimes that means loading more (when additional context genuinely helps). Usually it means loading less (when the default accumulation creates noise).

Next: Chapter 8 covers what happens when context can't be managed within the window — compaction, editing, and handoff strategies for loops that outlive their own context.

Chapter 8: Context Engineering II — Compaction, Editing, and Handoff

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

8.1 When the Window Fills

Every loop eventually faces a choice: stop, or continue with degraded context. A loop that runs 20 iterations on a complex task will fill its context window — regardless of how aggressively you manage it. The question isn't whether you'll hit the limit. It's what you do when you get there.

Three strategies exist, and production loops use all three:

  1. Compaction: Lossy compression of the existing context
  2. Context editing: Surgical removal of specific elements
  3. Handoff: Transferring state to a fresh context (new session or new agent)

Each has tradeoffs. Each is appropriate in different situations. Understanding when to use which is a core loop engineering skill.

8.2 Compaction as Lossy Checkpoint

Compaction takes a long conversation — hundreds of messages, tool results, reasoning chains — and compresses it into a summary that preserves the essential state while discarding the details. It's lossy by design: you lose the specifics of how you got here, but retain where "here" is.

What compaction preserves:

  • Current goal and progress toward it
  • Decisions made and their rationale
  • Key facts discovered during the loop
  • The current plan and what's been completed
  • Active errors or blockers

What compaction discards:

  • The full text of tool outputs (replaced by summaries)
  • Reasoning chains that led to abandoned approaches
  • Superseded plans and old state
  • The specific sequence of actions (replaced by outcome descriptions)

# Compaction summary (what a compacted context looks like)

## Goal
Fix the authentication race condition in auth_middleware.py

## Progress
- Identified root cause: token validation and session creation aren't atomic
- Attempted fix 1: mutex lock — worked but caused 3x latency regression
- Attempted fix 2: optimistic locking with retry — in progress

## Current state
- Branch: fix/auth-race-condition
- Modified files: auth_middleware.py (lines 42-67), tests/test_auth.py
- Test status: 44/47 passing. Failures: test_concurrent_login, test_session_race, test_token_refresh

## Key decisions
- Using optimistic locking over pessimistic (performance requirement)
- Added retry limit of 3 to prevent infinite retry loops
- Keeping backward compatibility with existing session format

## Next step
Complete the retry logic in auth_middleware.py:58, then run the failing tests

This summary is ~200 tokens. The full history it replaces might be 100K tokens. The compression ratio is 500:1, and the essential state is preserved.

The compaction risk

Compaction is lossy. You lose:

  • Nuance: The subtle reasoning about why approach A was abandoned might matter if approach B fails the same way
  • Evidence: The specific tool output that revealed a fact is gone; only the fact remains
  • Recovery paths: If the summary is wrong or incomplete, there's no way back to the original context

The mitigation: write compaction summaries before discarding the original. Store the full conversation externally (a log file, a database) so it can be re-read if the compacted context proves insufficient. Compaction should be "lossy in memory, lossless on disk."

8.3 Structured Handoff Notes

When a loop reaches context capacity and needs to continue in a fresh session, it performs a handoff: transferring accumulated state from the old context to the new one. A handoff note is a structured document that enables a fresh agent to continue work without re-discovering everything the previous agent learned.

A good handoff note contains:


# Handoff: [Task Name]

## Context
What the task is and why it matters. One paragraph.

## Completed work
What has been done so far. Specific files modified, tests passing/failing, decisions made.

## Current state
The exact situation the new agent inherits. File paths, line numbers, test results.

## Active plan
What the previous agent intended to do next. The receiving agent may revise.

## Known issues
Problems discovered but not yet resolved. Gotchas, constraints, edge cases.

## Critical constraints
Things the new agent must NOT do. Approaches already tried and failed.

The handoff quality problem

A handoff note is only as good as the agent that writes it. If the previous agent misunderstands its own state — or if its context is so degraded that it can't accurately summarize — the handoff note will be wrong, and the new agent will start from a misleading position.

Mitigations:

  1. Write handoff notes early: Don't wait until context is full. Write the handoff note while the agent's context is still fresh, and update it incrementally.
  2. Ground in artifacts: Reference specific files, line numbers, and test results rather than relying on the agent's memory of what it did.
  3. Verify post-handoff: The receiving agent's first action should be to verify the handoff note against reality (read the files, run the tests, check the state).

8.4 Designing Loops That Outlive Their Context

A well-designed loop doesn't panic when context fills. It's designed from the start to handle context overflow gracefully:

Pattern 1: The milestone loop

Break the work into milestones. At each milestone, write a checkpoint (compaction + handoff note) and optionally start a fresh context. Each milestone is achievable within a single context fill.


Milestone 1: Reproduce the bug [fits in one context]
  → Checkpoint: "Bug reproduced. Root cause is X. See test_file.py:23"

Milestone 2: Implement the fix [fresh context, starts from checkpoint]
  → Checkpoint: "Fix implemented in file.py:40-55. 3 tests still failing."

Milestone 3: Fix regressions [fresh context, starts from checkpoint]
  → Checkpoint: "All tests pass. Ready for review."

Pattern 2: The rolling window

Keep only the last N iterations in full context. Everything older is compacted into a running summary. The summary grows slowly; the detail window stays fixed.


Context structure:
  [System prompt + skills]           ~10K tokens (fixed)
  [Running summary of all history]   ~2K tokens (grows slowly)
  [Last 3 iterations in full]        ~30K tokens (fixed window)
  [Current iteration]                ~10K tokens (variable)

Pattern 3: The external state pattern

The loop's true state lives outside the context — in files, databases, or structured documents. The context contains only what's needed for the current step, loaded from external state on demand.


# External state pattern
state_file = Path("loop_state.json")

def load_context_for_step(step: int) -> str:
    state = json.loads(state_file.read_text())
    return f"""
    Goal: {state['goal']}
    Current step: {step}
    Completed steps: {state['completed']}
    Current state: {state['current_state']}
    Relevant context: {state.get(f'step_{step}_context', '')}
    """

def save_state_after_step(step: int, result: dict):
    state = json.loads(state_file.read_text())
    state['completed'].append(step)
    state['current_state'] = result['new_state']
    state_file.write_text(json.dumps(state, indent=2))

This pattern is the most robust for long-running loops. The context window is a working memory — a scratch pad for the current step — while the persistent state lives in durable storage.

8.5 Automatic Tool-Result Clearing

The single biggest source of context bloat in production loops is accumulated tool results. A file read returns 3K tokens. A search returns 5K tokens. A command output returns 2K tokens. After ten iterations with three tool calls each, you have 100K tokens of tool results — most of which are irrelevant to the current iteration.

The solution is automatic clearing: tool results are removed or summarized after they've been processed.


class ToolResultManager:
    def __init__(self, max_age_iterations: int = 2, max_size_tokens: int = 2000):
        self.max_age = max_age_iterations
        self.max_size = max_size_tokens
    
    def process_result(self, result: str, current_iteration: int) -> str:
        if len(result) > self.max_size:
            return self.summarize(result)
        return result
    
    def prune_old_results(self, messages: list, current_iteration: int) -> list:
        return [
            msg for msg in messages
            if msg.type != "tool_result" 
            or (current_iteration - msg.iteration) <= self.max_age
        ]

Claude Code implements this aggressively: tool results are available for the current iteration and the next, then they're cleared. If the model needs the information again, it re-reads the file or re-runs the tool. The re-read costs a few hundred tokens; keeping stale results costs thousands of tokens of noise in every subsequent iteration.

8.6 When to Compact vs. When to Handoff

Situation Strategy Rationale
Context 70% full, loop converging Compact in place Don't disrupt a working loop
Context 90% full, loop converging Compact aggressively Buy enough room to finish
Context full, loop stuck Handoff to fresh context The current context may be part of the problem
Context full, task has natural milestones Handoff at milestone Clean break, clear state
Multiple agents need to coordinate External state + minimal context Each agent loads only what it needs

The wrong choice: letting context fill to 100% without action. At capacity, the system must either truncate (losing information unpredictably) or fail (stopping the loop). Neither is acceptable. Context management should be proactive, not reactive.

8.7 The Compaction-Quality Tradeoff

Compaction introduces a fundamental tradeoff: you gain context space at the cost of information fidelity. How much to compact — and when — depends on the task:

High-fidelity tasks (code, data): Compact reasoning and history, but preserve exact file paths, line numbers, test results, and error messages verbatim. A summary that says "some tests failed" is useless; one that says "test_auth.py:test_expired_token fails with AssertionError at line 23" is actionable.

Low-fidelity tasks (research, writing): Can compact more aggressively. A summary of sources and key findings is often sufficient; the exact text of each source isn't needed in context (it can be re-retrieved if needed).

State-heavy tasks (multi-step transformations): The current state is critical; the history of how you got there is less important. Compact the journey, preserve the destination.

8.8 Designing for Compaction from the Start

The best compaction happens when the loop is designed for it from the beginning. Practices that make compaction effective:

  1. Maintain a running state document: Update it each iteration with the current situation. This becomes the compaction target — instead of summarizing a huge history, you already have a concise state description.
  1. Separate ephemeral from durable information: Tool results, reasoning chains, and intermediate states are ephemeral. Decisions, facts, and plan updates are durable. Store durable information in a structured format that survives compaction.
  1. Write checkpoints at natural boundaries: When a sub-task completes, when a decision is made, when a significant fact is discovered. Each checkpoint is a potential compaction point.
  1. Use external storage for large artifacts: File contents, command outputs, and search results should be stored externally and referenced by path, not embedded in context. This makes compaction trivial — you just drop the reference and re-read if needed.

Next: Chapter 9 dives deep into tool design — the engineering of affordances that make loops effective, including Tool Search, programmatic tool calling, and the economics of tool schemas.

Chapter 9: Tool Design at Scale

Level: A (Anchor) · ~6,000 words · Levels served: L2, L3, L4, L5

9.1 Tools Are the Loop's Hands

A model without tools can only think. A model with tools can act. The quality of those tools — their design, ergonomics, error messages, and composition — determines whether the loop can effectively interact with the world or flails blindly at it.

Tool design is the most underinvested area of loop engineering. Most practitioners grab whatever tools their framework provides and move on. But the difference between well-designed tools and poorly-designed tools is the difference between a loop that converges in 3 iterations and one that thrashes for 15.

9.2 The Three Audiences for a Tool

Every tool has three audiences, and it must serve all three:

Audience 1: The Model (Primary)

The model sees the tool's name, description, parameter schema, and (eventually) its error messages. This is the model's only way to understand what the tool does, when to use it, and what went wrong.

A tool that's opaque to the model is useless regardless of how powerful it is. If the model can't figure out when to call it or what arguments to pass, the tool might as well not exist.

Audience 2: The Harness Developer

The developer implements the tool, maintains it, and debugs failures. The tool should be easy to implement correctly, easy to test, and easy to extend.

Audience 3: The Human Reviewer

A human will eventually audit the loop's behavior — reviewing what tools were called, with what arguments, and what they returned. The tool's inputs and outputs should be interpretable by a human reading a trace log.

9.3 The Tool Description Is the API

For the model, the tool description IS the documentation. There's no other source of truth. The model won't read your README. It won't look at your source code. It makes every decision about whether and how to use the tool based solely on what appears in the tool's schema.

Bad tool description:


{
  "name": "exec",
  "description": "Execute a command",
  "parameters": {
    "cmd": {"type": "string"}
  }
}

Problems: What kind of command? What environment? What are the constraints? When should this be used vs. alternatives? What's the expected output format?

Good tool description:


{
  "name": "run_tests",
  "description": "Execute the project's test suite and return results. Use after making code changes to verify they don't break existing functionality. Returns structured output with pass/fail counts and failure details. For running a single test file, pass the file path. For the full suite, omit the path parameter.",
  "parameters": {
    "test_path": {
      "type": "string",
      "description": "Path to specific test file or directory. Omit to run full suite."
    },
    "verbose": {
      "type": "boolean",
      "description": "Include full stdout/stderr for failed tests. Default false."
    }
  }
}

The good description tells the model:

  • When to use it (after making code changes)
  • Why to use it (verify changes don't break things)
  • What it returns (structured pass/fail with failure details)
  • How to use it for different needs (single file vs. full suite)

9.4 Errors as Teaching Signal

When a tool call fails, the error message is the model's only feedback about what went wrong. A good error message teaches the model how to succeed on the next attempt. A bad error message forces the model to guess.

Bad error:


Error: Command failed with exit code 1

The model learns nothing. It might retry with the same arguments. It might try a completely different approach. It's guessing.

Good error:


Error: File 'src/auth.py' not found. 
Available files matching 'auth': src/authentication.py, src/middleware/auth_handler.py, tests/test_auth.py.
Did you mean one of these?

The model learns: the file doesn't exist, here are the actual options, pick one. The next tool call will almost certainly succeed.

The error design principle:

Every error message should answer three questions:

  1. What failed? — The specific operation that didn't work
  2. Why? — The reason for the failure
  3. What to try instead? — A concrete suggestion for the next attempt

class ToolError:
    def __init__(self, what: str, why: str, suggestion: str):
        self.message = f"Error: {what}. Reason: {why}. Try: {suggestion}"

# Examples:
ToolError(
    what="Cannot write to 'config.json'",
    why="File is read-only (permissions: 444)",
    suggestion="Use 'chmod 644 config.json' first, or write to a different location"
)

ToolError(
    what="Test execution timed out after 30s",
    why="Test 'test_large_dataset' appears to have an infinite loop",
    suggestion="Check the loop condition in test_large_dataset at line 45"
)

9.5 The Tool Search Tool Pattern

A production system might have 50+ tools available. Loading all their schemas into context costs ~55K tokens upfront — a quarter of a 200K window spent before the model does any work. Most of those tools won't be used in any given loop iteration.

The Tool Search Tool pattern solves this: instead of loading all tool schemas upfront, provide a single meta-tool that lets the model search for and load tool schemas on demand.


{
  "name": "tool_search",
  "description": "Search for available tools by capability. Returns tool names and descriptions matching your query. Use this to discover what tools are available before calling them. After finding the right tool, its full schema will be loaded automatically.",
  "parameters": {
    "query": {
      "type": "string",
      "description": "Natural language description of what you want to do. E.g., 'read a file', 'run tests', 'search code for a pattern'"
    }
  }
}

The economics:

  • Without Tool Search: 58 tools × ~950 tokens/schema = ~55K tokens loaded upfront in every message
  • With Tool Search: 1 tool schema (200 tokens) + loaded schemas for tools actually used (~3-5 tools × 950 = ~4.5K tokens)
  • Savings: ~50K tokens per message × many messages per loop = massive cost reduction

This is the exact pattern Claude Code uses. With 52+ registered tools, loading all schemas would consume 25% of context. Instead, tool schemas are loaded on demand — the model asks for what it needs, and only relevant schemas enter context.

Implementing Tool Search effectively:


class ToolRegistry:
    def __init__(self):
        self.tools: dict[str, ToolDefinition] = {}
        self.embeddings: dict[str, list[float]] = {}  # For semantic search
    
    def search(self, query: str, top_k: int = 5) -> list[ToolSummary]:
        """Return brief descriptions of matching tools without full schemas."""
        matches = self.semantic_search(query, top_k)
        return [ToolSummary(name=t.name, description=t.brief) for t in matches]
    
    def load_schema(self, tool_name: str) -> ToolDefinition:
        """Load full schema for a specific tool into the active tool set."""
        return self.tools[tool_name]

9.6 Programmatic Tool Calling

In a standard agent loop, the model decides which tool to call and the harness executes it. But some operations are better orchestrated in code — the harness calls tools programmatically, keeping intermediate results out of the model's context.

When to use programmatic tool calling:

  1. Data pipeline steps: Fetch from API → transform → validate → store. The model doesn't need to see every intermediate result.
  2. Batch operations: Apply the same operation to 100 items. The model decides the operation once; code executes it 100 times.
  3. Multi-tool compositions: Read file → parse → extract section → return to model. The model sees only the final extracted section, not the full file.

# Programmatic tool calling — orchestrate in code, show model only final result
async def get_relevant_code_context(file_path: str, function_name: str) -> str:
    """Called by harness, not by model. Composes multiple tools programmatically."""
    
    # Step 1: Read the file (tool call, not shown to model)
    full_file = await tools.read_file(file_path)
    
    # Step 2: Parse to AST and find the function (code, not a tool)
    tree = ast.parse(full_file)
    func_node = find_function(tree, function_name)
    
    # Step 3: Extract the function and its dependencies (code)
    func_code = ast.get_source_segment(full_file, func_node)
    deps = find_dependencies(tree, func_node)
    
    # Step 4: Return compact, relevant context to the model
    return f"Function {function_name}:\n{func_code}\n\nDependencies:\n{deps}"

The model sees a clean, focused context instead of a raw file dump. The harness did the work of locating and extracting relevant information. The model's context budget is spent on reasoning, not parsing.

The context savings are dramatic:

Approach Tokens in context Quality
Model reads full file (3000 lines) ~12K Low (buried in noise)
Model reads file + grep for function ~8K Medium
Programmatic extraction ~1.5K High (pure signal)

9.7 Tool Use Examples

Models learn tool-use patterns from examples even more than from descriptions. A tool with three examples of correct usage will be used correctly more often than a tool with a paragraph of description but no examples.


{
  "name": "search_code",
  "description": "Search for patterns in the codebase using ripgrep syntax.",
  "examples": [
    {
      "scenario": "Find all uses of a function",
      "call": {"pattern": "authenticate_user\\(", "file_type": "py"},
      "result_summary": "Returns file:line matches"
    },
    {
      "scenario": "Find a specific error message",
      "call": {"pattern": "\"Invalid token\"", "include_context": 3},
      "result_summary": "Returns matches with 3 lines of surrounding context"
    },
    {
      "scenario": "Find TODO comments in test files",
      "call": {"pattern": "TODO|FIXME", "path": "tests/"},
      "result_summary": "Returns all TODO/FIXME comments in test directory"
    }
  ]
}

The examples show the model:

  • What scenarios the tool is appropriate for
  • How to format arguments for each scenario
  • What kind of result to expect

This is more effective than detailed documentation because models learn from patterns, not from specifications. The examples ARE the specification, in a format the model naturally processes.

9.8 Tool Ergonomics for Loops

Tools designed for single-use (a human triggers one tool call) have different requirements than tools designed for loops (an agent calls them repeatedly across iterations). Loop-optimized tools need:

9.8.1 Idempotency

A tool called twice with the same arguments should produce the same result without side effects. In a loop, the model may retry a tool call after an error, or re-execute a plan from a checkpoint. Idempotent tools are safe to retry.

9.8.2 Incremental output

A tool that returns its full output every call forces the model to diff against the previous call mentally. A tool that returns only what changed since the last call is loop-friendly.


# Loop-unfriendly: returns entire test suite output every time
def run_tests() -> str:
    return subprocess.run(["pytest"], capture_output=True).stdout

# Loop-friendly: returns only what changed
def run_tests(previous_results: dict = None) -> str:
    results = parse_test_results(subprocess.run(["pytest"], capture_output=True))
    if previous_results:
        return format_delta(previous_results, results)
    return format_results(results)

9.8.3 Composability

Tools should be small and composable rather than large and monolithic. A tool that "reads a file, finds a function, and edits it" is hard to use in different contexts. Three tools — "read file," "find function," "edit range" — compose to handle any scenario.

9.8.4 Bounded output

A tool that can return unbounded output (e.g., grep with no result limit) is dangerous in a loop context. Always cap output size, paginate results, or summarize when output exceeds a threshold.

9.9 The Cost of Tool Schemas

Every tool schema in the active set consumes context tokens on every model call. For a loop running 10 iterations, a 1K-token tool schema that's never used costs 10K tokens across the loop — roughly $0.03 at typical pricing. Multiply by 30 unused tools, and you're paying $0.90 per loop in wasted schema overhead.

This is why Tool Search matters at scale. It's also why tool sets should be dynamic — load tools relevant to the current step, unload tools that won't be needed.


class DynamicToolSet:
    def __init__(self):
        self.always_available = ["read_file", "write_file", "search_code"]
        self.loaded_on_demand = {}
    
    def for_step(self, step_type: str) -> list[str]:
        """Return the tool set appropriate for this step type."""
        if step_type == "testing":
            return self.always_available + ["run_tests", "run_single_test"]
        elif step_type == "research":
            return self.always_available + ["web_search", "web_fetch"]
        elif step_type == "deployment":
            return self.always_available + ["deploy", "rollback", "health_check"]
        return self.always_available

9.10 Tool Design Checklist

Before adding a tool to a loop, verify:

  • [ ] Name is action-verb: run_tests not tests, read_file not file_content
  • [ ] Description states when to use: Not just what it does, but when it's the right choice
  • [ ] Parameters have descriptions: Every parameter explains its purpose and constraints
  • [ ] Errors are actionable: Every error message suggests what to try next
  • [ ] Output is bounded: Maximum output size is enforced
  • [ ] Idempotent by default: Safe to call twice with same arguments
  • [ ] Examples included: At least 2-3 usage examples in the schema
  • [ ] Cost is proportional: The tool's output size is proportional to its information value

Tools that fail this checklist will work — but they'll work poorly. The model will misuse them, misinterpret their output, and waste iterations on avoidable errors. In a loop, these small inefficiencies compound into significant cost and reliability problems.

Next: Chapter 10 covers MCP — the protocol that wires tools into a standard interface, turning "here's a suggestion" into "I did it and confirmed it worked."

Chapter 10: MCP — Wiring the Loop to the World

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L2, L3, L4

10.1 The Connector Problem

A loop that only operates on local files lives in a sandbox. Production work happens across systems: GitHub for code, Linear for tickets, Slack for communication, databases for data, CI/CD for deployment, monitoring for observability. Without connectors to these systems, the loop produces suggestions. With connectors, it produces outcomes.

The difference:

  • Without connectors: "I recommend creating a PR with these changes." (Human must act.)
  • With connectors: "I created PR #342, CI is green, I've tagged the reviewer, and I posted the summary to Slack." (Done.)

Model Context Protocol (MCP) is the standard interface for building these connectors. Announced by Anthropic in late 2024 and adopted broadly through 2025-2026, MCP provides a uniform way for AI systems to discover, connect to, and interact with external tools and data sources.

10.2 MCP Architecture

MCP follows a client-server architecture:


┌─────────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────┐
│   AI Application    │         │    MCP Server        │
│   (MCP Client)      │◀───────▶│    (Connector)       │
│                     │  JSON   │                      │
│  - Claude Code      │  RPC    │  - GitHub server     │
│  - Custom loop      │  over   │  - Database server   │
│  - Any harness      │  stdio/ │  - Slack server      │
│                     │  HTTP   │  - Custom server     │
└─────────────────────┘         └─────────────────────┘

The MCP server exposes:

  • Tools: Actions the model can take (create PR, send message, query database)
  • Resources: Data the model can read (file contents, database schemas, API docs)
  • Prompts: Pre-built prompt templates for common operations

The MCP client (your loop harness) connects to one or more servers, discovers their capabilities, and presents them to the model as available tools.

10.3 Why MCP Matters for Loops

MCP matters for three reasons specific to loop engineering:

Reason 1: Standardized discovery

Before MCP, every integration was custom: bespoke code to call GitHub's API, different code to call Slack's API, different code for each database. With MCP, the loop discovers available tools dynamically. Add a new MCP server, and the loop can immediately use its tools without code changes.


# Without MCP: hardcoded integrations
def create_pr(title, body, branch):
    response = requests.post(
        f"https://api.github.com/repos/{repo}/pulls",
        headers={"Authorization": f"token {token}"},
        json={"title": title, "body": body, "head": branch, "base": "main"}
    )
    return response.json()

# With MCP: tools discovered from server
# The loop just calls whatever tools are available
tools = mcp_client.list_tools()  # Discovers create_pr, list_prs, etc.
result = mcp_client.call_tool("create_pull_request", {
    "title": title, "body": body, "branch": branch
})

Reason 2: Code execution

MCP servers can provide sandboxed code execution — a critical capability for loops that need to test their own output. The loop generates code, the MCP server executes it in isolation, and the result comes back as a tool response.


{
  "tool": "execute_code",
  "arguments": {
    "language": "python",
    "code": "import pytest; result = pytest.main(['tests/', '-x'])",
    "timeout_seconds": 30,
    "sandbox": true
  }
}

Reason 3: Authentication delegation

MCP servers handle authentication to external services. The loop doesn't need credentials in its context — it just calls tools, and the server handles OAuth flows, API keys, and token refresh behind the scenes. This is a security win: credentials never enter the model's context where they could be leaked via prompt injection.

10.4 MCP Server Anatomy

An MCP server is a process that implements the MCP protocol. It can be written in any language that supports JSON-RPC communication. Here's the structure:


import { Server } from "@modelcontextprotocol/sdk/server";

const server = new Server({
  name: "github-connector",
  version: "1.0.0",
});

// Register tools
server.setRequestHandler("tools/list", async () => ({
  tools: [
    {
      name: "create_pull_request",
      description: "Create a new pull request. Use after pushing changes to a feature branch.",
      inputSchema: {
        type: "object",
        properties: {
          title: { type: "string", description: "PR title (max 72 chars)" },
          body: { type: "string", description: "PR description in markdown" },
          branch: { type: "string", description: "Source branch name" },
          base: { type: "string", default: "main", description: "Target branch" },
        },
        required: ["title", "body", "branch"],
      },
    },
    // ... more tools
  ],
}));

// Handle tool calls
server.setRequestHandler("tools/call", async (request) => {
  if (request.params.name === "create_pull_request") {
    const { title, body, branch, base } = request.params.arguments;
    const pr = await github.createPR({ title, body, head: branch, base });
    return { content: [{ type: "text", text: `PR #${pr.number} created: ${pr.url}` }] };
  }
});

10.5 Building Custom MCP Servers

When production needs exceed available community servers, you build custom ones. The pattern is straightforward:

  1. Identify the integration: What system does the loop need to interact with?
  2. Define the tools: What actions should be available? (Start minimal — 3-5 tools.)
  3. Design for the model: Write descriptions that tell the model when and how to use each tool.
  4. Handle errors gracefully: Return actionable error messages, not stack traces.
  5. Add authentication: Handle credentials server-side, never expose them in tool responses.

Common custom servers:

Server Tools Loop use case
Internal wiki search, read_page, update_page Research loops that need company knowledge
Deployment deploy, rollback, health_check Operations loops that ship to staging/prod
Monitoring query_metrics, get_alerts, acknowledge Incident response loops
Database query, describe_table, explain_plan Data analysis loops
CI/CD trigger_build, get_status, read_logs Build-fix loops

10.6 MCP in Fleet Architecture

In a fleet loop, different agents may need different MCP servers:


fleet_mcp_config:
  orchestrator:
    servers: [github, linear, slack]  # Coordination tools
  
  code_agent:
    servers: [filesystem, code_execution, tests]  # Implementation tools
  
  review_agent:
    servers: [filesystem, github]  # Read code, post comments
  
  deploy_agent:
    servers: [github, deployment, monitoring]  # Ship and watch

Each agent sees only the tools relevant to its role. This is the principle of least privilege applied to tool access — the code agent can't accidentally send Slack messages, and the review agent can't accidentally deploy.

10.7 Security Considerations

MCP servers are a supply chain risk. A malicious or compromised MCP server can:

  • Return misleading tool results (poisoning the loop's context)
  • Exfiltrate data from tool arguments (the model passes sensitive info to tools)
  • Execute unauthorized actions (if the server has broader permissions than intended)
  • Inject prompts via tool results (response content that manipulates the model)

Mitigations:

1. Vet servers carefully. Use official or well-maintained community servers. For custom servers, review the code.

2. Scope permissions tightly. The GitHub server should have read-only access unless the loop explicitly needs to create PRs. The database server should only have SELECT permissions unless mutations are required.

3. Audit tool calls. Log every tool call and its result. Flag unexpected patterns (tools called at unusual times, unusual argument patterns, sensitive data in arguments).

4. Validate tool results. Don't trust tool results blindly. If a tool returns code, validate it before execution. If it returns URLs, verify they point where expected.

10.8 MCP vs. Direct API Integration

When should you use MCP vs. writing direct API calls in your harness?

Factor MCP Direct Integration
Standardization Uniform interface for all services Custom code per service
Discovery Dynamic tool discovery Static, hardcoded
Reusability Same server works across loops Each loop reimplements
Latency Additional RPC hop Direct API call
Debugging Standardized tracing Custom logging
Complexity Server setup + protocol overhead Simple HTTP calls

Use MCP when: You have multiple loops that need the same integrations, you want dynamic tool discovery, or you're building a platform that others will extend.

Use direct integration when: You have a single loop with a single integration, latency is critical, or the integration is too simple to warrant the MCP overhead.

For most production loops, the MCP overhead is negligible and the standardization benefits are significant. Default to MCP unless you have a specific reason not to.

10.9 The Connector Transforms the Loop

Adding connectors fundamentally changes what a loop can do:

Without connectors (L2-L3):

  • Read local files, write local files
  • Run local commands
  • Produce artifacts for human delivery

With connectors (L3-L4):

  • Create PRs, merge code, deploy
  • Update tickets, notify teams
  • Query production systems
  • Monitor health post-deployment
  • Close the loop end-to-end without human delivery

The connector is what promotes a loop from "helpful assistant that produces suggestions" to "autonomous system that ships outcomes." It's the difference between generating a fix and deploying a fix.

Chapter 11: Skills — Reusable Project Knowledge

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L2, L3, L4, L5

11.1 The Cold-Start Tax

Every time a loop starts without project context, it pays a cold-start tax: tokens spent discovering what the project is, how it's structured, what the rules are, and how to verify work. This tax is paid on every single run.

For a typical code loop:

  • ~2K tokens to understand the tech stack
  • ~3K tokens to find relevant files
  • ~1K tokens to learn the testing approach
  • ~2K tokens to understand coding conventions
  • Total: ~8K tokens × cost per token × runs per day

At 50 runs per day, that's 400K tokens of repeated discovery — roughly $1.20/day in pure waste. Over a month, $36. Over a year, $432. For one loop. Multiply by the number of loops in your fleet.

Skills eliminate this tax. A skill file is project knowledge encoded once, loaded on demand, and reused across every run.

11.2 What a Skill Is

A skill is a document that teaches an agent everything it needs to know about a specific project, domain, or task — without requiring the agent to discover that knowledge from scratch.

A skill is NOT:

  • A prompt template (though it may contain prompt fragments)
  • A tool definition (though it may reference tools)
  • A full conversation history
  • A codebase summary

A skill IS:

  • Curated project knowledge, organized for agent consumption
  • Stable across runs (updated when the project changes, not when the loop runs)
  • Loaded into context on demand (not always present)
  • Designed for the model as the primary reader

11.3 SKILL.md Anatomy

The standard skill file format:


# [Project Name] — [Skill Purpose]

## Vision
One paragraph: what this project does and why it matters.

## Architecture
How the system is structured. Key components and their relationships.
Include a Mermaid diagram for complex architectures.

## Key Conventions
- Language/framework specifics
- Naming conventions
- File organization patterns
- Import ordering rules

## Rules (Must Follow)
Constraints the agent must never violate. These are hard rules, not suggestions.
- [ ] Never modify files in /migrations/ after they're applied
- [ ] All API endpoints require authentication
- [ ] Error responses use RFC 7807 format
- [ ] No direct database access from controllers

## Rules (Should Follow)
Strong preferences that may be overridden with justification.
- Prefer composition over inheritance
- Keep functions under 30 lines
- Use structured logging, not print statements

## Anti-Patterns (Never Do)
Things the agent must actively avoid. Often learned from past mistakes.
- Never use SELECT * in production queries
- Never store secrets in environment variables committed to git
- Never modify the ORM models without a migration

## Build & Verify
How to verify work is correct. This feeds directly into the loop's oracle.

Run tests

pytest tests/ -x --tb=short

Type check

pyright src/

Lint

ruff check src/ --fix

Build

docker build -t app .



## Common Tasks
Brief guides for tasks the loop handles frequently.

### Adding a new API endpoint
1. Define schema in schemas/
2. Implement handler in routes/
3. Add integration test in tests/integration/
4. Update OpenAPI spec

### Fixing a failing test
1. Read the test to understand intent
2. Read the error to understand the failure
3. Fix the source code, not the test (unless the test is wrong)
4. Run the specific test, then the full suite

11.4 Progressive Disclosure

Not every run needs all project knowledge. A bug fix loop doesn't need the deployment architecture. A documentation loop doesn't need the testing strategy. Loading everything into every context wastes tokens on irrelevant information.

Progressive disclosure loads skills in layers:

Layer 0 — Always loaded (~2K tokens):

  • Project name and purpose
  • Tech stack (language, framework, key deps)
  • Build and test commands

Layer 1 — Loaded for code tasks (~5K tokens):

  • Architecture overview
  • Coding conventions
  • File organization
  • Key rules

Layer 2 — Loaded on demand (~variable):

  • Domain-specific knowledge (auth patterns, data model, API design)
  • Common task guides
  • Anti-patterns and lessons learned

class SkillLoader:
    def load_for_task(self, task_type: str) -> str:
        context = self.load_layer_0()  # Always
        
        if task_type in ("code", "fix", "refactor", "test"):
            context += self.load_layer_1()
        
        if task_type == "auth":
            context += self.load_domain_skill("authentication")
        elif task_type == "api":
            context += self.load_domain_skill("api_design")
        
        return context

11.5 Skill Composition

Large projects have multiple skills that compose:


project_skill.md          — Overall project context (Layer 0-1)
├── auth_skill.md         — Authentication domain knowledge
├── api_skill.md          — API design patterns and conventions
├── testing_skill.md      — Testing strategy and patterns
├── deployment_skill.md   — Deployment process and constraints
└── data_skill.md         — Data model and migration patterns

The loop loads the base skill plus domain-specific skills based on the task. A fleet orchestrator might load only the base skill (to plan) while delegating domain-specific work to specialists who load the relevant domain skill.

11.6 Skill Versioning

Skills change as projects evolve. A skill file that says "we use Jest for testing" when the project migrated to Vitest last month will cause the loop to use the wrong test runner.

Version management approaches:

1. Git-tracked skills: Skills live in the repository alongside the code. When the code changes, the skill is updated in the same PR. This ensures skills and code stay synchronized.

2. Auto-generated skills: A script reads the project structure and generates baseline skills automatically. Human-curated additions are maintained separately and merged with the auto-generated base.


# Auto-generate skill base from project structure
skill-gen --project . --output .claude/skills/project_skill.md

# Output includes:
# - Detected tech stack (from package.json, requirements.txt, etc.)
# - File structure overview
# - Build commands (from Makefile, package.json scripts, etc.)
# - Test commands (detected from config files)

3. Skill-per-branch: For projects with long-lived branches that diverge significantly, maintain branch-specific skill overrides.

11.7 Skill Quality Metrics

How do you know if a skill is good? Two metrics:

1. First-attempt success rate: How often does the loop succeed without needing to re-discover information that should be in the skill? If the loop frequently reads files to learn things the skill should have told it, the skill is incomplete.

2. Token efficiency: Compare total tokens consumed with and without the skill. A good skill reduces total loop token consumption by 30-60% despite adding tokens to the initial context.

Common skill quality issues:

Symptom Cause Fix
Loop asks "what testing framework?" Missing from skill Add Build & Verify section
Loop uses wrong file paths Stale architecture section Update with current structure
Loop violates project conventions Missing from Rules section Add discovered conventions
Loop recreates existing patterns No Common Tasks section Add task guides for frequent work
Loop reads too many files in discovery Skills too vague Add specific file paths and patterns

11.8 Skills for Different Loop Types

Different loops benefit from different skill emphases:

Coding loop skills emphasize:

  • Architecture and file structure
  • Testing commands and patterns
  • Coding conventions and anti-patterns
  • Dependency management

Research loop skills emphasize:

  • Domain expertise boundaries
  • Trusted sources and how to access them
  • Quality criteria for findings
  • Output format requirements

Operations loop skills emphasize:

  • System topology and dependencies
  • Runbook procedures
  • Escalation paths
  • SLA thresholds

Content loop skills emphasize:

  • Voice and tone guidelines
  • Audience definition
  • Structure templates
  • Quality rubrics

11.9 The Skill Lifecycle

Skills aren't write-once artifacts. They evolve:

1. Bootstrap: Auto-generate from project structure. Covers basics: stack, commands, file layout.

2. Curate: Human adds domain knowledge, rules, anti-patterns, and task guides. This is where the real value lives — things the auto-generator can't know.

3. Iterate: As the loop runs and encounters issues, update the skill. Every time the loop fails because of missing context, that context belongs in the skill.

4. Prune: Remove stale information. Skills that grow without pruning become noise. If a section hasn't been relevant in months, consider removing it.

5. Split: When a skill exceeds ~10K tokens, split it into composable sub-skills with progressive disclosure.

11.10 Skills vs. CLAUDE.md

In the Claude Code ecosystem, CLAUDE.md serves a similar purpose to skills — project instructions loaded into context. The distinction:

  • CLAUDE.md: Global project instructions. Always loaded. Covers everything.
  • Skills: Composable, domain-specific knowledge modules. Loaded on demand.

For simple projects, CLAUDE.md is sufficient. For complex projects with multiple domains, skills provide finer-grained control over what context is loaded when.

The evolution: start with CLAUDE.md for overall project context. When CLAUDE.md exceeds 15K tokens or when different tasks need different knowledge, split into skills.
