Chapter 12: Memory and Between-Run Consolidation

Level: A (Anchor) · ~6,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

12.1 The Goldfish Problem

A model without memory wakes up every session as a stranger. It doesn't know what was tried yesterday, what failed last week, what the user corrected, or what patterns recur. Every run starts from scratch — rediscovering the project, re-making past mistakes, re-learning preferences the user already taught it.

This is the goldfish problem. The model's context window is a working memory that lasts exactly one session. When the session ends, everything learned is gone.

For single-use interactions, this doesn't matter. For loops that run daily — reviewing PRs, maintaining code quality, managing infrastructure — it's catastrophic. A loop that can't learn from its own history is doomed to repeat its mistakes indefinitely.

Memory is the system that solves this: capturing what was learned in each run and making it available to future runs.

12.2 The Four Types of Memory

Memory in agentic systems mirrors the taxonomy from cognitive science:

Working Memory

What's currently in the context window. Fast to access, limited in capacity, gone when the session ends. This is what the model is actively reasoning about.

  • Capacity: The context window (128K-1M tokens, depending on model)
  • Persistence: One session only
  • Cost to access: Zero (already loaded)
  • Use: Current-step reasoning, in-progress work

Episodic Memory

Records of specific past events — what happened, when, and what the outcome was. "Last Tuesday, the auth loop failed because it tried to modify a read-only config file." Episodic memories are timestamped, specific, and tied to particular runs.

  • Capacity: Unbounded (stored externally)
  • Persistence: Days to weeks
  • Cost to access: Retrieval overhead (search + load)
  • Use: Avoiding repeated mistakes, learning from past failures

Semantic Memory

Accumulated knowledge — facts, relationships, and patterns that have been abstracted from multiple episodes. "This project uses UTC internally and converts to local time only at the display layer." Semantic memories are timeless truths (until invalidated), not tied to specific events.

  • Capacity: Unbounded
  • Persistence: Months to permanent
  • Cost to access: Retrieval overhead
  • Use: Project understanding, domain knowledge, stable facts

Procedural Memory

Learned workflows and skills — how to do things. "When tests fail with a database error, check if the migration has been applied before investigating the code." Procedural memories are if-then rules or step sequences that have been validated through experience.

  • Capacity: Unbounded
  • Persistence: Permanent (until workflow changes)
  • Cost to access: Loaded with skills
  • Use: Task execution, automatic recovery from known failure modes

12.3 Memory Storage Mechanisms

Memory must be stored somewhere the model can access across sessions. The options, from simplest to most sophisticated:

File-Based Memory

The simplest and most robust approach. Memory lives in markdown or JSON files in a known location. The model reads and writes these files directly.


.claude/memory/
├── MEMORY.md           # Index of all memories
├── episodes/
│   ├── 2026-07-15.md   # What happened on this date
│   └── 2026-07-16.md
├── facts/
│   ├── project_arch.md # Semantic: architecture facts
│   └── conventions.md  # Semantic: coding conventions
├── procedures/
│   ├── fix_db_errors.md    # Procedural: how to handle DB failures
│   └── deploy_process.md   # Procedural: deployment steps
└── feedback/
    ├── user_prefs.md   # User corrections and preferences
    └── false_positives.md  # Things to stop flagging

Advantages: Simple, transparent, version-controllable, human-readable, no infrastructure needed.

Disadvantages: No semantic search (must read files sequentially), can grow large, no automatic relevance ranking.

Structured Database Memory

Memory stored in a database (SQLite, PostgreSQL) with metadata for search and retrieval.


CREATE TABLE memories (
    id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    type TEXT,          -- episodic, semantic, procedural
    content TEXT,
    created_at TIMESTAMP,
    last_accessed TIMESTAMP,
    access_count INTEGER,
    importance INTEGER, -- 1-5
    project_path TEXT,
    embedding BLOB      -- for semantic search
);

Advantages: Semantic search via embeddings, structured queries, automatic relevance ranking, metadata tracking.

Disadvantages: Infrastructure overhead, less transparent, harder for humans to browse.

Hybrid (File + Search)

The most practical approach for production: memories stored as files (human-readable, version-controlled) with a search index built on top (fast retrieval, relevance ranking).


class MemorySystem:
    def __init__(self, memory_dir: Path):
        self.dir = memory_dir
        self.index = SearchIndex(memory_dir)
    
    def save(self, memory: Memory) -> None:
        file_path = self.dir / memory.type / f"{memory.key}.md"
        file_path.write_text(memory.to_markdown())
        self.index.update(memory)
    
    def search(self, query: str, limit: int = 5) -> list[Memory]:
        return self.index.search(query, limit)
    
    def get_relevant(self, current_task: str) -> list[Memory]:
        """Return memories relevant to the current task."""
        return self.search(current_task, limit=10)

12.4 The Memory Lifecycle

Memory isn't just storage. It's a lifecycle: capture → store → consolidate → recall → forget.

Capture

Deciding what to remember. Not everything is worth storing. The capture criteria:

  • Novel: Information the loop didn't know before this run
  • Useful: Information likely to help in future runs
  • Non-derivable: Can't be easily rediscovered from the code or git history
  • Stable: Won't become stale within a few runs

What NOT to capture:

  • Current git state (derivable from git log)
  • File contents (read from the filesystem)
  • Debugging solutions (the fix is in the code)
  • Ephemeral task details (not useful next run)

Store

Writing the memory to persistent storage with appropriate metadata: type, timestamp, importance, project association, relationships to other memories.

Consolidate

The critical step that distinguishes a memory system from a log file. Consolidation is the process of:

  1. Deduplicating: Multiple episodes that teach the same lesson are merged into one semantic memory
  2. Resolving contradictions: When two memories conflict, the more recent and better-evidenced one wins
  3. Surfacing patterns: When the same issue recurs across multiple episodes, create a procedural memory for handling it
  4. Updating importance: Memories that are frequently accessed increase in importance; rarely-accessed memories decrease

Anthropic calls this process "dreaming" — a background consolidation that runs between sessions, curating accumulated memories into useful knowledge.


class MemoryConsolidator:
    def consolidate(self, memories: list[Memory]) -> list[Memory]:
        # Find duplicate episodes
        duplicates = self.find_duplicates(memories)
        for group in duplicates:
            merged = self.merge_into_semantic(group)
            self.replace(group, merged)
        
        # Detect recurring patterns
        patterns = self.detect_patterns(memories)
        for pattern in patterns:
            if pattern.frequency >= 3:
                proc = self.create_procedural(pattern)
                self.store(proc)
        
        # Resolve contradictions
        contradictions = self.find_contradictions(memories)
        for pair in contradictions:
            winner = self.resolve(pair)  # More recent, better evidenced
            self.archive(pair.loser)
        
        # Decay unused memories
        stale = [m for m in memories if m.days_since_access > 90]
        for memory in stale:
            memory.importance -= 1
            if memory.importance <= 0:
                self.archive(memory)

Recall

Loading relevant memories into context at the start of a run. Not all memories are relevant to every task — recall must be selective:


def prepare_context_with_memory(task: str, memory_system: MemorySystem) -> str:
    # Always load: user preferences, critical project facts
    always = memory_system.get_by_type("preference") + \
             memory_system.get_by_importance(min_importance=5)
    
    # Task-relevant: search for memories related to current work
    relevant = memory_system.search(task, limit=10)
    
    # Recent episodes: what happened in the last few runs
    recent = memory_system.get_recent_episodes(days=7, limit=5)
    
    # Compose into context
    return format_memories(always + relevant + recent)

Forget

Not all memories should persist forever. Forgetting is a feature:

  • Stale facts: "The API uses version 2" when it's been upgraded to version 3
  • Superseded procedures: "Deploy with Jenkins" when the team moved to GitHub Actions
  • Resolved episodes: "Bug X exists" when bug X was fixed
  • Low-value memories: Memories that are never recalled, never relevant

Forgetting prevents memory bloat — a growing pile of irrelevant memories that consume context tokens without providing value. Active pruning keeps the memory system lean and relevant.

12.5 Cross-Run Learning Patterns

Pattern 1: Error Memory

Every time a loop fails, record the error and the resolution. Next time the same error pattern appears, apply the known resolution directly.


# Error Memory: Database connection timeout

## Pattern
Error message: "psycopg2.OperationalError: connection timed out"
Context: occurs after deploy, when new instances start

## Resolution
1. Check if the connection pool is exhausted (SELECT count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity)
2. If yes, restart the connection pool (kill idle connections)
3. If no, check if the migration lock is held (pg_locks table)

## History
- 2026-07-10: Occurred after deploy. Root cause: migration lock.
- 2026-07-15: Occurred after deploy. Root cause: pool exhaustion from health checks.

Pattern 2: User Preference Learning

When a user corrects the loop ("don't do X", "always do Y"), record the preference. Apply it in all future runs without the user needing to repeat themselves.


# Preference: Code Review Style

## Rules learned from user corrections
- Never flag single-letter variables in loops (i, j, k are fine)
- Don't suggest type annotations for test helper functions
- Always flag any function over 50 lines, regardless of complexity
- Prefer "not X" over "X is False" in Python conditionals

## Source
- 2026-07-05: User said "stop flagging loop variables"
- 2026-07-08: User said "test helpers don't need types, they change too fast"
- 2026-07-12: User said "I want to know about long functions always"

Pattern 3: Project Evolution Tracking

As the project changes across runs, memory tracks the evolution — what changed, why, and what the implications are.


# Evolution: Auth System

## Timeline
- 2026-06-01: Session-based auth (express-session + Redis)
- 2026-06-15: Added JWT for API clients (dual auth: session + JWT)
- 2026-07-01: Migrating fully to JWT (session deprecated, will be removed)
- 2026-07-20: Sessions removed. Pure JWT now.

## Implications
- Old code referencing req.session is deprecated
- Token refresh is now handled client-side
- Auth middleware checks JWT only (no more cookie parsing)

12.6 Memory and Loop Quality

Memory directly improves loop quality in measurable ways:

Memory Type Quality Improvement Mechanism
Error memory Fewer iterations on known problems Skip diagnosis, go straight to fix
User preferences Fewer corrections needed Match user expectations on first pass
Procedural memory Faster task completion Known-good procedures, no exploration
Semantic memory Better decisions Richer context for reasoning
Episode memory Avoid repeated mistakes Don't try approaches that failed before

The compound effect: a loop with well-maintained memory converges faster (fewer iterations), produces higher-quality output (matches preferences and avoids known pitfalls), and costs less (less wasted exploration) with every passing week.

12.7 Memory Anti-Patterns

The Hoarder

Saving everything. Every tool result, every reasoning step, every intermediate state. Memory bloats until retrieval returns noise instead of signal. Fix: Strict capture criteria. If it's derivable, don't store it.

The Amnesia Loop

Never consolidating. Episodes accumulate without being synthesized into semantic or procedural memory. The system "remembers" individual events but never learns patterns. Fix: Schedule consolidation after every N runs.

The Stale Oracle

Memories that were true once but aren't anymore. "Use pytest-asyncio for async tests" when the project migrated to anyio. The loop follows outdated procedures and fails. Fix: Verify memories against reality before acting on them. Timestamp and review regularly.

The Overfitted Memory

Procedural memories that are too specific. "When test_auth_expiry fails, add 1 to the timeout value" — a memorized fix for a symptom, not the underlying cause. Fix: Capture the root cause, not the specific workaround.

12.8 Implementation: The File-Based Memory Tool

The simplest production-ready memory implementation:


from pathlib import Path
from datetime import datetime
import frontmatter

class FileMemory:
    def __init__(self, base_dir: Path):
        self.base_dir = base_dir
        self.base_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
    
    def save(self, key: str, content: str, memory_type: str, 
             importance: int = 3) -> Path:
        file_path = self.base_dir / f"{key}.md"
        doc = frontmatter.Post(
            content=content,
            name=key,
            type=memory_type,
            importance=importance,
            created=datetime.now().isoformat(),
            last_accessed=datetime.now().isoformat(),
        )
        file_path.write_text(frontmatter.dumps(doc))
        self._update_index(key, doc.metadata)
        return file_path
    
    def recall(self, query: str, limit: int = 5) -> list[str]:
        """Simple keyword search across memory files."""
        results = []
        for file_path in self.base_dir.glob("*.md"):
            content = file_path.read_text()
            if self._matches(query, content):
                results.append(content)
                self._touch(file_path)  # Update last_accessed
        return sorted(results, key=self._relevance_score)[:limit]
    
    def forget(self, key: str) -> None:
        file_path = self.base_dir / f"{key}.md"
        archive_path = self.base_dir / "archive" / f"{key}.md"
        file_path.rename(archive_path)
        self._update_index(key, remove=True)

Simple. File-based. Human-readable. Version-controllable. No database infrastructure required. For most loops, this is sufficient.

Next: Chapter 13 provides the decision framework for choosing between skills, tools, MCP servers, subagents, prompts, and code — the primitives that compose every loop.

Chapter 13: Choosing Your Primitive

Level: B (Brief) · ~2,800 words · Levels served: L2, L3, L4

13.1 The Primitive Zoo

When building a loop, you have six primitives available at every decision point:

  1. Prompt: Instructions to the model in natural language
  2. Code: Deterministic logic in your harness
  3. Tool: A function the model can call
  4. MCP Server: An external service exposing tools and resources
  5. Skill: Reusable project knowledge loaded into context
  6. Subagent: A separate model execution with its own context

Each has a different cost profile, context footprint, reliability characteristic, and appropriate use case. The most common mistake in loop engineering is using the wrong primitive for the job — typically, using a model call where code would suffice, or using a subagent where a tool would work.

13.2 The Decision Table

When you need... Use Not
Deterministic transformation Code Prompt (non-deterministic)
Reasoning about ambiguous input Prompt Code (can't handle ambiguity)
Interaction with external system Tool/MCP Prompt (can't act on world)
Stable project knowledge Skill Prompt (rediscovered each run)
Independent evaluation Subagent Prompt (shares maker's context)
Parallel execution on different data Subagent Sequential prompts (slow)
Simple format conversion Code Tool (overhead not justified)
Complex multi-step operation Tool (composite) Multiple simple tools (coordination burden on model)
Knowledge that changes per project Skill Hardcoded prompt (not portable)

13.3 Cost and Context Profile

Each primitive carries a context cost (tokens consumed) and an execution cost (compute/time):

Primitive Context Cost Execution Cost Latency
Code 0 tokens CPU only <1ms
Prompt N tokens (instruction) Inference 1-30s
Tool (schema) ~800-1500 tokens per tool Varies Tool-dependent
MCP Server ~1000 tokens per tool exposed RPC + execution 100ms-10s
Skill 2K-15K tokens loaded 0 (just context) 0 (pre-loaded)
Subagent Full context per agent Full inference 10-120s

The total cost of a loop is the sum of its primitives across all iterations. Choosing cheaper primitives where they're sufficient has a multiplicative effect:


Loop cost = Σ (primitive_cost × iterations)

Using code vs. prompt for a parse step:
  Prompt: 500 tokens × 10 iterations = 5,000 tokens
  Code: 0 tokens × 10 iterations = 0 tokens
  Savings: 5,000 tokens per loop run

13.4 When to Use Code

Use code — not the model — for anything deterministic:

  • Parsing structured output: JSON/YAML parsing, regex extraction, schema validation
  • File operations: Reading, writing, copying, moving files
  • Data transformation: Sorting, filtering, mapping, formatting
  • Conditional logic: If-then routing based on clear criteria
  • Accumulation: Counting, summing, tracking state across iterations
  • Validation: Type checking, schema conformance, format verification

The rule: if the operation has no ambiguity — if a competent programmer could write it as a pure function — it should be code, not a model call.


# BAD: Using the model to parse test output
result = model.generate("Extract the test counts from this output: " + test_output)

# GOOD: Using code to parse test output
import re
match = re.search(r"(\d+) passed, (\d+) failed", test_output)
passed, failed = int(match.group(1)), int(match.group(2))

13.5 When to Use a Prompt

Use a prompt — an in-context instruction to the model — for:

  • Reasoning about ambiguous situations: What approach should we take? What's wrong here?
  • Generating novel content: Code, text, plans, explanations
  • Making judgment calls: Is this good enough? Should we continue or stop?
  • Synthesizing information: Combining multiple sources into a coherent understanding

13.6 When to Use a Tool

Use a tool when the model needs to interact with something outside its context:

  • External systems: Files, databases, APIs, services
  • Verification: Running tests, type checkers, linters
  • Information gathering: Searching code, reading documentation, querying data

Prefer fewer, well-designed tools over many narrow ones. The model's ability to choose correctly degrades with the number of options. A tool set of 5-10 well-designed tools is easier for the model to navigate than 50 narrowly-scoped ones.

13.7 When to Use an MCP Server

Use an MCP server instead of a direct tool when:

  • Multiple loops need the same integration: The server is shared infrastructure
  • Authentication is complex: OAuth flows, token refresh, credential management
  • The integration is maintained separately: Updates to the external service don't require updating every loop
  • You want dynamic discovery: Loops should be able to find new capabilities without code changes

Don't use an MCP server when:

  • The integration is trivial (a single HTTP call)
  • Latency is critical (the RPC hop adds ~50-100ms)
  • Only one loop will ever use it

13.8 When to Use a Skill

Use a skill (loaded context document) when:

  • The knowledge is stable: It doesn't change between runs
  • The knowledge is needed every run: The cold-start tax is real
  • The knowledge is non-derivable: Can't be easily discovered from the code itself
  • The knowledge shapes behavior: Rules, preferences, conventions that affect output quality

Don't use a skill when:

  • The information changes frequently (it'll be stale)
  • The information is easily derivable (reading a config file is cheaper)
  • The information is rarely needed (progressive disclosure, load on demand)

13.9 When to Use a Subagent

Use a subagent — a separate model execution with its own context — when:

  • Context isolation is needed: The maker and checker must not share context
  • Parallel execution provides speedup: Multiple independent tasks can run simultaneously
  • The task exceeds one context window: Too much information for a single model to hold
  • Specialization helps: Different tasks benefit from different prompts/tools/models
  • Independence improves quality: Multiple perspectives catch more issues

Don't use a subagent when:

  • A tool call would suffice (subagents are expensive for simple operations)
  • The tasks are sequential and dependent (parallelism provides no benefit)
  • The context is small enough for one model (splitting adds coordination overhead)
  • Communication overhead between agents exceeds the benefit

13.10 The Composition Checklist

When designing a loop, for each step ask:

  1. Is this deterministic? → Code
  2. Does this need reasoning? → Prompt (stay in current context)
  3. Does this need external interaction? → Tool or MCP
  4. Does this need project knowledge? → Skill
  5. Does this need independence from the current context? → Subagent
  6. Is this shared across multiple loops? → MCP server

If multiple answers apply, prefer the cheaper option. Code > Tool > Prompt > Subagent, in ascending order of cost.

13.11 Anti-Patterns

Using a subagent for a tool call: "Spawn an agent to check if the file exists." A filesystem tool does this for zero inference cost.

Using a prompt for a parse: "Tell me what JSON key has value X." A jq command or dict lookup is instant and deterministic.

Using a tool for a code operation: Calling a "sort" tool when sorted() in the harness code is free.

Using a skill for volatile information: Loading today's metrics into a skill file that's stale by tomorrow.

Using code for ambiguous decisions: Writing complex heuristics when the model could reason about the decision naturally.

The right primitive is the one that solves the problem at the lowest cost with the highest reliability. When in doubt, start with code and escalate to model calls only when deterministic solutions are insufficient.

Chapter 14: Triggers and Automations — The Heartbeat

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

14.1 The Automation Rule

If you start the loop by hand, the human is still in the loop — they've just moved from "reviewer" to "initiator." The loop runs when they remember. It doesn't run when they forget. Their attention remains the bottleneck, just at a different stage.

The automation rule: a loop that requires human initiation is not fully automated. The trigger — the mechanism that starts the loop — is as important as the loop itself. Without automation, you have a powerful tool. With automation, you have a system.

14.2 Trigger Types

Schedule Triggers

The simplest automation: run the loop at fixed intervals.


# Cron-based schedule triggers
every_morning:
  cron: "57 8 * * 1-5"  # Weekdays at ~9am
  loop: "security_review"
  
every_hour:
  cron: "7 * * * *"     # Hourly (off :00 to avoid thundering herd)
  loop: "pr_scan"

nightly:
  cron: "23 2 * * *"    # 2:23am daily
  loop: "dependency_audit"

Best for: Monitoring loops, periodic scans, daily maintenance, scheduled checks.

Event Triggers

The loop starts when something happens in an external system.


# Event-based triggers
pr_opened:
  event: "github.pull_request.opened"
  loop: "code_review"
  args: { pr_number: "{{ event.number }}" }

ticket_ready:
  event: "linear.issue.status_changed"
  condition: "event.to_state == 'ready_for_dev'"
  loop: "implementation"
  args: { issue_id: "{{ event.id }}" }

deploy_completed:
  event: "github.deployment.completed"
  loop: "post_deploy_verification"

Best for: Reactive loops that respond to changes, CI/CD integration, workflow automation.

Threshold Triggers

The loop starts when a metric crosses a boundary.


error_spike:
  metric: "error_rate_5m"
  condition: "> 0.01"  # 1% error rate
  loop: "incident_diagnosis"
  cooldown: "15m"      # Don't re-trigger for 15 minutes

cost_alert:
  metric: "daily_token_spend"
  condition: "> 50.00"  # $50/day
  loop: "cost_investigation"
  cooldown: "4h"

Best for: Anomaly detection, SLA monitoring, resource management.

Webhook Triggers

External systems call your automation endpoint directly.


@app.post("/hooks/trigger")
async def webhook_trigger(payload: WebhookPayload):
    if payload.event == "review_requested":
        await start_loop("security_review", args=payload.data)
    elif payload.event == "build_failed":
        await start_loop("build_diagnosis", args=payload.data)

Best for: Integration with systems that support outbound webhooks (GitHub, Slack, CI tools).

Chained Triggers

One loop's completion triggers another loop.


pipeline:
  - loop: "code_review"
    on_success: "merge_check"
  - loop: "merge_check"
    on_success: "deploy"
  - loop: "deploy"
    on_success: "post_deploy_verify"
    on_failure: "rollback"

Best for: Multi-stage pipelines, approval workflows, dependent operations.

14.3 Idempotency

A trigger may fire more than once. Network retries, duplicate webhooks, overlapping schedules — all produce repeated invocations. Every loop started by a trigger must be idempotent: running it twice with the same input must produce the same outcome without harmful side effects.

Idempotency patterns:

1. Check-before-act: Before the loop starts work, verify that the work hasn't already been done.


async def security_review_loop(pr_number: int):
    # Check if we already reviewed this PR version
    existing_review = await get_existing_review(pr_number)
    if existing_review and existing_review.commit == await get_head_commit(pr_number):
        return  # Already reviewed this version, skip
    
    # Proceed with review...

2. Deduplication keys: Track which triggers have been processed using a unique key.


class TriggerDeduplicator:
    def __init__(self):
        self.processed = set()
    
    async def should_process(self, trigger_id: str) -> bool:
        if trigger_id in self.processed:
            return False
        self.processed.add(trigger_id)
        return True

3. Last-write-wins: For loops that produce artifacts (comments, files, reports), overwrite previous output rather than appending. Running twice produces one result, not two.

14.4 Re-Entrancy

What happens when a trigger fires while the loop is already running? Three strategies:

Queue: Buffer the new trigger and run it after the current invocation completes. Simple, ensures no concurrent execution, but creates latency.

Skip: Discard the new trigger if the loop is already running. Appropriate when the current run will handle the new event as part of its normal operation.

Replace: Cancel the current run and start fresh with the new trigger. Appropriate when the new event invalidates the current run's work (e.g., new commits pushed while reviewing old commits).


class LoopScheduler:
    def __init__(self, strategy: str = "queue"):
        self.strategy = strategy
        self.running: dict[str, asyncio.Task] = {}
        self.queue: dict[str, list] = {}
    
    async def trigger(self, loop_name: str, args: dict):
        if loop_name in self.running:
            if self.strategy == "queue":
                self.queue.setdefault(loop_name, []).append(args)
            elif self.strategy == "skip":
                return  # Discard
            elif self.strategy == "replace":
                self.running[loop_name].cancel()
                self.running[loop_name] = asyncio.create_task(
                    self.run_loop(loop_name, args)
                )
        else:
            self.running[loop_name] = asyncio.create_task(
                self.run_loop(loop_name, args)
            )

14.5 Cooldown and Rate Limiting

Triggers can fire too frequently. A threshold trigger on error rate might fire every second during an outage. An event trigger on file changes might fire on every save during active development. Without rate limiting, the loop consumes unbounded resources.

Cooldown: After the loop runs, ignore new triggers for a specified period.

Rate limiting: Maximum N invocations per time window.

Debouncing: Wait until triggers stop arriving for a specified period before running. Appropriate for file-change triggers where multiple changes happen in rapid succession.


class DebouncedTrigger:
    def __init__(self, delay_seconds: float = 5.0):
        self.delay = delay_seconds
        self.pending: asyncio.Task | None = None
    
    async def fire(self, args: dict):
        if self.pending:
            self.pending.cancel()
        self.pending = asyncio.create_task(self._delayed_run(args))
    
    async def _delayed_run(self, args: dict):
        await asyncio.sleep(self.delay)
        await self.run_loop(args)

14.6 Trigger Observability

Every trigger invocation should be logged with:

  • When it fired
  • What event caused it
  • Whether it was processed, skipped, or queued
  • The loop's outcome (success, failure, budget exhausted)
  • Duration and cost

This data answers critical operational questions:

  • Are triggers firing too often? (Adjust cooldowns or thresholds)
  • Are loops failing frequently? (Investigate the failure pattern)
  • Is the cost within budget? (Adjust trigger frequency or loop scope)
  • Are there dead triggers? (Triggers that fire but never produce useful work)

14.7 Production Trigger Architecture

A production automation system typically looks like:


┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐
│   Sources    │     │   Router     │     │   Executor   │
│              │────▶│              │────▶│              │
│ - Cron       │     │ - Dedup      │     │ - Start loop │
│ - Webhooks   │     │ - Rate limit │     │ - Monitor    │
│ - Events     │     │ - Route      │     │ - Report     │
│ - Thresholds │     │ - Queue      │     │ - Cleanup    │
└──────────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────────────┘

The router is the critical piece — it prevents duplicate processing, enforces rate limits, routes events to the appropriate loop, and manages the queue of pending work.

14.8 Designing Your Trigger Strategy

Start with these questions:

  1. What events matter? List the events that should trigger loop execution.
  2. How quickly must you respond? Determines whether you need event triggers (immediate) or schedule triggers (periodic).
  3. What's the maximum cost per day? Determines rate limits and cooldowns.
  4. What happens if you miss an event? Determines whether you need guaranteed delivery or can tolerate occasional skips.
  5. What happens if you process the same event twice? Determines your idempotency requirements.

For most teams starting with loop automation:

  • Week 1: One schedule trigger (daily security scan or weekly dependency audit)
  • Week 2: One event trigger (PR-opened review loop)
  • Month 2: Add threshold triggers (error rate, cost alerts)
  • Month 3: Add chained triggers (review → merge → deploy pipeline)

Build the automation incrementally. Each new trigger is a commitment to maintaining and monitoring an autonomous system. Don't add triggers faster than you can observe their behavior.

Next: Chapter 15 covers Goal Specification — turning human intent into machine-checkable acceptance criteria, the highest-leverage skill in loop engineering.

Chapter 15: Goal Specification

Level: A (Anchor) · ~5,800 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

15.1 The Highest-Leverage Skill

If you could invest in exactly one skill to improve your loops, invest in goal specification. Everything downstream — oracle design, stop conditions, cost management, fleet coordination — depends on the quality of the goal. A precise goal enables a precise oracle. A vague goal forces a vague oracle, which produces unreliable verification, which produces unreliable loops.

Goal specification is the art of turning human intent ("make it work," "fix the bug," "write good documentation") into machine-checkable acceptance criteria that an oracle can evaluate without human judgment.

This is harder than it sounds. Humans communicate intent through implication, shared context, and unstated standards. Machines evaluate criteria through explicit checks against formal specifications. The gap between these two modes is where loops fail.

15.2 The Spectrum of Goal Precision

Goals exist on a precision spectrum:


Vague ───────────────────────────────────────────────── Precise

"Make it better"   "Fix the auth"   "Make test X pass"   "test_auth_expired
                                                          returns 401 with
                                                          RFC 7807 body when
                                                          token.exp < now()"

The further right your goal, the more reliable your loop. Each position on the spectrum has different implications:

Precision Level Oracle Type Iterations Needed Cost Predictability
Vague Human judgment Unpredictable Unpredictable
Directional Rubric (model judge) 3-10 Medium
Specific Property tests 2-5 High
Exact Deterministic check 1-3 Very high

15.3 Turning Intent into Criteria

The conversion process has three steps:

Step 1: Identify the observable outcome

Ask: "What is true when this is done that isn't true now?" The answer must be something observable — not a feeling, not a preference, but a measurable state change.

  • Bad: "The code should be cleaner" (not observable)
  • Good: "All functions are under 30 lines, no function has more than 3 parameters, cyclomatic complexity is below 10 for every function" (measurable)

Step 2: Make it machine-checkable

Ask: "Could I write a program that checks this without calling a human?" If yes, you have a goal. If no, you need to decompose further or accept that a model judge will be needed.


# Machine-checkable goal: all tests pass
def goal_met() -> bool:
    result = subprocess.run(["pytest", "tests/", "-x"], capture_output=True)
    return result.returncode == 0

# Machine-checkable goal: complexity thresholds
def goal_met() -> bool:
    report = run_complexity_analysis("src/")
    return all(f.complexity < 10 for f in report.functions)

# NOT machine-checkable (requires judgment): "code is readable"
# Needs decomposition into checkable sub-goals

Step 3: Specify failure modes

Ask: "What does unacceptable look like?" Defining failure is often easier than defining success, and it constrains the solution space.

  • "The response must not contain a stack trace" (failure: exposing internals)
  • "The function must handle null input without throwing" (failure: unhandled exception)
  • "The documentation must cover all public methods" (failure: undocumented method)

15.4 Goal Structures

Binary Goals

The simplest form: pass/fail. Either the criteria is met or it isn't.


goal:
  type: binary
  criteria: "All tests in tests/auth/ pass"
  check: "pytest tests/auth/ -x && echo PASS || echo FAIL"

Best for: Bug fixes, test-related tasks, compilation, linting.

Threshold Goals

The output must meet a minimum quality level on a quantitative scale.


goal:
  type: threshold
  criteria: "Documentation coverage exceeds 90%"
  check: "doc-coverage src/ --format json | jq '.coverage'"
  threshold: 0.90

Best for: Quality metrics, performance targets, coverage goals.

Convergent Goals

The loop runs until no new issues are found — convergence indicates completeness.


goal:
  type: convergent
  criteria: "No new security findings in two consecutive passes"
  check: "Run security scan, compare findings with previous run"
  convergence_threshold: 2  # Consecutive clean passes

Best for: Discovery tasks (finding bugs, security issues, documentation gaps).

Comparative Goals

The output must be better than a baseline by some measurable amount.


goal:
  type: comparative
  criteria: "Response time is 30% faster than baseline"
  baseline: "benchmarks/baseline.json"
  check: "Run benchmarks, compare with baseline"
  improvement_threshold: 0.30

Best for: Optimization tasks, refactoring, performance improvements.

Composite Goals

Multiple criteria that must ALL be satisfied.


goal:
  type: composite
  criteria:
    - type: binary
      check: "All tests pass"
    - type: binary
      check: "Type checker reports zero errors"
    - type: threshold
      check: "Coverage > 80%"
      threshold: 0.80
    - type: binary
      check: "No lint warnings"
  all_required: true

Best for: Complex tasks with multiple quality dimensions.

15.5 Acceptance Criteria as Executable Artifacts

The most powerful form of goal specification: acceptance criteria that are themselves executable programs. The goal isn't described — it's embodied in code that can run.

Test-as-goal:


# The goal IS the test. If this passes, the task is done.
def test_expired_token_returns_401():
    token = create_token(expires_at=datetime.now() - timedelta(hours=1))
    response = client.get("/api/protected", headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {token}"})
    assert response.status_code == 401
    assert response.json()["type"] == "https://errors.example.com/expired-token"
    assert "token has expired" in response.json()["detail"].lower()

The loop's job is to make this test pass. The test IS the oracle. No ambiguity about what "done" means.

Contract-as-goal:


# Property-based contract: must hold for ALL valid inputs
from hypothesis import given, strategies as st

@given(st.text(), st.text())
def test_search_contract(query: str, document: str):
    """Search must: return results that contain the query,
    never return results that don't contain the query,
    return results in relevance order."""
    results = search(query, document)
    for result in results:
        assert query.lower() in result.text.lower()
    for i in range(len(results) - 1):
        assert results[i].score >= results[i+1].score

Schema-as-goal:


{
  "$schema": "http://json-schema.org/draft-07/schema#",
  "type": "object",
  "required": ["title", "body", "author", "published_at"],
  "properties": {
    "title": { "type": "string", "minLength": 10, "maxLength": 100 },
    "body": { "type": "string", "minLength": 500 },
    "author": { "type": "string" },
    "published_at": { "type": "string", "format": "date-time" }
  },
  "additionalProperties": false
}

15.6 Rubrics as Specifications

When deterministic checks aren't possible (creative tasks, reasoning tasks, quality assessments), rubrics bridge the gap. A rubric is a structured scoring guide that enables consistent evaluation — by a model judge or a human.

Anatomy of a good rubric:


rubric:
  name: "Code Review Quality"
  dimensions:
    - name: "Correctness"
      weight: 0.4
      levels:
        5: "Identifies all actual bugs with correct explanations"
        4: "Identifies most bugs, no false positives"
        3: "Identifies some bugs, minor false positives"
        2: "Misses major bugs or has significant false positives"
        1: "Fails to identify obvious issues"
    
    - name: "Actionability"
      weight: 0.3
      levels:
        5: "Every finding has a specific fix with file:line reference"
        4: "Most findings have specific fixes"
        3: "Findings describe the problem but not the fix"
        2: "Findings are vague, hard to act on"
        1: "Findings don't map to actionable changes"
    
    - name: "Severity Assessment"
      weight: 0.3
      levels:
        5: "Severity ratings match actual risk accurately"
        4: "Mostly accurate severity, occasional over/under-rating"
        3: "Severity is roughly directional"
        2: "Severity ratings seem arbitrary"
        1: "Critical issues rated low, trivial issues rated high"
  
  passing_score: 3.5  # Weighted average must exceed this

Using rubrics in loops:

The rubric is provided to a judge agent (separate from the maker agent). The judge scores the output against each dimension. If the weighted average exceeds the threshold, the loop considers the goal met.


async def rubric_evaluation(output: str, rubric: Rubric) -> float:
    judge_prompt = f"""
    Evaluate this output against the rubric below.
    Score each dimension 1-5 with a one-sentence justification.
    
    Output to evaluate:
    {output}
    
    Rubric:
    {rubric.to_text()}
    """
    scores = await judge_model.generate(judge_prompt, schema=RubricScores)
    return rubric.weighted_average(scores)

15.7 The Goal Decomposition Pattern

Complex goals that can't be directly machine-checked can often be decomposed into sub-goals that can:


High-level goal (not directly checkable):
  "Write comprehensive documentation for the auth module"

Decomposed into checkable sub-goals:
  1. All public functions have docstrings (ast check)
  2. Every function's docstring includes: description, params, returns, raises (format check)
  3. At least one usage example per function (pattern check)
  4. All cross-references resolve to existing functions (link check)
  5. Readability score > grade 10 on Flesch-Kincaid (metric check)
  6. No function is undocumented (coverage check)

Each sub-goal is independently checkable. The composite goal is met when all sub-goals pass. The decomposition transforms an uncheckable goal into a set of checkable ones.

15.8 Goal Anti-Patterns

The Moving Goal

A goal that changes as the loop works. "Make the code better" → the model makes one change → "but also handle this case" → the model handles it → "and also..." This creates an infinite loop because the target is never stable.

Fix: Define the complete goal before the loop starts. If new requirements emerge, stop the current loop, update the goal, and restart.

The Proxy Goal

Optimizing a measurable proxy rather than the actual intent. "Increase test coverage to 95%" when the actual goal is "ensure correctness." The loop writes trivial tests that increase coverage without testing meaningful behavior.

Fix: Include quality constraints alongside quantity metrics. "Coverage > 95% AND all tests verify at least one assertion about business logic."

The Unfalsifiable Goal

A goal that's defined in terms the oracle can't evaluate. "The code should be elegant." "The documentation should be clear." These feel like goals, but no oracle can determine when they're met.

Fix: Operationalize. "Elegant" → "no function exceeds 20 lines, names describe intent, no nested conditionals deeper than 2." "Clear" → "readability grade 8, no jargon without definition, every concept has an example."

The Implicit Goal

A goal that exists only in the human's mind. The loop is told "fix the bug" but the human also expects the fix to maintain backward compatibility, follow the existing code style, not change the public API, and include a test. These unstated expectations lead to technically-correct-but-unacceptable output.

Fix: Explicit composite goals that include ALL expectations, not just the primary one.

15.9 From Goal to Loop Contract

The goal specification feeds directly into the Loop Contract:


# Human intent: "Fix the login bug"
# ↓ Specification process
# ↓

loop_contract:
  goal:
    primary: "test_login_with_expired_session passes"
    constraints:
      - "All existing tests continue to pass (no regression)"
      - "Public API of auth module unchanged"
      - "Changes limited to src/auth/ directory"
    
  oracle:
    primary: "pytest tests/test_login.py::test_login_with_expired_session"
    constraints:
      - "pytest tests/ -x (full regression)"
      - "git diff --stat | grep -v 'src/auth/' should be empty"
      - "python -c 'import auth; help(auth)' matches baseline"
    
  budget:
    max_iterations: 5
    max_tokens: 300_000
    
  stop_condition:
    success: "All oracle checks pass"
    failure: "Budget exhausted OR 3 consecutive iterations with no progress"
    
  escalation_path:
    on_failure: "Create GitHub issue with diagnosis, @ team lead"

The precision of the goal determines the precision of everything else. A vague goal produces a vague contract. A precise goal produces a precise, enforceable contract.

15.10 Practicing Goal Specification

Like any skill, goal specification improves with practice. Here's a training exercise:

Take any task you'd give to an AI assistant. Write it as you normally would. Then rewrite it with:

  1. Observable outcome (what's true when done?)
  2. Machine-checkable criteria (what program verifies this?)
  3. Explicit constraints (what must NOT change?)
  4. Failure definition (what's unacceptable?)

Before: "Update the README"

After: "README.md includes: project description (>50 words), installation instructions that work when followed literally (verified by running them in a clean Docker container), API documentation for all exported functions (verified by comparing with grep -r 'export function'), and zero broken links (verified by linkchecker)."

The second version takes longer to write. It runs autonomously and reliably. The first version requires human review every single time.

Chapter 16: Planning and Replanning

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4

16.1 The Plan as Durable Artifact

In a loop, the plan isn't just a thought the model has and then forgets. It's a durable artifact — a document that persists across iterations, gets updated as reality changes, and provides continuity when context is compacted or the loop is handed off.

A plan in memory is fragile. A plan in a file is persistent. A plan in a task list is actionable.


# Plan: Fix authentication race condition

## Status: In Progress (iteration 3/5)

## Steps
- [x] 1. Reproduce the bug (test_concurrent_login)
- [x] 2. Identify root cause (token validation not atomic)
- [ ] 3. Implement fix (optimistic locking with retry)  ← CURRENT
- [ ] 4. Verify fix (all auth tests pass)
- [ ] 5. Regression check (full test suite)

## Decisions Made
- Using optimistic locking over mutex (performance req)
- Retry limit: 3 attempts before failure

## Failed Approaches
- Mutex lock: worked but 3x latency regression (iteration 1)

This plan survives compaction. If the context is summarized, the plan file remains intact and readable. If a new agent takes over (handoff), it can read the plan and continue from the current step.

16.2 Plan Depth

How detailed should a plan be? The answer depends on the task's uncertainty:

Low uncertainty tasks (plan deeply):

The path from start to finish is clear. Each step is well-defined. The plan can specify exact files, exact changes, exact verifications.


## Steps
1. Edit src/auth.py:42 — add expiry check before validation
2. Edit tests/test_auth.py — add test_expired_token test case
3. Run pytest tests/test_auth.py -x
4. If pass → run full suite
5. If fail → read error, fix, goto 3

High uncertainty tasks (plan shallowly):

The path isn't clear yet. Early steps will reveal information that changes later steps. Plan only the next 2-3 steps, and replan after each.


## Steps
1. Investigate: what's causing the performance regression?
2. Based on findings, identify the top 3 bottlenecks
3. [REPLAN after step 2 — approach depends on what we find]

The planning depth rule: Plan only as far as you can see clearly. Don't plan step 10 when steps 4-6 might invalidate your assumptions about what step 10 should be.

16.3 Decomposition Depth

A plan step should be achievable in a bounded number of model calls (ideally 1-3). If a step requires more than 5 calls to complete, it should be decomposed into sub-steps.

Too coarse:


Step 3: Implement the entire authentication system

This is a project, not a step. It'll take dozens of iterations and the plan provides no guidance on how to proceed.

Too fine:


Step 3a: Open file auth.py
Step 3b: Navigate to line 42
Step 3c: Type "if token.expired:"
Step 3d: Press enter
Step 3e: Type "    return Response(401)"

This is micro-management. The model is perfectly capable of writing code without keystroke-level instructions.

Right granularity:


Step 3: Add token expiry check in auth.py before the existing validation logic.
         Expired tokens should return 401 with an RFC 7807 error body.

One clear objective. The model knows what to do. It can do it in 1-2 calls.

16.4 Replanning as Progress vs. Thrash

Replanning — revising the plan based on new information — is a healthy part of loop execution. But replanning can also be a symptom of thrashing: the model keeps changing its approach without making progress.

Healthy replanning (progress):

  • Triggered by new information ("tests revealed the bug is in a different file than expected")
  • The new plan builds on what was learned
  • Each replan moves closer to the goal
  • Replans decrease in scope (narrowing down)

Unhealthy replanning (thrash):

  • Triggered by failure without diagnosis ("that didn't work, let's try something else")
  • The new plan ignores what was learned
  • Each replan is equally far from the goal
  • Replans maintain or increase in scope (flailing)

Detecting thrash:


def is_thrashing(plan_history: list[Plan]) -> bool:
    if len(plan_history) < 3:
        return False
    
    # Plans keep changing but completion doesn't advance
    completion_rates = [p.percent_complete for p in plan_history[-3:]]
    if max(completion_rates) - min(completion_rates) < 5:
        return True  # No progress across 3 replans
    
    # Plans are oscillating (approach A → B → A)
    if plan_history[-1].approach == plan_history[-3].approach:
        return True  # Went back to an earlier approach
    
    return False

When thrashing is detected, the loop should stop and escalate. More iterations won't help — the problem requires a fundamentally different approach or human insight.

16.5 Plan-as-Memory

The plan is also a memory device. It records:

  • What was tried (preventing re-attempts of failed approaches)
  • What was decided (preventing decision reversal)
  • What was learned (preserving discoveries across iterations)

When context is compacted, the plan survives because it's a file. When a new agent inherits the work, the plan provides continuity. The plan is the loop's external memory for its current task.

16.6 Todo State as Memory

A lightweight alternative to a full plan file: a todo list that tracks step status.


# Claude Code's task system as plan state
tasks = [
    {"id": 1, "subject": "Reproduce bug", "status": "completed"},
    {"id": 2, "subject": "Identify root cause", "status": "completed"},
    {"id": 3, "subject": "Implement fix", "status": "in_progress"},
    {"id": 4, "subject": "Verify fix", "status": "pending"},
    {"id": 5, "subject": "Regression check", "status": "pending"},
]

The todo list is simpler than a full plan but serves the same purpose: durable state that persists across iterations and context boundaries. The model marks tasks complete as it progresses, providing clear visibility into where it is.

16.7 The Plan Update Protocol

When the plan needs updating (new information, failed step, changed requirements), follow a protocol:

  1. State what changed: "Step 3 failed because X"
  2. State what you learned: "This tells us Y"
  3. Update the plan: Modify the remaining steps based on the new information
  4. Record the failed approach: Add it to the "Failed Approaches" section so it's not retried

## Update (iteration 3)
### What changed
Step 3 (mutex approach) completed but verification revealed 3x latency.

### What we learned
- Synchronization overhead is unacceptable for the hot path
- Need a non-blocking approach
- The race window is ~5ms based on the test timing

### Revised steps
- [x] 3. ~~Implement mutex lock~~ REVERTED (latency regression)
- [ ] 3. Implement optimistic locking with retry ← NEW APPROACH
- [ ] 4. Verify fix (all auth tests pass, latency < baseline * 1.1)
- [ ] 5. Regression check

### Failed approaches (do not retry)
- Mutex lock: correct but 3x latency. Unacceptable for p99 < 50ms requirement.

16.8 Planning in Fleet Loops

In a fleet (Chapter 24), the orchestrator creates the plan and delegates steps to specialists. This creates a coordination challenge: the plan must be visible to all agents, and updates by any agent must propagate.

Centralized planning:

The orchestrator owns the plan. Specialists report results. The orchestrator updates the plan and assigns next steps.


Orchestrator: [Owns plan file]
    ├── Specialist A: "Execute step 3, report result"
    ├── Specialist B: "Execute step 4, report result"
    └── Orchestrator: [Updates plan based on results, assigns next steps]

Decentralized planning:

Each specialist has its own sub-plan for its domain. The orchestrator manages dependencies between sub-plans but doesn't micro-manage within them.


Orchestrator: [High-level plan: milestones and dependencies]
    ├── Specialist A: [Own plan for steps 3-5]
    ├── Specialist B: [Own plan for steps 6-8]
    └── Orchestrator: [Wait for A, then unblock C]

Centralized planning is simpler but creates a bottleneck (everything flows through the orchestrator). Decentralized planning is more efficient but requires clear interfaces between specialists' domains.

16.9 When Not to Plan

Not every task needs a plan. The overhead of planning (creating the artifact, maintaining it, checking against it) is wasted on simple tasks:

  • Single-step fixes with clear resolution
  • Tasks completable in one model call
  • Exploratory tasks where the goal is to learn, not to produce

The planning threshold: if the task requires more than 3 steps or has meaningful uncertainty about the approach, plan. If it's straightforward and achievable in 1-2 steps, just do it.

Chapter 17: Execution and State — Worktrees, Sandboxes, Checkpoints

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4

17.1 The Isolation Imperative

When a loop executes, it modifies state: files change, databases get written, APIs get called. Without isolation, these modifications are permanent and immediate. A mistake in iteration 3 corrupts the state that iteration 4 needs. Two parallel agents editing the same file produce garbage. A failed loop leaves the workspace in an inconsistent state that the developer must clean up manually.

Isolation solves this: give each execution a contained environment where it can work freely, verify its output, and only commit changes to the real world when verification passes.

17.2 Git Worktrees for Code Loops

The most common isolation mechanism for code-modifying loops is the git worktree — a second checkout of the same repository on a separate branch, in a separate directory.


# Create an isolated workspace
git worktree add .claude/worktrees/fix-auth -b fix/auth-race

# Agent works in the isolated directory
# All changes happen on the fix/auth-race branch
# The main workspace is untouched

# On success: merge back
git merge fix/auth-race

# On failure: delete and try again
git worktree remove .claude/worktrees/fix-auth
git branch -D fix/auth-race

Why worktrees, not branches alone?

A branch switch changes the working directory in-place. If two agents need to work simultaneously (fleet loop), they can't both switch branches in the same directory. Worktrees give each agent its own directory with its own branch — true parallel execution without file conflicts.

The worktree lifecycle:


CREATE ──→ WORK ──→ VERIFY ──→ MERGE or DISCARD
  │          │         │           │
  │          │         │           └─ Success: merge to main
  │          │         │              Failure: rm worktree
  │          │         │
  │          │         └─ Run tests, check goals
  │          │
  │          └─ Agent edits, creates, deletes files
  │
  └─ Fresh branch from origin/main

Cost of worktrees:

  • Setup: ~200-500ms (git operations)
  • Disk: full copy of working tree (can be significant for large repos)
  • Merge complexity: potential conflicts when merging back
  • Cleanup: must be explicitly removed or they accumulate

Use worktrees when: Multiple agents edit code in parallel, or a single agent needs to safely experiment without corrupting the main workspace.

Don't use when: A single agent works sequentially on one file at a time. The overhead isn't justified — just work directly and revert on failure.

17.3 Sandboxes for Execution

Code execution carries risk. A loop that can run arbitrary commands might accidentally:

  • Delete important files (rm -rf /)
  • Consume all disk space (infinite loop writing to disk)
  • Exhaust memory (memory leak in generated code)
  • Make unwanted network calls
  • Modify system configuration

Sandboxes contain these risks by limiting what the execution can access:

Container-based sandbox:


import docker

def execute_in_sandbox(code: str, timeout: int = 30) -> str:
    client = docker.from_env()
    container = client.containers.run(
        "python:3.12-slim",
        command=["python", "-c", code],
        mem_limit="512m",
        cpu_period=100000,
        cpu_quota=50000,  # 50% of one CPU
        network_disabled=True,  # No network access
        read_only=True,  # Can't modify filesystem
        detach=True,
        timeout=timeout,
    )
    container.wait(timeout=timeout)
    output = container.logs().decode()
    container.remove()
    return output

Filesystem sandbox:


import tempfile
from pathlib import Path

class FilesystemSandbox:
    def __init__(self):
        self.root = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="loop-sandbox-"))
    
    def write(self, relative_path: str, content: str):
        full_path = self.root / relative_path
        assert full_path.resolve().is_relative_to(self.root)  # Prevent path traversal
        full_path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        full_path.write_text(content)
    
    def commit_to_real(self, real_root: Path):
        """Copy verified changes from sandbox to real filesystem."""
        for file in self.root.rglob("*"):
            if file.is_file():
                dest = real_root / file.relative_to(self.root)
                dest.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
                shutil.copy2(file, dest)
    
    def cleanup(self):
        shutil.rmtree(self.root)

17.4 Checkpoints and Resumability

A loop that crashes at iteration 7 of 10 shouldn't restart from iteration 1. Checkpoints save the loop's state at regular intervals so it can resume from the last good state.

What to checkpoint:


@dataclass
class LoopCheckpoint:
    iteration: int
    plan_state: dict          # Which steps completed
    modified_files: list[str]  # Files changed since start
    discoveries: list[str]     # Facts learned during the loop
    budget_spent: float        # Tokens/cost consumed so far
    last_verification: dict    # Results of last oracle check
    timestamp: str

When to checkpoint:

  • After every successful iteration (the loop made verified progress)
  • Before any risky operation (destructive action, expensive API call)
  • At natural milestones (sub-task completed, phase transition)
  • When context is about to be compacted (preserve state before lossy operation)

Resuming from a checkpoint:


async def resume_loop(checkpoint_path: Path) -> Result:
    checkpoint = load_checkpoint(checkpoint_path)
    
    # Verify checkpoint is still valid
    if not verify_workspace_matches_checkpoint(checkpoint):
        raise StaleCheckpointError("Workspace changed since checkpoint")
    
    # Restore state
    plan = load_plan(checkpoint.plan_state)
    budget = Budget(remaining=checkpoint.budget_remaining)
    
    # Continue from last iteration
    for iteration in range(checkpoint.iteration, max_iterations):
        result = await execute_step(plan.current_step)
        verification = await verify(result)
        
        if verification.passed:
            plan.advance()
            save_checkpoint(create_checkpoint(iteration, plan, budget))
        
        if plan.complete:
            return Result(success=True)
    
    return Result(success=False, reason="budget_exhausted")

17.5 Rollback Strategies

When a loop fails — tests break, the output is wrong, the approach doesn't work — you need to undo its changes and return to a known-good state.

Git-based rollback (code loops):


# Option 1: Reset the worktree (if using worktrees)
git worktree remove .claude/worktrees/failed-attempt
git branch -D failed-attempt

# Option 2: Revert to a checkpoint commit
git reset --hard checkpoint-commit-sha

# Option 3: Selective revert (undo specific changes)
git checkout main -- src/auth.py  # Restore one file from main

Filesystem rollback (general):


class TransactionalFilesystem:
    def __init__(self, root: Path):
        self.root = root
        self.originals: dict[Path, str | None] = {}  # path → original content (None = didn't exist)
    
    def write(self, path: Path, content: str):
        if path not in self.originals:
            self.originals[path] = path.read_text() if path.exists() else None
        path.write_text(content)
    
    def rollback(self):
        for path, original in self.originals.items():
            if original is None:
                path.unlink(missing_ok=True)  # File was created — delete it
            else:
                path.write_text(original)     # File was modified — restore
        self.originals.clear()
    
    def commit(self):
        self.originals.clear()  # Discard rollback info — changes are permanent

17.6 State Management Patterns

Pattern 1: Immutable iterations

Each iteration produces a new state directory. On success, the latest state becomes the "current" state. On failure, it's discarded.


states/
├── iteration_001/  # Initial state
├── iteration_002/  # After step 1
├── iteration_003/  # After step 2 (current)
└── iteration_004/  # In progress (may be discarded)

Pattern 2: Write-ahead log

Before any modification, record what's about to change. On crash, replay the log to determine what happened and what needs to be undone.


class WriteAheadLog:
    def __init__(self, log_path: Path):
        self.log_path = log_path
    
    def record(self, operation: str, target: str, before: str, after: str):
        entry = {"op": operation, "target": target, "before": before, "after": after}
        with self.log_path.open("a") as f:
            f.write(json.dumps(entry) + "\n")
    
    def undo_last(self):
        entries = self.log_path.read_text().strip().split("\n")
        if entries:
            last = json.loads(entries[-1])
            Path(last["target"]).write_text(last["before"])

Pattern 3: Branch-per-attempt

Each iteration works on its own branch. Only verified branches get merged to the main working branch.


main ──────────────────────────────────────────→
       \         \         \
        attempt-1  attempt-2  attempt-3
        (failed)   (failed)   (merged ✓)

17.7 Parallel Execution Safety

In a fleet loop, multiple agents may execute simultaneously. Safety requires:

  1. No shared mutable state: Each agent has its own worktree, own files, own sandbox
  2. Coordination through the orchestrator: Agents don't communicate directly — they report to the lead
  3. Merge strategy defined in advance: When parallel work needs to combine, the merge rules are explicit

async def parallel_execution(tasks: list[Task], orchestrator: Agent):
    # Create isolated workspace per agent
    worktrees = [create_worktree(f"agent-{i}") for i in range(len(tasks))]
    
    # Execute in parallel
    results = await asyncio.gather(*[
        execute_in_worktree(task, worktree)
        for task, worktree in zip(tasks, worktrees)
    ])
    
    # Merge verified results
    for result, worktree in zip(results, worktrees):
        if result.verified:
            merge_worktree(worktree)
        else:
            discard_worktree(worktree)

17.8 The Execution Checklist

Before a loop iteration executes:

  • [ ] Workspace is in a known-good state (checkpoint verified)
  • [ ] Changes are isolated (worktree, sandbox, or transaction)
  • [ ] Rollback mechanism is in place
  • [ ] Budget limits are enforced (timeout, resource caps)
  • [ ] Irreversible actions require approval

After execution:

  • [ ] Verification run against the goal
  • [ ] If passed: commit changes, advance plan
  • [ ] If failed: rollback, record failure, update plan
  • [ ] Checkpoint saved

Chapter 18: The Stop Problem

Level: A (Anchor) · ~5,500 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

18.1 The Most Under-Taught Topic

Every loop engineering tutorial teaches you how to start a loop. Almost none teach you how to stop one. This asymmetry is dangerous because a loop that can't stop is worse than a loop that never starts — it burns money, produces garbage, and blocks the system from doing useful work.

The stop problem is: given a loop that's running, how do you know when to terminate it? The answer isn't always "when the goal is met." Sometimes the answer is "when progress has stalled," "when the budget is exhausted," "when the approach is provably wrong," or "when continuing would make things worse."

18.2 The Five Termination Conditions

A well-designed loop can terminate for exactly five reasons:

1. Success

The oracle confirms that the goal is met. This is the happy path — the loop produced verified output.


if oracle.evaluate(output) >= goal.threshold:
    return TerminationReason.SUCCESS

2. Budget Exhaustion

The loop has consumed its allocated resources without achieving the goal. More resources won't help — either the problem is too hard for this loop, or the approach is wrong.


if budget.tokens_spent >= budget.max_tokens:
    return TerminationReason.BUDGET_EXHAUSTED
if budget.iterations >= budget.max_iterations:
    return TerminationReason.BUDGET_EXHAUSTED
if budget.wall_clock >= budget.max_time:
    return TerminationReason.BUDGET_EXHAUSTED

3. No Progress

The loop is running but not improving. The last N iterations produced output at the same quality level — the loop has plateaued.


def detect_no_progress(scores: list[float], window: int = 3) -> bool:
    if len(scores) < window:
        return False
    recent = scores[-window:]
    return max(recent) - min(recent) < 0.05  # Less than 5% variance

4. Oscillation

The loop is undoing and redoing the same changes — modifying a file, then reverting, then re-modifying. This is a sign that the loop has conflicting constraints it can't resolve.


def detect_oscillation(actions: list[Action], window: int = 6) -> bool:
    if len(actions) < window:
        return False
    # Check if actions repeat in a cycle
    half = window // 2
    first_half = actions[-window:-half]
    second_half = actions[-half:]
    return actions_equivalent(first_half, second_half)

5. External Signal

Something outside the loop triggered termination: a human interrupt, a dependency failure, a context change that invalidates the current work.


if interrupt_signal.is_set():
    return TerminationReason.HUMAN_INTERRUPT
if dependency_changed_since(loop_start):
    return TerminationReason.CONTEXT_STALE

18.3 Oscillation: The Silent Killer

Oscillation is the most insidious termination condition because it looks like activity. The loop is doing things — editing files, running tests, producing output — but it's not making progress. It's going in circles.

Common oscillation patterns:

The flip-flop: The model adds code to fix one test, which breaks another test, so it reverts, which re-breaks the first test.


Iteration 1: Add null check → test_null passes, test_valid fails
Iteration 2: Remove null check → test_valid passes, test_null fails
Iteration 3: Add null check → test_null passes, test_valid fails
(repeats forever)

The style war: The model reformats code, then "fixes" the formatting because it looks wrong, then reformats again.

The scope creep cycle: The model expands scope to fix an issue, then contracts scope because it's too complex, then expands again.

Detecting oscillation:


class OscillationDetector:
    def __init__(self, window: int = 4, similarity_threshold: float = 0.8):
        self.history: list[str] = []  # Sequence of file states or actions
        self.window = window
        self.threshold = similarity_threshold
    
    def record(self, state: str):
        self.history.append(state)
    
    def is_oscillating(self) -> bool:
        if len(self.history) < self.window:
            return False
        
        # Check if current state is similar to state N iterations ago
        for lookback in range(2, self.window):
            if len(self.history) > lookback:
                current = self.history[-1]
                past = self.history[-(lookback + 1)]
                if self.similarity(current, past) > self.threshold:
                    return True
        return False
    
    def similarity(self, a: str, b: str) -> float:
        # Simple: exact match or hash comparison
        # Better: diff-based similarity ratio
        return SequenceMatcher(None, a, b).ratio()

Breaking oscillation:

When oscillation is detected, the loop should not simply retry. It should:

  1. Diagnose: Why is the loop oscillating? What conflicting constraints exist?
  2. Escalate: Report the conflict to the orchestrator or human
  3. Reframe: Attempt a fundamentally different approach, not a variation of the same one

18.4 Deadlock in Fleet Loops

In multi-agent systems, deadlock is a termination condition unique to fleets: Agent A waits for Agent B's output, while Agent B waits for Agent A's output. Neither can proceed.


Agent A: "I need the API schema from Agent B before I can write tests"
Agent B: "I need the test structure from Agent A before I can design the API"
→ Deadlock: neither can proceed

Detection:


class DeadlockDetector:
    def __init__(self, timeout: float = 60.0):
        self.agent_states: dict[str, AgentState] = {}
        self.timeout = timeout
    
    def check(self) -> bool:
        # Find agents that haven't progressed
        stuck = [
            agent_id for agent_id, state in self.agent_states.items()
            if state.time_since_last_progress() > self.timeout
        ]
        
        if len(stuck) < 2:
            return False
        
        # Check for circular dependencies
        for agent_id in stuck:
            if self.has_circular_dependency(agent_id, stuck):
                return True
        return False

Resolution:

  • Break the cycle: The orchestrator provides a default value for one of the dependencies, breaking the circular wait
  • Reorder: Restructure the dependency graph so work can proceed serially
  • Timeout: The first agent to timeout produces a best-effort output, unblocking the other

18.5 Graceful Degradation

Not every termination needs to produce a perfect result. When a loop terminates due to budget or no-progress, it should still produce the best output it achieved — not nothing.


class GracefulLoop:
    def __init__(self):
        self.best_output: str | None = None
        self.best_score: float = 0.0
    
    async def run(self, goal: Goal, budget: Budget) -> Result:
        for iteration in range(budget.max_iterations):
            output = await self.execute_step()
            score = await self.verify(output)
            
            # Track best result seen so far
            if score > self.best_score:
                self.best_output = output
                self.best_score = score
            
            if score >= goal.threshold:
                return Result(status="success", output=output, score=score)
            
            if self.should_stop(budget, iteration):
                return Result(
                    status="partial",
                    output=self.best_output,
                    score=self.best_score,
                    reason=self.stop_reason
                )
        
        return Result(
            status="budget_exhausted",
            output=self.best_output,
            score=self.best_score
        )

The key: always return the best output achieved, even on non-success termination. A 70%-quality result is more valuable than no result. The human or downstream system can decide whether to use it.

18.6 Budget Design

Budget isn't just "how much can I spend." It's a termination signal and a convergence indicator.

Setting the right budget:

Too tight: The loop terminates before it can converge. Tasks that would succeed given one more iteration fail consistently. Symptom: high rate of budget-exhausted terminations with scores just below the threshold.

Too loose: The loop wastes money on problems it can't solve. A hard problem consumes the entire budget without converging. Symptom: expensive runs that terminate at budget with low scores.

Right-sized: The loop succeeds within budget 80-90% of the time, with 10-20% escalating to human review. The budget is sufficient for typical tasks and constrains atypical ones.

Budget by task type:

Task Type Typical Budget Reasoning
Simple bug fix 3 iterations, 100K tokens Usually converges in 1-2 iterations
Feature implementation 7 iterations, 500K tokens More exploration and testing needed
Research synthesis 5 iterations, 300K tokens Converges once sources are found
Code review 2 iterations, 200K tokens Usually right on first pass, one retry
Security audit 10 iterations, 1M tokens Needs thoroughness, multiple passes

Dynamic budget adjustment:


class AdaptiveBudget:
    def __init__(self, base_budget: Budget, history: list[LoopResult]):
        self.base = base_budget
        self.history = history
    
    def for_task(self, task: Task) -> Budget:
        # Find similar past tasks
        similar = [r for r in self.history if r.task_type == task.type]
        
        if not similar:
            return self.base  # No history, use default
        
        # Set budget at the 80th percentile of past successful runs
        successful = [r for r in similar if r.status == "success"]
        if successful:
            p80_iterations = percentile([r.iterations for r in successful], 80)
            p80_tokens = percentile([r.tokens_used for r in successful], 80)
            return Budget(
                max_iterations=int(p80_iterations * 1.2),  # 20% headroom
                max_tokens=int(p80_tokens * 1.2),
            )
        
        return self.base

18.7 The Hand-Off Decision

When a loop terminates without success, the escalation path determines what happens next. The hand-off should include everything the next handler (human or different loop) needs:


# Escalation Report

## Task
Fix the authentication race condition

## Status
Terminated: NO_PROGRESS after 5 iterations

## Best result achieved
Score: 6/10 (threshold: 8/10)
3 of 5 tests passing. 2 remain: test_concurrent_login, test_session_race

## What was tried
1. Mutex lock — correct but 3x latency (failed performance constraint)
2. Optimistic locking — partial success, handles simple case but not concurrent writes
3. Event sourcing approach — too complex, abandoned after exceeding scope

## What I believe the issue is
The race window is between token validation (auth.py:42) and session creation (auth.py:67).
Both operations need to be atomic, but the database doesn't support multi-table transactions
with the current ORM configuration.

## Recommended next steps
- Consider database-level locking (SELECT FOR UPDATE)
- Or restructure to single-table operation (merge token and session)
- Human judgment needed on which approach aligns with architecture goals

18.8 Stop Condition Anti-Patterns

No stop condition at all: The loop runs until context fills or the session crashes. This is the most dangerous anti-pattern — it guarantees either wasted money or corrupted state.

Only success stops: The loop terminates on success but has no other termination condition. If the goal is unreachable, the loop runs forever.

Arbitrary iteration limit: "Stop after 10 iterations" without considering whether the task type typically needs 3 or 30. Some tasks fail at 10 that would succeed at 12. Other tasks thrash for 10 iterations that should have stopped at 3.

No oscillation detection: The loop flip-flops between two states indefinitely, consuming budget without progress. Each iteration "looks like" work because the model is actively making changes — but the changes cancel each other out.

Ignoring no-progress: The model confidently tries variations of the same approach, scoring the same each time. Without no-progress detection, it'll consume the entire budget before stopping.

18.9 Implementing Stop Logic


class StopController:
    def __init__(self, config: StopConfig):
        self.config = config
        self.scores: list[float] = []
        self.states: list[str] = []
        self.start_time = time.time()
        self.tokens_used = 0
    
    def record_iteration(self, score: float, state: str, tokens: int):
        self.scores.append(score)
        self.states.append(state)
        self.tokens_used += tokens
    
    def should_stop(self) -> tuple[bool, str | None]:
        # Success
        if self.scores and self.scores[-1] >= self.config.success_threshold:
            return True, "success"
        
        # Budget
        if len(self.scores) >= self.config.max_iterations:
            return True, "max_iterations"
        if self.tokens_used >= self.config.max_tokens:
            return True, "max_tokens"
        if time.time() - self.start_time >= self.config.max_time:
            return True, "timeout"
        
        # No progress
        if len(self.scores) >= self.config.progress_window:
            recent = self.scores[-self.config.progress_window:]
            if max(recent) - min(recent) < self.config.progress_epsilon:
                return True, "no_progress"
        
        # Oscillation
        if len(self.states) >= 4:
            if self.states[-1] == self.states[-3] and self.states[-2] == self.states[-4]:
                return True, "oscillation"
        
        return False, None

This is the minimum viable stop controller. Production systems add: score trend analysis (if scores are decreasing, stop early), approach diversity checking (if the same approach has been tried 3 times, stop), and external signal handling (interrupts, dependency changes).
