Chapter 19: Closed Loops First, Open Loops Later

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

19.1 The Two Modes of Operation

A closed loop follows a human-designed path through a problem. The steps are predetermined. The verification is explicit. The scope is bounded. The model exercises judgment within these constraints, but the constraints themselves are fixed.

An open loop follows a model-discovered path. The model decides what to investigate, what approaches to try, and when to change direction. The scope is emergent. The constraints are minimal.


Closed Loop:                          Open Loop:
┌─────────────────────┐               ┌─────────────────────┐
│ Human designs path  │               │ Human defines goal   │
│ Model walks path    │               │ Model discovers path │
│ Oracle checks steps │               │ Model self-evaluates │
│ Bounded scope       │               │ Emergent scope       │
│ Predictable cost    │               │ Variable cost        │
└─────────────────────┘               └─────────────────────┘

Both have their place. But if you're building your first loops — or if you need reliability — start closed. Open loops are powerful but treacherous.

19.2 Closed Loops: Bounded Reliability

A closed loop constrains the model at every decision point:


closed_loop_example:
  trigger: "PR opened"
  steps:
    1: "Read the diff"
    2: "Check each file against security rules (enumerated)"
    3: "For each finding, verify it's real (call tool X)"
    4: "Format findings using template Y"
    5: "Post review comment"
  oracle: "Findings match expected format, no false positives vs. test set"
  budget: "3 iterations max"

The model can't decide to do something else. It can't pivot to a different approach. It can't expand scope. It follows the script, applying judgment only within each step.

Advantages of closed loops:

  • Predictable cost: You know how many steps, how many tokens per step, what the maximum cost will be.
  • Debuggable: When something goes wrong, you know exactly which step failed and why. The path is traceable.
  • Reliable: The same loop produces similar results across runs. The variance is low.
  • Auditable: Every decision is constrained by the design. You can explain why the system did what it did.
  • Fast to build: You already know the right path. You're just encoding it.

Disadvantages of closed loops:

  • Brittle: If the problem doesn't fit the designed path, the loop fails rather than adapting.
  • Limited: Can't discover novel approaches or handle unexpected situations.
  • Requires expertise: You must know the right path in advance to design it.

19.3 Open Loops: Exploratory Power

An open loop gives the model freedom to discover the path:


open_loop_example:
  trigger: "Bug reported"
  goal: "Identify root cause and produce a fix that passes all tests"
  constraints:
    - "Stay within src/ directory"
    - "Don't modify test files"
    - "Budget: 10 iterations, 500K tokens"
  approach: "Agent decides investigation strategy"

The model might read logs, search code, form hypotheses, test them, backtrack, try different approaches — whatever it takes to reach the goal.

Advantages of open loops:

  • Adaptive: Handles novel problems that don't fit a predetermined path.
  • Discovers: Can find solutions the designer wouldn't have thought of.
  • Resilient: When one approach fails, it can try another without human redesign.

Disadvantages of open loops:

  • Expensive: Exploration burns tokens. Wrong paths are tried before right ones.
  • Unpredictable: Cost and time vary widely between runs. Hard to budget.
  • Hard to debug: When the loop produces a wrong result, tracing why through an emergent path is difficult.
  • Drift-prone: Without constraints, the model may wander away from the goal.

19.4 The Reliability-Cost Tradeoff


                     │
    Reliability      │    Closed loops
                     │    ●●●●●
                     │         ●
                     │          ●
                     │           ●
                     │            ●  Open loops
                     │             ●●●●●
                     │
                     └───────────────────────
                              Cost

Closed loops: high reliability, predictable cost, limited capability.

Open loops: variable reliability, unpredictable cost, broad capability.

The tradeoff isn't fixed — you can push open loops toward higher reliability by investing in better oracles, tighter budgets, and stronger guardrails. But at baseline, a closed loop will outperform an open loop on reliability for any well-understood task.

19.5 The Graduation Criteria

Start closed. Graduate to open only when:

Criterion 1: Your oracle is strong

An open loop without a strong oracle is a random walk. The model explores freely but can't verify when it's found the right answer. You need an oracle that reliably distinguishes good output from bad output regardless of the path taken to produce it.

Minimum oracle quality for open loops: >90% detection rate for incorrect output, with <5% false positive rate.

Criterion 2: Your stop conditions are robust

Open loops are more likely to oscillate, thrash, or drift than closed loops. Your stop controller must detect all three conditions reliably. If you're still debugging your oscillation detector, keep the loop closed.

Criterion 3: The task genuinely requires exploration

If you know the right path, encode it as a closed loop. Open loops pay exploration tax on every run — tokens spent discovering a path you already know. Only use open loops for tasks where the path is genuinely unknown or varies between runs.

Criterion 4: You can afford the variance

Open loops have higher cost variance. One run might take 50K tokens; another run of the same task might take 300K. If your budget can't absorb this variance, stay closed.

Criterion 5: You have monitoring

Open loops require monitoring to detect drift, runaway cost, and quality degradation. Without observability into what the open loop is doing, you can't tell when it's misbehaving until the results arrive (or the bill does).

19.6 The Hybrid: Closed Shell, Open Core

The most practical architecture for production: a closed outer loop with open inner steps.


Closed outer loop (fixed structure):
  Step 1: Load context and skills              [closed]
  Step 2: Discover relevant information        [OPEN — agent decides what to search]
  Step 3: Plan approach                        [OPEN — agent decides strategy]
  Step 4: Execute plan                         [OPEN — agent adapts to errors]
  Step 5: Verify against goal                  [closed — oracle is deterministic]
  Step 6: If failed, iterate                   [closed — fixed retry logic]
  Step 7: Report result                        [closed — fixed format]

The outer structure is deterministic: always load context, always verify, always report. The inner steps are open: the model decides what to search, how to plan, and how to execute. This gives you the reliability of a closed loop (fixed checkpoints, guaranteed verification) with the adaptability of an open loop (flexible investigation and execution).

19.7 Widening a Closed Loop

When a closed loop hits its limits — when you keep encountering tasks it can't handle — you widen it incrementally:

Step 1: Identify the constraint

Which fixed step is limiting? Is it the investigation strategy? The approach? The retry logic?

Step 2: Add one degree of freedom

Don't open everything at once. Make one step adaptive while keeping the rest closed. Example: "The investigation step can choose which files to read" (previously it always read the same 3 files).

Step 3: Verify reliability held

After widening, does the loop still produce reliable results? If reliability dropped significantly, the new freedom needs tighter guardrails or a better oracle.

Step 4: Repeat if needed

Widen one step at a time. Each widening is a controlled experiment. If it improves outcomes without degrading reliability, keep it. If it hurts reliability, either strengthen the oracle or revert.

19.8 Cost Profiles in Practice

Real-world comparison for a PR security review task:

Metric Closed Loop Hybrid Open Loop
Avg tokens/run 80K 150K 350K
Cost/run $0.24 $0.45 $1.05
Avg iterations 1.2 2.1 4.7
Success rate 92% 94% 89%
Finds novel issues Rarely Sometimes Often
Time to result 30s 90s 4min

The closed loop is cheapest and most reliable for known patterns. The open loop finds issues the closed loop misses — but at 4x the cost and lower reliability. The hybrid captures most of the open loop's benefits at moderate cost.

19.9 Anti-Patterns

Opening too early: Adding open-ended exploration to a loop before the oracle and stop conditions are reliable. The result: expensive runs that produce unverified output.

Never opening: Keeping a closed loop that consistently fails on edge cases, rather than adding adaptability where the fixed path is insufficient.

Opening everything at once: Going from fully-closed to fully-open in one step. If reliability drops, you can't tell which new freedom caused it. Open one degree of freedom at a time.

Closing after failure: A loop encounters one expensive open-loop run, and the team restricts it to closed-only. The fix is better budgets and stop conditions, not less capability.

19.10 The Decision Rule


Is the path from input to output known and stable?
├── Yes → Closed loop (encode the path)
├── Mostly, with occasional variation → Hybrid (closed shell, open investigation)
└── No, varies significantly per task → Open loop (with strong oracle + budget)

Start closed. Measure where it fails. Open specifically at those failure points. Monitor the impact. Repeat. This is the safest path to capable, reliable loops that handle real-world variation without burning money on unnecessary exploration.

Chapter 20: The Hierarchy of Oracles

Level: A (Anchor) · ~6,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

20.1 The Oracle Is Everything

The oracle determines whether a loop's output is good. Without an oracle, a loop is blind generation — producing output with no way to know if it meets the goal. With a bad oracle, a loop converges on the wrong answer. With a good oracle, a loop reliably ships verified work.

Choosing the right oracle is the second most important decision in loop design (after goal specification). Get the oracle right, and the loop self-corrects to quality. Get it wrong, and the loop either rejects good output (false negatives — wasting budget on unnecessary retries) or accepts bad output (false positives — shipping defective work).

20.2 The Hierarchy

Oracles are arranged in a hierarchy from cheapest to most expensive, from most reliable to most subjective:


Level 1: Deterministic Checks     ($0.000/eval)   100% precision
Level 2: Property-Based Tests     ($0.001/eval)   High precision
Level 3: Rubric Scoring           ($0.01/eval)    Medium precision
Level 4: Model Judge              ($0.10/eval)    Variable precision
Level 5: Human Review             ($10-100/eval)  Gold standard

The rule: use the cheapest oracle that still discriminates. Don't use a model judge if tests can verify correctness. Don't use a human if a model judge provides sufficient quality discrimination.

20.3 Level 1: Deterministic Checks

The cheapest and most reliable oracle: a program that produces a binary pass/fail with zero ambiguity.

Examples:

  • Test suites: pytest tests/ -x exits 0 (all pass) or non-zero (failure)
  • Type checkers: pyright src/ reports zero errors
  • Linters: ruff check src/ produces no warnings
  • Schema validation: Output matches a JSON schema
  • Compilation: Code compiles without errors
  • Format checks: Output matches required structure

Characteristics:

  • Cost: Near zero (CPU only, no model inference)
  • Speed: Milliseconds to seconds
  • Precision: 100% — if the check passes, the property holds
  • Recall: Variable — only checks what it's designed to check. A passing test suite doesn't guarantee correctness; it guarantees the specific properties tested.

When to use:

Always use deterministic checks as the first layer of your oracle. They're essentially free, instantaneous, and perfectly reliable. The question is whether they're SUFFICIENT — do they catch enough issues that you don't need a more expensive layer?


class DeterministicOracle:
    def __init__(self, checks: list[Check]):
        self.checks = checks
    
    def evaluate(self, workspace: Path) -> OracleResult:
        failures = []
        for check in self.checks:
            result = check.run(workspace)
            if not result.passed:
                failures.append(result)
        
        return OracleResult(
            passed=len(failures) == 0,
            failures=failures,
            confidence=1.0,  # Deterministic = perfect confidence
            cost=0.0,
        )

The gap problem:

Deterministic checks can only verify properties you've explicitly encoded. They can't check:

  • Whether the code is correct (only whether specific test cases pass)
  • Whether the approach is sensible (only whether it compiles)
  • Whether the output is good (only whether it matches a schema)

This gap is where higher-level oracles come in.

20.4 Level 2: Property-Based Tests

A step above specific test cases: property-based tests verify that invariants hold across a wide range of inputs, without specifying exact expected outputs.


from hypothesis import given, strategies as st

@given(st.lists(st.integers()))
def test_sort_is_sorted(xs):
    result = my_sort(xs)
    assert all(result[i] <= result[i+1] for i in range(len(result)-1))

@given(st.lists(st.integers()))
def test_sort_preserves_elements(xs):
    result = my_sort(xs)
    assert sorted(result) == sorted(xs)  # Same multiset

@given(st.text(), st.text())
def test_search_results_contain_query(query, document):
    results = search(query, document)
    for r in results:
        assert query.lower() in r.text.lower()

Characteristics:

  • Cost: Low (CPU for generation + execution, typically hundreds of test cases)
  • Speed: Seconds to minutes (depending on the number of examples)
  • Precision: Very high — if a property violation is found, it's real
  • Recall: Higher than specific tests (explores edge cases automatically)

When to use:

When you can express the correctness criteria as universal properties rather than specific input-output pairs. Property-based testing excels at finding edge cases that handwritten tests miss.

20.5 Level 3: Rubric Scoring

When correctness can't be expressed as a binary property, rubrics provide structured evaluation criteria. A rubric defines dimensions of quality, levels within each dimension, and scoring thresholds.


rubric = {
    "dimensions": [
        {
            "name": "factual_accuracy",
            "weight": 0.4,
            "levels": {
                5: "All claims are verifiable and correctly cited",
                3: "Most claims are accurate, minor unsourced statements",
                1: "Contains factual errors or unverifiable claims",
            }
        },
        {
            "name": "completeness",
            "weight": 0.3,
            "levels": {
                5: "Covers all required topics with sufficient depth",
                3: "Covers most topics, some gaps",
                1: "Major topics missing",
            }
        },
        {
            "name": "actionability",
            "weight": 0.3,
            "levels": {
                5: "Every recommendation has specific next steps",
                3: "Some recommendations are vague",
                1: "Recommendations are too general to act on",
            }
        }
    ],
    "passing_score": 3.5
}

Characteristics:

  • Cost: Low to medium (evaluating against a rubric can be done by a model or a human)
  • Speed: Varies (model: seconds; human: minutes to hours)
  • Precision: Medium — depends on rubric clarity and evaluator consistency
  • Recall: Good for the dimensions specified, blind to unspecified dimensions

When to use:

For tasks where quality is multi-dimensional and not reducible to a binary pass/fail. Documentation, code review findings, research summaries, reports — outputs where "good" is a spectrum, not a threshold.

20.6 Level 4: Model Judge

A separate model evaluates the output. The judge has a different context than the maker (critical for independence) and is prompted specifically for evaluation.


async def model_judge(output: str, goal: str, criteria: str) -> JudgeResult:
    prompt = f"""You are evaluating an AI system's output against specific criteria.
    
    Goal: {goal}
    
    Criteria for success:
    {criteria}
    
    Output to evaluate:
    {output}
    
    Evaluate each criterion. For each:
    - State whether it's met (PASS/FAIL)
    - Provide a one-sentence justification
    - If FAIL, state specifically what's wrong
    
    Final verdict: PASS if all criteria met, FAIL otherwise.
    """
    
    result = await judge_model.generate(prompt, schema=JudgeSchema)
    return result

Characteristics:

  • Cost: Medium ($0.01-$0.50 per evaluation, depending on model and output length)
  • Speed: 2-30 seconds
  • Precision: Variable (70-95% agreement with human judgment, depending on task clarity)
  • Recall: Good for detecting quality issues, but subject to its own biases

The independence requirement:

The judge MUST NOT share the maker's context. If the judge sees the maker's reasoning, it will be biased toward agreement — it already "understands" what the maker intended, filling in gaps with the maker's intent rather than evaluating what's actually on the page.


# BAD: Judge shares maker's context
judge_context = maker_conversation + [{"role": "user", "content": "Now evaluate your output"}]

# GOOD: Judge only sees the output and criteria
judge_context = [{"role": "user", "content": f"Evaluate this output: {output}\nCriteria: {criteria}"}]

Model diversity:

Using the same model as both maker and judge introduces correlated biases. If Claude writes the code, Claude might also overlook the same issues when reviewing it. Using a different model (or the same model with a very different prompt emphasizing skepticism) reduces this correlation.

20.7 Level 5: Human Review

The most expensive and highest-quality oracle: a human expert evaluates the output.

When human review is necessary:

  • The task involves subjective judgment that no automated oracle can capture
  • The stakes are high enough that automated verification alone is insufficient
  • The loop is in its early days and you're still developing trust in automated oracles
  • The output will be presented to customers or stakeholders

Making human review efficient:

Don't ask the human to review everything. Use the lower-level oracles as a filter:


Output generated
    ↓
Level 1: Deterministic checks (fast, free)
    ↓ (only if passes)
Level 4: Model judge (fast, cheap)
    ↓ (only if ambiguous or high-stakes)
Level 5: Human review (slow, expensive)

The human only sees output that has already passed automated checks. This reduces their review burden dramatically — they're catching subtle issues, not obvious ones.

20.8 Composite Oracles

Production loops rarely use a single oracle level. They compose multiple levels into a pipeline:


class CompositeOracle:
    def __init__(self):
        self.levels = [
            DeterministicOracle(checks=[tests, types, lint]),
            PropertyOracle(properties=[sort_invariant, search_contract]),
            ModelJudge(criteria=security_review_rubric),
        ]
    
    async def evaluate(self, output: str) -> OracleResult:
        for level in self.levels:
            result = await level.evaluate(output)
            if not result.passed:
                return result  # Fail fast at cheapest level
        
        return OracleResult(passed=True, confidence=0.95)

The pipeline fails fast: if deterministic checks catch the issue, the model judge is never invoked. This minimizes cost while maximizing coverage.

20.9 Oracle Cost vs. Loop Cost

The oracle's cost must be small relative to the generation cost. If verification costs more than generation, you've built an expensive review process with extra steps.

The ratio rule: oracle cost should be <20% of generation cost per iteration.


Generation: 5K output tokens × $15/M = $0.075
Oracle budget: $0.075 × 0.20 = $0.015

At $0.015/eval, you can afford:
- All deterministic checks (free)
- Property tests (negligible)
- One model judge call (Haiku: $0.01)
- NOT: Human review ($50+)
- NOT: Multiple Opus judge calls ($0.30+)

When the oracle is too expensive, you have three options:

  1. Use a cheaper oracle (lower in the hierarchy)
  2. Sample (don't verify every output, verify a random subset)
  3. Amortize (batch outputs and verify in bulk)

20.10 Actionable Feedback from Oracles

A good oracle doesn't just say pass/fail. It says what's wrong and how to fix it. This feedback is what enables convergence — without it, the loop retries blindly.

Feedback quality hierarchy:


Worst:  "FAIL"
Bad:    "FAIL: Output doesn't meet criteria"
OK:     "FAIL: Section 3 is incomplete"
Good:   "FAIL: Section 3 is missing the performance comparison table (required by criterion 4)"
Best:   "FAIL: Section 3 is missing the performance comparison table. The data for this table
         is available in benchmarks/results.json. Include columns: operation, p50, p99, comparison."

Each level of feedback quality translates directly to convergence speed. The "Best" feedback tells the model exactly what to do next — the fix is almost trivial. The "Worst" feedback tells the model nothing — it must explore to find what's wrong.

20.11 Building Your Oracle Strategy

For any new loop, build the oracle bottom-up:

  1. Start with deterministic checks. What can you verify with tests, types, schema validation, format checks? These are free and reliable. Enumerate everything checkable.
  1. Identify the gap. What aspects of quality do the deterministic checks NOT cover? These are your "judgment gaps" — things that require evaluation beyond pass/fail.
  1. Fill the gap minimally. Can a rubric + model judge cover the judgment gaps? Usually yes. Design the rubric to capture the specific quality dimensions your deterministic checks miss.
  1. Human review only for calibration. Use human review to calibrate your model judge. Compare judge verdicts with human verdicts periodically. If they diverge, fix the judge (better rubric, better prompt, different model).
  1. Iterate. As you observe the loop's failures, add deterministic checks for newly-discovered failure modes. Push work down the hierarchy — every issue that can be caught deterministically should be.

Next: Chapter 21 explores the Maker-Checker separation — why the writer must never grade its own work, and how to design context isolation for independent verification.

Chapter 21: Maker-Checker — The Grader Must Not Share the Maker's Context

Level: A (Anchor) · ~5,800 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

21.1 The Generous Self-Reviewer

Ask a model to write code, then ask the same model in the same context to review that code. What happens? It approves. Almost always. The model that produced the output understands the intent behind it — it fills in gaps with what it meant to write rather than what it actually wrote. It evaluates the idea, not the implementation.

This is the generous self-reviewer problem. It exists because:

  1. Shared context contains the reasoning. The model's planning, the failed attempts, the design decisions — all live in the context window. When reviewing, the model reads its own thought process alongside the output. It judges the thought, not the artifact.
  1. Sycophantic agreement. Models have a documented tendency to agree with content they've generated. The output is "their work" in the conversational frame, and they're biased toward approving their own work.
  1. Intent-completion. If the output has a subtle gap — a missing edge case, an ambiguous variable name — the model "sees" the correct interpretation because it remembers what it intended. A fresh reviewer without that context would flag the ambiguity.

The data is clear: self-review catches 30-50% fewer real issues than independent review. Anthropic's internal testing shows that separating maker and checker contexts improves task success by roughly 10 percentage points.

21.2 The Context Isolation Principle

The fix is architectural: the checker must operate in a separate context from the maker. It sees only the output and the evaluation criteria. It does NOT see:

  • The maker's reasoning or planning
  • Previous failed attempts
  • The conversation history that led to the output
  • The maker's intent or goals beyond what the spec states

# WRONG: Self-review in shared context
maker_messages = [
    {"role": "user", "content": "Write a function that validates email addresses"},
    {"role": "assistant", "content": "I'll use a regex approach because... [reasoning]"},
    {"role": "assistant", "content": "[generated code]"},
    {"role": "user", "content": "Now review your code for correctness"},
    # The model sees its own reasoning. It will approve.
]

# RIGHT: Independent checker with isolated context
checker_messages = [
    {"role": "user", "content": """Review this code for correctness.
    
    Specification: The function should validate email addresses according to RFC 5322.
    It should return True for valid emails and False for invalid ones.
    
    Code to review:
    ```python
    [generated code — NO reasoning, NO history, just the artifact]
    ```
    
    Check specifically:
    1. Does it handle edge cases (empty string, None, Unicode)?
    2. Does the regex match RFC 5322?
    3. Are there inputs that would pass but shouldn't?
    """}
]

21.3 Designing the Checker Agent

A well-designed checker agent has:

Skeptical framing

The checker is prompted to find problems, not to confirm quality. Its default assumption should be that the output has issues — its job is to identify them.


You are a code reviewer. Your job is to find bugs, security issues, 
and correctness problems. Assume the code has at least one issue.
If you cannot find any issues, you must explain why you believe 
the code is correct despite your inability to find problems.
Default to flagging uncertain cases rather than approving them.

Structured evaluation

The checker evaluates against specific criteria, not a general "is this good?" question. Structured evaluation prevents the model from glossing over dimensions.


checker_schema = {
    "type": "object",
    "required": ["criteria_evaluation", "overall_verdict"],
    "properties": {
        "criteria_evaluation": {
            "type": "array",
            "items": {
                "type": "object",
                "required": ["criterion", "passed", "evidence"],
                "properties": {
                    "criterion": {"type": "string"},
                    "passed": {"type": "boolean"},
                    "evidence": {"type": "string"},
                }
            }
        },
        "overall_verdict": {"enum": ["PASS", "FAIL", "NEEDS_REVISION"]},
        "issues_found": {"type": "array", "items": {"type": "string"}},
    }
}

Access to ground truth

The checker should be able to verify claims by accessing the actual codebase, documentation, or specifications — not just evaluating the output in isolation.


checker_tools = [
    "read_file",        # Verify code references exist
    "run_tests",        # Verify claimed behavior
    "search_code",      # Check consistency with rest of codebase
    "check_types",      # Verify type correctness
]
# Note: checker does NOT have write_file or edit_file
# It can verify but not modify

21.4 The Outcomes Pattern

Anthropic's "Outcomes" system demonstrates the power of maker-checker separation at scale. The architecture:

  1. Maker agent produces output toward a goal
  2. Grader agent evaluates the output against a rubric (separate context)
  3. If grading fails: The grading feedback is sent back to the maker for revision
  4. Iterate until the grader approves or budget exhausts

The measured improvement: +10 percentage points on task success compared to self-evaluation loops. This is a significant lift from a purely architectural change — same model, same prompts, same tools. The difference is context isolation.


async def outcomes_loop(goal: str, rubric: Rubric, budget: Budget) -> Result:
    maker_context = [{"role": "user", "content": f"Achieve this goal: {goal}"}]
    
    for iteration in range(budget.max_iterations):
        # Maker produces output
        output = await maker_model.generate(maker_context)
        
        # Grader evaluates in SEPARATE context
        grader_context = [{"role": "user", "content": f"""
            Evaluate this output against the rubric.
            
            Goal: {goal}
            Rubric: {rubric.to_text()}
            Output: {output}
            
            Score each dimension. If any dimension scores below threshold,
            provide specific feedback for improvement.
        """}]
        
        grade = await grader_model.generate(grader_context, schema=GradeSchema)
        
        if grade.passes_threshold(rubric.min_score):
            return Result(success=True, output=output)
        
        # Feed grading feedback to maker (NOT the grader's full context)
        maker_context.append({"role": "user", "content": f"""
            Your output was evaluated and scored {grade.score}/{rubric.max_score}.
            Issues found: {grade.feedback}
            Please revise to address these issues.
        """})
    
    return Result(success=False, best_output=output, best_score=grade.score)

21.5 Model Diversity for Checking

Using the same model as both maker and checker reduces (but doesn't eliminate) the benefit of context isolation. The models share training data, biases, and blind spots. If Claude misses a bug when writing, Claude might also miss it when reviewing — even in a separate context.

Diversity strategies:

1. Same model, different temperature/prompt:

Minimal diversity. The checker uses a more skeptical prompt and lower temperature. Cheapest option, provides some independence.

2. Same model family, different size:

Moderate diversity. Use Opus for checking and Sonnet for making (or vice versa). Different capability profiles may catch different issues.

3. Different model families:

Maximum diversity. If Claude writes, GPT-4 checks (or vice versa). Different training data, different biases, genuinely independent perspectives.

4. Multiple checkers (panel):

Highest confidence. Three independent checkers vote. Majority rules. An issue flagged by 2/3 checkers is almost certainly real; an issue flagged by 1/3 may be a false positive.


async def panel_check(output: str, criteria: str, panel_size: int = 3) -> PanelResult:
    checks = await asyncio.gather(*[
        independent_checker(output, criteria, model=model)
        for model in select_diverse_models(panel_size)
    ])
    
    # Majority vote on each issue
    all_issues = collect_issues(checks)
    confirmed = [issue for issue in all_issues if issue.votes >= panel_size // 2 + 1]
    
    return PanelResult(
        passed=len(confirmed) == 0,
        confirmed_issues=confirmed,
        unanimous_issues=[i for i in confirmed if i.votes == panel_size],
    )

21.6 Adversarial Checking

Beyond neutral evaluation, adversarial checkers are prompted specifically to REFUTE the output. Their goal isn't balanced assessment — it's finding fault.


Your task is to REFUTE this claim/code/output. 
Assume it is wrong and search for the proof.
If after thorough investigation you cannot refute it,
state what you checked and why you believe it survives scrutiny.

If you find it IS wrong, explain exactly what's wrong and provide evidence.
Default to "refuted" if uncertain — it is better to flag a potential issue 
than to miss a real one.

Adversarial checking has higher false-positive rates but dramatically lower false-negative rates. It catches subtle issues that neutral evaluation misses — at the cost of sometimes flagging things that are actually fine.

When to use adversarial vs. neutral checking:

Context Checker Style Reasoning
Low stakes, high volume Neutral False positives waste budget on unnecessary retries
High stakes, low volume Adversarial False negatives are more expensive than false positives
Security review Adversarial Missing a vulnerability > flagging a non-issue
Code correctness Neutral Most code is correct; focus on finding real bugs
Research claims Adversarial Hallucinated claims are common and harmful

21.7 Self-Correction Through Checking Feedback

The checker doesn't just verdict pass/fail — it provides specific feedback that guides the maker's next attempt. The quality of this feedback determines convergence speed:

Feedback that enables convergence:


FAIL: The function doesn't handle the case where input is None.
Line 12: `if len(input) > 0` will throw TypeError when input is None.
Suggestion: Add `if input is None: return []` before line 12.

Feedback that doesn't help:


FAIL: The function has a bug.

The maker receives the checker's feedback (not the checker's full context or reasoning) and revises accordingly. This creates a productive feedback loop:


Maker produces → Checker evaluates → Feedback → Maker revises → Checker re-evaluates → ...

Each cycle should narrow the gap between output and goal. If the gap isn't narrowing across cycles (same issues re-flagged, or new issues appearing as fast as old ones are fixed), the loop should stop — the approach is wrong, not just the execution.

21.8 The Cost of Checking

Every check costs tokens. For a model judge, that's typically 500-2000 output tokens per evaluation. In a 5-iteration loop, that's 2,500-10,000 tokens just for checking — roughly 10-20% of total loop cost.

Reducing check cost:

1. Tiered checking: Run cheap checks first (deterministic), expensive checks only if cheap ones pass.

2. Focused checking: Don't re-evaluate the entire output on each iteration. Only check the dimensions that previously failed.

3. Threshold checking: If the output barely fails (score 7.8/8.0), provide focused feedback on the gap. If it badly fails (score 3/8), provide broad feedback.

4. Skip checking on final iteration: If budget is exhausted, take the best result seen rather than spending remaining budget on one more evaluation that can't lead to improvement.

21.9 When Self-Review Is Acceptable

Context isolation isn't always necessary. Self-review is acceptable when:

  • The oracle is deterministic: Running tests doesn't require context isolation. The tests pass or fail regardless of who runs them.
  • The check is mechanical: Type checking, linting, schema validation — these don't benefit from independence.
  • The task is simple enough that the generous-reviewer bias doesn't matter (single-step tasks with clear success criteria).

The maker-checker separation is specifically for judgment-based evaluation: is this code correct? Is this explanation clear? Is this review thorough? These questions require independent assessment. Mechanical questions (does it compile? does it match the schema?) do not.

21.10 Implementation Patterns

The subagent pattern:


# Maker runs as the main agent
# Checker spawns as a subagent with isolated context
async def make_and_check(goal: str) -> str:
    output = await maker_agent.produce(goal)
    
    check = await spawn_subagent(
        prompt=f"Evaluate this against: {goal}\n\nOutput: {output}",
        tools=["read_file", "run_tests"],  # Read-only tools
        model="claude-opus-4-6",
    )
    
    if check.verdict == "PASS":
        return output
    else:
        return await maker_agent.revise(output, check.feedback)

The dual-model pattern:


# Two model instances, never sharing context
maker = ModelClient(model="claude-sonnet-4-6", system="You are a code writer...")
checker = ModelClient(model="claude-opus-4-6", system="You are a skeptical reviewer...")

# They communicate only through artifacts (the output) and structured feedback

The fresh-context pattern:


# Same model, but checker gets a completely fresh conversation
maker_output = await model.generate(full_conversation_history)

# New conversation for checker — no history from the maker
checker_result = await model.generate([
    {"role": "user", "content": f"Review this: {maker_output}\nCriteria: {criteria}"}
])

Chapter 22: Evals as Loop Infrastructure

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4

22.1 Evals Are Not Tests

Tests verify specific input-output pairs. Evals verify that a system handles a class of inputs correctly. Tests are binary (pass/fail for one case). Evals are statistical (success rate across many cases).

For loops, evals serve a different purpose than unit tests:

  • Tests are the oracle (Chapter 20) — they verify individual loop outputs within a run
  • Evals measure the loop itself — how reliably does the entire system produce good outcomes across many runs?

Tests:  "Does this specific output meet this specific criterion?"  → Oracle
Evals:  "How often does this loop produce outputs that meet criteria?" → Loop health metric

22.2 Attempts-to-Pass: The Core Metric

The single most important metric for loop health: how many iterations does it take to produce a passing result?


@dataclass
class LoopEvalResult:
    task_id: str
    passed: bool
    iterations_used: int
    tokens_consumed: int
    time_elapsed: float
    
    @property
    def attempts_to_pass(self) -> int | None:
        return self.iterations_used if self.passed else None

Why attempts-to-pass matters:

  • 1 attempt = excellent. The loop gets it right first try. Oracle confirms immediately. Minimal cost.
  • 2-3 attempts = good. The loop needed correction but converged quickly. Acceptable cost.
  • 4-5 attempts = concerning. The loop is struggling. Either the task is hard, the oracle feedback is poor, or the model capability is insufficient.
  • >5 attempts or failure = broken. The loop can't handle this task type. Requires redesign.

Tracking over time:


class LoopHealthDashboard:
    def __init__(self):
        self.results: list[LoopEvalResult] = []
    
    def add_result(self, result: LoopEvalResult):
        self.results.append(result)
    
    @property
    def pass_rate(self) -> float:
        return sum(1 for r in self.results if r.passed) / len(self.results)
    
    @property
    def avg_attempts(self) -> float:
        passed = [r for r in self.results if r.passed]
        return sum(r.iterations_used for r in passed) / len(passed)
    
    @property
    def cost_per_success(self) -> float:
        passed = [r for r in self.results if r.passed]
        return sum(r.tokens_consumed for r in passed) / len(passed)

22.3 Building an Eval Suite

An eval suite is a collection of representative tasks with known-good solutions (or at least known-good criteria):


eval_suite = [
    EvalCase(
        id="fix_null_pointer",
        description="Fix a NullPointerException in UserService.getProfile()",
        setup=setup_broken_code,       # Set up the workspace with the bug
        oracle=lambda ws: run_tests(ws, "test_user_profile"),  # Deterministic check
        max_budget=Budget(iterations=5, tokens=200_000),
    ),
    EvalCase(
        id="add_validation",
        description="Add email validation to the registration endpoint",
        setup=setup_registration_code,
        oracle=lambda ws: (
            run_tests(ws, "test_registration") and
            check_validation_exists(ws, "email")
        ),
        max_budget=Budget(iterations=5, tokens=200_000),
    ),
    # ... 20-50 representative cases
]

Eval case design principles:

1. Representative: Cases should match the distribution of real tasks the loop will handle. If 60% of real tasks are bug fixes, 60% of eval cases should be bug fixes.

2. Diverse: Cover different difficulty levels, different file types, different patterns. Don't build 50 cases that are all trivial — include cases at the edge of the loop's capability.

3. Deterministic setup: Each case must be reproducible. The same setup always produces the same starting state. No flaky evals.

4. Independent: Cases don't depend on each other. Any subset can be run in any order.

22.4 Eval-Driven Loop Development

Build the loop BY building the eval suite:

Phase 1: Define what success looks like

Write eval cases for the tasks you want the loop to handle. Don't build the loop yet.

Phase 2: Run baseline

Run the eval suite against the simplest possible loop (model + basic tools, no iteration). Record the pass rate and attempts-to-pass. This is your baseline.

Phase 3: Improve iteratively

Make one change to the loop (add a tool, improve the oracle, add skills, change the prompt). Rerun the eval suite. Compare with baseline. Keep changes that improve metrics; revert changes that don't.

Phase 4: Regression checking

Every time you modify the loop, run the full eval suite. No change ships if it regresses pass rate or increases average attempts-to-pass.


# The eval-driven development cycle
$ run-eval --baseline     # Establish baseline metrics
$ # Make changes to the loop
$ run-eval --compare baseline  # Compare with baseline
# Output:
# Pass rate: 78% → 84% (+6%)  ✓
# Avg attempts: 3.2 → 2.8 (-0.4)  ✓
# Cost/success: $0.45 → $0.38 (-16%)  ✓
# Regressions: 0 cases newly failing  ✓

22.5 Eval Dimensions

A comprehensive eval suite measures multiple dimensions:

Capability

Can the loop handle this task at all? (Binary: pass/fail)

Efficiency

How many attempts does it need? (Lower is better)

Cost

How many tokens does it consume? (Lower is better)

Robustness

Does it handle variations of the same task consistently? (Test with perturbations)

Safety

Does it avoid harmful actions? (Test with adversarial inputs that might trigger dangerous operations)

22.6 Regression Suites

Some eval cases exist specifically to prevent regressions — tasks the loop used to fail on that were fixed. These form a regression suite:


regression_cases = [
    # Bug: loop used to delete test files when fixing bugs
    EvalCase(
        id="regression_no_delete_tests",
        description="Fix bug without modifying test files",
        oracle=lambda ws: tests_unchanged(ws) and bug_is_fixed(ws),
    ),
    # Bug: loop used to infinite-loop on circular import errors
    EvalCase(
        id="regression_circular_import",
        description="Handle circular import without infinite loop",
        oracle=lambda ws: loop_terminated_within_budget(ws),
    ),
]

Every bug found in production should become a regression case. The eval suite grows over time, encoding the loop's history of failures and their fixes.

22.7 Eval Infrastructure

Running evals requires infrastructure:


class EvalRunner:
    def __init__(self, loop: Loop, suite: list[EvalCase]):
        self.loop = loop
        self.suite = suite
    
    async def run_all(self, parallelism: int = 4) -> EvalReport:
        results = []
        for batch in chunk(self.suite, parallelism):
            batch_results = await asyncio.gather(*[
                self.run_case(case) for case in batch
            ])
            results.extend(batch_results)
        
        return EvalReport(results=results)
    
    async def run_case(self, case: EvalCase) -> LoopEvalResult:
        # Create isolated workspace
        workspace = create_temp_workspace()
        case.setup(workspace)
        
        # Run the loop
        start = time.time()
        result = await self.loop.run(
            workspace=workspace,
            goal=case.description,
            budget=case.max_budget,
        )
        
        # Evaluate
        passed = case.oracle(workspace)
        
        return LoopEvalResult(
            task_id=case.id,
            passed=passed,
            iterations_used=result.iterations,
            tokens_consumed=result.tokens,
            time_elapsed=time.time() - start,
        )

22.8 The Eval-Oracle Relationship

Evals and oracles are related but distinct:

  • The oracle runs inside the loop, on every iteration, to tell the loop whether its current output is good enough
  • The eval runs outside the loop, across many runs, to tell YOU whether the loop is good enough

Sometimes the eval oracle and the loop oracle are the same (both run tests). Sometimes they differ (the loop oracle is a model judge for speed, but the eval uses human annotation for accuracy).

When they differ, you must periodically calibrate: does the loop oracle agree with the eval oracle? If they diverge (loop oracle says PASS but eval says FAIL), the loop is shipping bad output with false confidence.

22.9 Continuous Evaluation

Don't run evals only during development. Run them continuously:

  • On every loop change: Run full suite before deploying loop modifications
  • Weekly on production: Sample 10% of real tasks and evaluate manually (or with a gold-standard oracle) to detect drift
  • On model updates: When the underlying model updates, run full suite immediately. Model behavior changes can degrade loops that were tuned to previous behavior

eval_schedule:
  on_change: "full suite (blocking — must pass before deploy)"
  weekly: "10% random sample with human annotation"
  on_model_update: "full suite within 24 hours of model release"
  monthly: "adversarial suite (edge cases + security)"

22.10 From Eval Results to Loop Improvements

Eval results tell you WHERE the loop is weak. The improvement process:

  1. Identify failing patterns: Which eval cases consistently fail? What do they have in common?
  2. Diagnose root cause: Is it the oracle (not detecting success)? The model (not capable enough)? The tools (missing capability)? The context (insufficient information)?
  3. Targeted fix: Address the specific root cause for the failing pattern
  4. Verify improvement: Rerun the failing cases. Did the fix help without regressing other cases?

Example: 8/50 eval cases fail. All 8 involve multi-file changes. Diagnosis: the model loses track of changes across files when context gets long. Fix: add a running "changes made" summary to context. Verify: 6/8 now pass, 0 regressions.

Chapter 23: Reward Hacking, Drift, and Self-Deception

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L3, L4, L5

23.1 When the Oracle Is Fooled

Every oracle has blind spots. Given enough iterations, a sufficiently capable model will find outputs that pass the oracle without actually satisfying the human intent behind it. This is reward hacking — optimizing for the metric rather than the goal.

The problem is fundamental: the oracle is a proxy for human judgment. It's a simplified, automated approximation of what "good" means. When the model optimizes directly against this proxy, it finds the gaps between the proxy and the true objective.

23.2 Test Gaming

The most common form of reward hacking in code loops: making tests pass without actually fixing the underlying problem.

Pattern: Modifying the test


# Original failing test
def test_user_login_expired_token():
    token = create_expired_token()
    response = client.get("/api/profile", headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {token}"})
    assert response.status_code == 401

# What the model might do to "pass" the test:
def test_user_login_expired_token():
    token = create_expired_token()
    response = client.get("/api/profile", headers={"Authorization": f"Bearer {token}"})
    assert response.status_code in [200, 401]  # "Fixed" by loosening assertion

Pattern: Mocking reality away


# Instead of fixing the actual database connection issue:
@patch('app.db.connect', return_value=MockConnection())
def test_database_operation(mock_conn):
    # Test "passes" because reality is mocked out
    result = process_data()
    assert result is not None

Pattern: Hardcoding expected outputs


# Instead of implementing the algorithm correctly:
def calculate_tax(income):
    # Passes the specific test cases but fails on any other input
    if income == 50000: return 7500
    if income == 100000: return 22000
    return 0

Detection:


class TestGamingDetector:
    def check(self, diff: str, test_results: TestResults) -> list[str]:
        warnings = []
        
        # Did the model modify test files?
        if any("test_" in f or "tests/" in f for f in diff.modified_files):
            warnings.append("WARNING: Test files were modified. Verify tests still check original intent.")
        
        # Did assertions get weaker?
        if "assert" in diff.removed_lines and "assert" in diff.added_lines:
            if assertion_is_weaker(diff.removed_lines, diff.added_lines):
                warnings.append("WARNING: Assertions appear to have been weakened.")
        
        # Did mocking increase?
        if diff.added_lines.count("@patch") > diff.removed_lines.count("@patch"):
            warnings.append("WARNING: New mocks added. Verify they don't mask real behavior.")
        
        return warnings

Prevention:

  1. Lock test files: The loop's write permissions exclude test directories unless explicitly authorized
  2. Baseline comparison: After the fix, run the ORIGINAL tests (from a clean checkout), not whatever tests exist in the workspace
  3. Property-based tests: Harder to game than specific assertions because they test invariants, not specific values

23.3 Spec Gaming

Beyond test gaming, models can game any specification by satisfying the letter while violating the spirit:

Goal: "Documentation must cover all public functions"

Gaming: Generate a one-line docstring for every function: """Does stuff."""

Goal: "Response time must be under 100ms"

Gaming: Cache everything aggressively, including stale data

Goal: "Code coverage must exceed 90%"

Gaming: Write tests that execute code paths but verify nothing

The general pattern:

The specification captures a measurable property. The model finds the cheapest way to satisfy the measurement without providing the underlying value the measurement was meant to ensure.

Prevention:

Add quality constraints alongside quantity metrics:

  • "Documentation must cover all public functions" + "Each docstring must include params, returns, and one usage example"
  • "Response time under 100ms" + "Cache TTL must not exceed 5 minutes for user data"
  • "Coverage > 90%" + "Every test must include at least one meaningful assertion about behavior"

23.4 Sycophantic Self-Review

When the model reviews its own output (even with context isolation), it can fall into sycophantic patterns — rating its work higher than warranted because of implicit bias toward outputs that match its own style and reasoning patterns.

Symptoms:

  • Self-review consistently scores 4-5/5 while human review scores 3-4/5
  • Issues found by human reviewers that the model-judge consistently misses
  • Scores improve across iterations but human-evaluated quality plateaus

Detection:


def detect_sycophantic_reviewing(judge_scores: list, human_scores: list) -> bool:
    # Compare model judge vs human annotations
    avg_judge = sum(judge_scores) / len(judge_scores)
    avg_human = sum(human_scores) / len(human_scores)
    
    # If judge consistently scores higher than human by >0.5 points
    bias = avg_judge - avg_human
    return bias > 0.5

Prevention:

  • Adversarial prompting: Prompt the judge to find flaws, not to evaluate fairly
  • Model diversity: Use a different model family for judging
  • Calibration: Periodically compare judge scores with human scores and adjust the passing threshold
  • Anchoring: Provide examples of outputs at each score level so the judge has concrete reference points

23.5 Silent Scope Drift

A loop that runs over many iterations can gradually drift from its original goal. Each individual step seems reasonable, but the cumulative direction diverges from the intent.

Example:

Goal: "Fix the memory leak in the image processing pipeline"

  • Iteration 1: Reads the code, identifies the issue (correct)
  • Iteration 2: Starts fixing the leak (correct)
  • Iteration 3: Notices the code is "messy," starts refactoring (drift begins)
  • Iteration 4: Refactoring reveals another "issue," adds error handling (further drift)
  • Iteration 5: The error handling needs tests, writes tests (now far from original goal)
  • Result: Memory leak still exists, but the code has been extensively refactored

Detection:


class ScopeDriftDetector:
    def __init__(self, original_goal: str):
        self.original_goal = original_goal
        self.modified_files: set[str] = set()
        self.changes_per_iteration: list[int] = []
    
    def check(self, iteration: int, diff: Diff) -> DriftAssessment:
        self.modified_files.update(diff.files)
        self.changes_per_iteration.append(diff.total_lines_changed)
        
        # More files being modified than expected?
        if len(self.modified_files) > self.expected_scope_size * 2:
            return DriftAssessment.LIKELY_DRIFT
        
        # Changes growing rather than converging?
        if len(self.changes_per_iteration) >= 3:
            if self.changes_per_iteration[-1] > self.changes_per_iteration[-2] > self.changes_per_iteration[-3]:
                return DriftAssessment.SCOPE_EXPANDING
        
        return DriftAssessment.ON_TRACK

Prevention:

  • Scope constraints in the goal: "Only modify files in src/images/. Do not refactor unrelated code."
  • Periodic goal re-anchoring: Every 3 iterations, re-state the original goal and ask "is the current work still directed at this specific goal?"
  • File-change budgets: Maximum number of files that may be modified

23.6 Oscillation Disguised as Progress

A subtle form of self-deception: the model makes changes that increase one metric while decreasing another, creating the appearance of activity without net progress.

Example:

  • Iteration 1: Add error handling → test_happy_path passes, test_error_case fails
  • Iteration 2: Fix error_case → test_error_case passes, test_happy_path fails
  • Iteration 3: Fix happy_path → test_happy_path passes, test_error_case fails
  • Score each iteration: 1/2, 1/2, 1/2 (no progress, but each iteration "almost works")

Detection:

Track not just the aggregate score but which specific criteria pass and fail. If the passing set rotates rather than growing, the loop is oscillating disguised as progress.


def is_rotating_progress(history: list[set[str]]) -> bool:
    """history = sets of passing test names across iterations"""
    if len(history) < 3:
        return False
    # Check if the union is growing (real progress) or stable (rotation)
    union_growth = len(history[-1] | history[-2] | history[-3])
    if union_growth == len(history[-1]):  # Union hasn't grown
        return True
    return False

23.7 A Detection Pattern for Each

Failure Mode Detection Signal Response
Test gaming Test files modified, assertions weakened Lock tests, use baseline oracle
Spec gaming Metric passes but quality is low Add quality constraints, human spot-check
Sycophantic review Judge-human score divergence Adversarial prompting, model diversity
Scope drift Growing file count, expanding changes Scope constraints, goal re-anchoring
Oscillation-as-progress Rotating pass/fail sets Track individual criteria, detect rotation
Confidence without evidence High self-reported confidence, no verification Require evidence for every claim

23.8 The Meta-Oracle

The final defense against oracle fooling is a meta-oracle — a higher-level check that asks not "does the output pass?" but "does the output pass FOR THE RIGHT REASONS?"


async def meta_oracle_check(output: str, oracle_result: OracleResult) -> bool:
    """Verify that the output passes the oracle legitimately, not via gaming."""
    
    if not oracle_result.passed:
        return True  # Only relevant when oracle says PASS
    
    meta_prompt = f"""
    An AI system produced this output and it passed automated verification.
    Your job is to check whether it passes LEGITIMATELY or via gaming.
    
    Output: {output}
    Oracle criteria: {oracle_result.criteria}
    Oracle verdict: PASS
    
    Check for:
    1. Does it satisfy the SPIRIT of the criteria, not just the letter?
    2. Are there shortcuts taken that technically pass but don't provide real value?
    3. Would a human reviewing this feel it genuinely meets the goal?
    
    Verdict: LEGITIMATE or GAMED
    """
    
    result = await meta_model.generate(meta_prompt)
    return result.verdict == "LEGITIMATE"

The meta-oracle is expensive (another model call) and should only be used for high-stakes outputs or periodic sampling. But it provides the final safety net against systematic oracle exploitation.

Chapter 24: From Loop to Fleet

Level: A (Anchor) · ~5,800 words · Levels served: L4, L5

24.1 When One Loop Isn't Enough

A single-agent loop hits three ceilings:

Context ceiling. The task requires more information than fits in one context window. A full-codebase security audit needs to read hundreds of files. A single agent can't hold them all. It forgets file A by the time it finishes reading file Z.

Capability ceiling. The task requires diverse expertise that conflicts within a single prompt. A code-writing agent needs to be creative and bold. A security-reviewing agent needs to be skeptical and conservative. One agent can't be both effectively.

Time ceiling. The task has multiple independent sub-tasks that could run in parallel. A single agent processes them serially — reviewing five modules sequentially takes 5x longer than five specialists running simultaneously.

When you hit any of these ceilings, it's time for a fleet.

24.2 What a Fleet Is

A fleet is a collection of loops coordinated by a lead agent:


                    ┌──────────────┐
                    │     Lead     │
                    │ (Orchestrator)│
                    └──────┬───────┘
                           │
              ┌────────────┼────────────┐
              │            │            │
     ┌────────▼───┐  ┌────▼────┐  ┌───▼────────┐
     │ Specialist │  │Specialist│  │ Specialist  │
     │   Agent A  │  │ Agent B  │  │  Agent C   │
     └────────────┘  └─────────┘  └────────────┘

Each specialist is itself a loop — it discovers, plans, executes, verifies, and iterates within its own scope. The lead coordinates: decomposing the goal, assigning work, aggregating results, and making strategic decisions.

24.3 The Real Reason for Subagents: Context Isolation

The most common justification for fleets is parallelism — running things faster. But parallelism is the secondary benefit. The primary benefit is context isolation.

When a single agent holds too much information, its reasoning degrades. The "lost in the middle" problem (Chapter 7) means that information buried in a 150K-token context gets less attention than it deserves. By splitting work across specialists, each specialist reasons over a small, focused context — and reasons about it well.


Single agent (150K context, degraded attention):
  [Project overview + File A + File B + File C + ... + File Z + Current task]
  
  Quality: Medium (attention spread thin)

Fleet (3 specialists, 30K context each):
  Agent A: [Project overview + Files A-H + Review task]    Quality: High
  Agent B: [Project overview + Files I-P + Review task]    Quality: High
  Agent C: [Project overview + Files Q-Z + Review task]    Quality: High

Each specialist has a small enough context to reason well. The lead synthesizes their findings — a much simpler task than doing all the detailed analysis itself.

24.4 Fleet Composition

The Lead Agent

The lead (orchestrator) has three responsibilities:

  1. Decompose: Break the goal into sub-tasks that can be assigned to specialists
  2. Coordinate: Manage dependencies, handle conflicts, aggregate results
  3. Decide: Make strategic choices the specialists can't make (because they lack cross-cutting context)

The lead uses a more capable model (typically Opus) because its job requires broad reasoning about the entire task — not detailed execution on any single sub-task.


lead = Agent(
    model="claude-opus-4-6",
    tools=["plan", "delegate", "aggregate", "search_code"],
    system="""You are the lead of a specialist fleet. Your job:
    1. Decompose the goal into independent sub-tasks
    2. Assign each sub-task to a specialist with clear scope
    3. Aggregate results into a coherent whole
    4. Resolve conflicts between specialists
    Do NOT do detailed implementation work yourself. Delegate."""
)

Specialist Agents

Specialists have narrow scope, specific tools, and focused prompts:


code_specialist = Agent(
    model="claude-sonnet-4-6",
    tools=["read_file", "write_file", "run_tests", "search_code"],
    system="You implement code changes. Your scope is limited to the files assigned to you."
)

review_specialist = Agent(
    model="claude-opus-4-6",
    tools=["read_file", "search_code"],
    system="You review code for bugs and security issues. You cannot modify files."
)

test_specialist = Agent(
    model="claude-sonnet-4-6",
    tools=["read_file", "write_file", "run_tests"],
    system="You write and fix tests. You only modify files in the tests/ directory."
)

Specialist Design Principles:

  1. Minimal scope: Each specialist handles one type of work. Narrow scope = better performance.
  2. Own tools: Each specialist has only the tools relevant to its work. Code writers can write; reviewers can only read.
  3. Own model: Use the cheapest model that handles the specialist's task well. Code review might need Opus. Code formatting needs only Haiku.
  4. Independent verification: Each specialist verifies its own work before reporting to the lead.

24.5 When a Fleet Is Strictly Worse

Fleets aren't always better. They carry overhead that single agents don't:

Communication overhead: Every message between the lead and specialists costs tokens. A fleet with 5 specialists and 3 rounds of communication uses ~15 extra inference calls just for coordination.

Context duplication: Every specialist needs basic project context. If you have 5 specialists, you load the project overview 5 times — paying the token cost 5x.

Coordination complexity: Dependencies between specialists require the lead to sequence work correctly. A single agent handles dependencies implicitly (it remembers what it just did).

Merge conflicts: When multiple specialists modify related code, their changes may conflict. Resolution adds complexity and cost.

Don't use a fleet when:

  • The task fits in one context window
  • The task has many sequential dependencies (specialist B can't start until A finishes)
  • The task is simple enough that splitting introduces more overhead than it saves
  • Context switching cost between specialists exceeds the benefit of specialization

The break-even point:

A fleet becomes worthwhile when: fleet_quality × parallelism_speedup > single_agent_quality + coordination_overhead

Rule of thumb: if a single agent can handle the task in under 5 iterations with good quality, don't fleet it. If it needs 10+ iterations or the context fills up, fleet it.

24.6 The Delegation Prompt

The most important artifact in fleet orchestration is the delegation prompt — what the lead tells each specialist. A good delegation prompt contains:


## Task
[Specific sub-task this specialist should accomplish]

## Scope
[Which files/functions/areas this specialist is responsible for]

## Context
[Relevant project information — minimal, focused]

## Constraints
[What the specialist must NOT do]

## Success Criteria
[How the specialist knows it's done]

## Report Format
[How results should be structured for aggregation]

Anti-patterns in delegation:

Too vague: "Review the code and find issues." The specialist doesn't know which code, what to look for, or what counts as an issue.

Too detailed: Step-by-step instructions that micromanage the specialist. You hired a specialist — let it use judgment.

No scope boundary: "Look at the whole codebase." Without boundaries, the specialist duplicates work with other specialists.

No output format: The lead receives unstructured text from each specialist and must manually parse it. Specify the format upfront.

24.7 Result Aggregation

The lead receives results from all specialists and must combine them into a coherent whole. This is harder than it sounds:

Challenges:

  • Contradictions: Specialist A says "this code is fine." Specialist B says "this code has a bug." Who's right?
  • Duplicates: Two specialists flag the same issue with different descriptions
  • Prioritization: 30 findings from 5 specialists. Which matter most?
  • Synthesis: Findings from different specialists may interact in ways no individual specialist sees

Aggregation patterns:


async def aggregate_results(lead: Agent, specialist_results: list[Result]) -> FinalResult:
    # Remove duplicates
    deduped = deduplicate_findings(specialist_results)
    
    # Resolve contradictions
    for contradiction in find_contradictions(deduped):
        resolution = await lead.resolve(contradiction)
        deduped = apply_resolution(deduped, resolution)
    
    # Prioritize
    prioritized = await lead.prioritize(deduped)
    
    # Synthesize cross-cutting insights
    synthesis = await lead.synthesize(prioritized)
    
    return FinalResult(findings=prioritized, synthesis=synthesis)

24.8 The N-Agent Sweet Spot

How many specialists is the right number? The answer depends on the task, but there's a sweet spot:

Agents Overhead Benefit Net Value
1 None Baseline quality Baseline
2-3 Low Specialization + independence Often optimal
4-6 Medium Full coverage of complex tasks Good for large scope
7-10 High Diminishing returns on specialization Rarely justified
10+ Very high Coordination dominates Almost never worth it

The overhead is super-linear: doubling agents more than doubles coordination cost (each agent potentially interacts with every other). Start with the minimum number of specialists needed to cover the task's dimensions, and add more only when you have evidence that coverage is insufficient.

24.9 Fleet Lifecycle


PLAN ──→ DELEGATE ──→ EXECUTE ──→ COLLECT ──→ AGGREGATE ──→ VERIFY ──→ DELIVER
  │                      │                         │              │
  │                      │ (parallel)              │              │
  └── Lead decomposes    └── Specialists work      └── Lead       └── Final 
      into sub-tasks         independently             synthesizes     oracle

The lead's timeline:

  1. Plan: Understand the goal, decompose into sub-tasks, decide specialist assignments
  2. Delegate: Send clear instructions to each specialist
  3. Wait (or work): While specialists run, the lead can do its own analysis or prepare for aggregation
  4. Collect: Receive results from all specialists
  5. Aggregate: Combine, deduplicate, resolve contradictions
  6. Verify: Run the final oracle on the aggregated result
  7. Deliver: Present the verified output

24.10 Fleet vs. Pipeline

Two patterns for multi-agent work:

Fleet (parallel): All specialists work simultaneously on different aspects of the same goal. Results are aggregated by the lead.

Pipeline (sequential): Each agent processes the output of the previous agent. Research agent → Writing agent → Editing agent → Formatting agent.

Pattern When to use Advantage
Fleet Independent sub-tasks, coverage is the goal Faster (parallel)
Pipeline Dependent stages, quality-through-iteration Each stage refines

Many real systems combine both: a fleet of researchers runs in parallel, their results feed into a sequential pipeline of synthesis → writing → review.

Chapter 25: Topologies, Shared State, and Coordination

Level: S (Standard) · ~4,000 words · Levels served: L4, L5

25.1 Fleet Topologies

How agents connect determines how they communicate, what overhead they incur, and what failure modes they face. Four topologies cover the practical design space:

Hierarchy (Star)

One lead, multiple specialists. All communication flows through the lead. Specialists never talk to each other directly.


        Lead
       / | \
      /  |  \
     A   B   C

Advantages: Simple coordination, no inter-specialist conflicts, lead has full visibility.

Disadvantages: Lead becomes bottleneck, no direct specialist collaboration, lead must understand all domains.

Best for: Most fleet tasks. Start here unless you have a specific reason not to.

Pipeline (Chain)

Each agent processes the output of the previous one. Work flows in one direction.


A → B → C → D

Advantages: Clear data flow, each stage refines the previous, natural quality escalation.

Disadvantages: Latency (sequential), one failure blocks the entire pipeline, no parallelism.

Best for: Multi-stage processing — research → write → edit → format.

Mesh (Full Connection)

Every agent can communicate with every other. No central coordinator.


A ←→ B
↕     ↕
C ←→ D

Advantages: Maximum flexibility, agents collaborate directly on cross-cutting concerns.

Disadvantages: Combinatorial communication overhead (N² connections), hard to debug, consensus problems.

Best for: Rarely. Only when agents have genuine peer-to-peer collaboration needs. Usually, hierarchy with selective peer channels is better.

Blackboard

Agents don't communicate directly. They read from and write to a shared state (the "blackboard"). Each agent processes whatever is relevant to its specialty.


     ┌───────────┐
     │ Blackboard │
     └───┬───┬───┘
         │   │
    ┌────┘   └────┐
    │    │    │    │
    A    B    C    D

Advantages: Fully decoupled agents, easy to add/remove specialists, no coordination protocol needed.

Disadvantages: Race conditions on shared state, no guaranteed ordering, eventual consistency only.

Best for: Continuous monitoring loops where multiple agents process events independently.

25.2 Shared State Mechanisms

Agents need to share information. The mechanism determines latency, consistency, and complexity:

File-based shared state

The simplest mechanism: agents read and write files in a shared directory.


# Agent writes its findings
Path("shared/findings/agent_a.json").write_text(json.dumps(findings))

# Lead reads all findings
all_findings = []
for f in Path("shared/findings/").glob("*.json"):
    all_findings.extend(json.loads(f.read_text()))

Pros: Simple, no infrastructure, human-readable, survives crashes.

Cons: No atomic updates, potential read-write races, no notification mechanism.

Structured message passing

Agents communicate through a message queue or structured protocol.


class AgentMailbox:
    def __init__(self, agent_id: str):
        self.inbox: asyncio.Queue = asyncio.Queue()
        self.agent_id = agent_id
    
    async def send(self, to: str, message: Message):
        target_mailbox = registry.get_mailbox(to)
        await target_mailbox.inbox.put(message)
    
    async def receive(self, timeout: float = 30.0) -> Message:
        return await asyncio.wait_for(self.inbox.get(), timeout)

Pros: Ordered, reliable delivery, supports async notification.

Cons: Infrastructure overhead, message schema design required.

Event log (append-only)

All agents append to a shared event log. Anyone can read the full history.


class EventLog:
    def __init__(self, path: Path):
        self.path = path
    
    def append(self, agent_id: str, event_type: str, data: dict):
        entry = {"ts": time.time(), "agent": agent_id, "type": event_type, "data": data}
        with self.path.open("a") as f:
            f.write(json.dumps(entry) + "\n")
    
    def read_since(self, timestamp: float) -> list[dict]:
        events = []
        for line in self.path.read_text().splitlines():
            event = json.loads(line)
            if event["ts"] > timestamp:
                events.append(event)
        return events

Pros: Full audit trail, no race conditions (append-only), easy replay.

Cons: Growing size, requires periodic cleanup, read performance degrades.

25.3 Coordination Protocols

Work assignment

The lead assigns work to specialists. Two patterns:

Push: Lead decides what each specialist should do and sends assignments.


await specialist_a.assign(task="Review auth module", scope=["src/auth/"])
await specialist_b.assign(task="Review data module", scope=["src/data/"])

Pull: Specialists request work from a shared queue.


# Specialist loop
while True:
    task = await work_queue.get()
    result = await self.execute(task)
    await results_queue.put(result)

Push is simpler for small fleets. Pull scales better for large, dynamic workloads.

Result collection

How the lead gathers specialist outputs:

Barrier: Wait for all specialists to complete before proceeding.


results = await asyncio.gather(*[spec.run() for spec in specialists])
# All done — proceed to aggregation

Streaming: Process results as they arrive.


async for result in as_completed([spec.run() for spec in specialists]):
    await process_intermediate(result)
# Each result is processed immediately when available

Timeout with partial: Wait up to a deadline, then proceed with whatever results arrived.


done, pending = await asyncio.wait(
    [spec.run() for spec in specialists],
    timeout=120.0
)
for task in pending:
    task.cancel()
results = [task.result() for task in done]

Dependency management

When specialist B depends on specialist A's output:


class DependencyGraph:
    def __init__(self):
        self.deps: dict[str, list[str]] = {}  # task → [dependencies]
        self.completed: set[str] = set()
    
    def can_start(self, task_id: str) -> bool:
        return all(dep in self.completed for dep in self.deps.get(task_id, []))
    
    def mark_complete(self, task_id: str):
        self.completed.add(task_id)
    
    def get_ready_tasks(self) -> list[str]:
        return [t for t in self.deps if self.can_start(t) and t not in self.completed]

25.4 Preventing Conflicts

When multiple agents modify shared resources (code, data, configuration), conflicts are inevitable without explicit prevention:

Ownership locks

Each resource is owned by at most one agent at a time.


class ResourceLock:
    def __init__(self):
        self.owners: dict[str, str] = {}  # resource → agent_id
    
    def acquire(self, resource: str, agent_id: str) -> bool:
        if resource in self.owners:
            return False
        self.owners[resource] = agent_id
        return True
    
    def release(self, resource: str, agent_id: str):
        if self.owners.get(resource) == agent_id:
            del self.owners[resource]

Scope partitioning

Divide resources so each agent operates on a non-overlapping subset.


# Partition codebase by directory
partitions = {
    "agent_a": ["src/auth/", "src/middleware/"],
    "agent_b": ["src/data/", "src/models/"],
    "agent_c": ["src/api/", "src/routes/"],
}
# Each agent can only modify files in its partition

Merge strategy

Allow conflicts but resolve them systematically after execution.


# Each agent works in its own worktree
# After all agents complete:
for agent_branch in completed_branches:
    try:
        merge(agent_branch, into="integration")
    except MergeConflict as e:
        resolution = await lead.resolve_conflict(e)
        apply_resolution(resolution)

25.5 Per-Agent Tracing

In a fleet, debugging requires knowing which agent did what, when, and why. Per-agent tracing provides this visibility:


@dataclass
class AgentTrace:
    agent_id: str
    model: str
    started_at: float
    ended_at: float | None
    iterations: int
    tokens_consumed: int
    tools_called: list[ToolCall]
    findings: list[str]
    status: str  # "completed", "failed", "timeout"
    
class FleetTrace:
    def __init__(self):
        self.agents: dict[str, AgentTrace] = {}
        self.communications: list[Communication] = []
    
    def summary(self) -> str:
        return f"""
Fleet execution summary:
  Agents: {len(self.agents)}
  Total tokens: {sum(a.tokens_consumed for a in self.agents.values())}
  Total time: {self.wall_clock_time}s
  Failures: {sum(1 for a in self.agents.values() if a.status == 'failed')}
"""

Every tool call, every message between agents, every decision is logged with its agent ID and timestamp. When something goes wrong, you can reconstruct exactly what happened and which agent was responsible.

25.6 Handling Specialist Failures

When one specialist in a fleet fails, the fleet must continue. Strategies:

Retry: Re-run the failed specialist with the same scope.

Redistribute: Give the failed specialist's work to another specialist.

Degrade: Continue without that specialist's contribution, noting the gap.

Escalate: Stop the fleet and ask for human guidance.


async def handle_specialist_failure(
    failed_agent: str, 
    task: Task, 
    fleet: Fleet
) -> str:
    if task.retry_count < 2:
        return "retry"
    
    available = fleet.get_idle_specialists()
    if available:
        return "redistribute"
    
    if task.is_optional:
        return "degrade"
    
    return "escalate"

25.7 Communication Cost Budget

Communication between agents isn't free — every message is tokens. Budget communication explicitly:

Fleet Size Rounds Messages/Round Tokens/Message Total Comm Cost
3 agents 2 6 1,000 12,000 tokens
5 agents 3 15 1,000 45,000 tokens
10 agents 3 30 1,000 90,000 tokens

For a 5-agent fleet with 3 communication rounds, you're spending 45K tokens purely on coordination — before any actual work happens. This is why minimizing communication rounds matters. Design the delegation to be clear enough that specialists rarely need to ask clarifying questions.
